#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:**

- COMPLIANCE AS MARKET ENTRY BARRIER: AI startups entering financial services face a unique regulatory density — horizontal AI laws (EU AI Act) PLUS sector-specific financial regulation (MiFID, DORA, MiCA, ECOA). A fintech building a credit scoring product must comply with high-risk AI obligations (documentation, testing, conformity assessment) AND traditional fair lending laws. This creates significant fixed compliance costs that disproportionately affect early-stage startups.
- REGULATORY ARBITRAGE OPPORTUNITY: The divergence between EU (comprehensive), US (patchwork with deregulatory signals), and UK (light-touch principles) creates strategic choices for AI fintech founders. A startup could initially target the UK market (FCA sandbox, no AI-specific rules) to build track record before tackling EU compliance. Conversely, achieving EU AI Act compliance first can serve as a global quality signal.
- REGTECH AS BUSINESS OPPORTUNITY: The complexity of compliance creates a massive market for RegTech solutions. Startups building AI compliance tools (model monitoring, bias testing, documentation automation, explainability layers) serve a growing market as financial institutions race to meet Aug 2026 high-risk deadlines. Compliance technology has clear ROI and fewer competitors than consumer fintech.
- AI WASHING RISK: Entrepreneurs must be extremely careful about AI claims in marketing and investor materials. SEC actively enforces AI washing — Delphia and Global Predictions were fined for exaggerating AI capabilities; Nate Inc. founder faced criminal charges for fabricating AI functionality. Claim only what your AI actually does.
- SANDBOX STRATEGY: EU AI Act mandates at least one regulatory sandbox per Member State by Aug 2026, with priority access for SMEs/startups (free of charge). Sandbox participation provides legal certainty, reduces compliance risk, and creates a competitive advantage through regulatory learning. FCA's Supercharged Sandbox offers similar benefits in the UK.
- CREDIT SCORING AND LENDING: The highest-impact regulatory area for AI startups. CFPB requires specific adverse action reasons (not generic 'algorithm said no'), mandatory search for less discriminatory alternatives, and disparate impact analysis. Startups must build explainability into AI credit models from day one — retrofitting is far more expensive.
- OPEN SOURCE TRAP: Open-source credit scoring or lending AI models receive NO exemption from EU AI Act high-risk obligations. Entrepreneurs who assume open-source licensing reduces regulatory burden in financial services are mistaken.
- MIFID III HORIZON: Academic and regulatory signals suggest MiFID II will be updated with binding AI provisions. Startups building AI for investment services (robo-advisory, algorithmic trading) should anticipate stricter future requirements for model design, explainability, and data governance beyond current non-binding ESMA guidance.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary fit for regulatory framework analysis and compliance planning. Also relevant to Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) for Build vs Buy decisions influenced by regulatory requirements, and Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) for understanding compliance costs, regulatory arbitrage, and RegTech market opportunities.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official regulatory texts (EU AI Act, MiFID II, DORA, MiCA), enforcement agency publications (SEC, CFPB, EBA, ESMA, FCA), international body reports (FSB, Basel Committee, GAO), and reputable law firm analyses (Goodwin, Linklaters, Bird & Bird, A&O Shearman, Sidley Austin). Academic sources include Oxford Law Blog and Harvard Data Science Review.

**Cross Reference:**

- EU AI Act Annex III high-risk classification confirmed by: EBA factsheet (Nov 2025), Goodwin Law analysis (Aug 2024, June 2025), Consultancy.eu analysis, GARP analysis, Harvard Data Science Review (Summer 2025)
- SEC AI washing enforcement confirmed by: SEC.gov press releases, Harvard Law School Forum on Corporate Governance, NYSBA, Mayer Brown, DLA Piper, Holland & Knight
- CFPB AI credit decisioning guidance confirmed by: CFPB.gov official publications, Skadden analysis, Morrison Foerster analysis, consumer finance law monitors
- FCA principles-based approach confirmed by: FCA.org.uk official publications, PwC UK analysis, Freshfields Bruckhaus Deringer analysis, Kennedys Law analysis
- MiFID II algorithmic trading requirements confirmed by: ESMA Interactive Single Rulebook, Norton Rose Fulbright, KPMG, Dechert
- FSB AI monitoring framework confirmed by: FSB.org official publications, Moody's analysis, BIS/Basel analysis
- EBA AI Act mapping confirmed by: EBA official factsheet, Regulation Tomorrow, A&O Shearman FinReg, Linklaters analysis

**Caveats:**

- RAPIDLY EVOLVING: The US regulatory landscape under the Trump administration is shifting toward deregulation — the SEC's withdrawal of the Predictive Data Analytics rule and the Dec 2025 state preemption EO signal significant policy changes. However, enforcement on AI washing and fair lending continues.
- CFPB UNCERTAINTY: CFPB's future enforcement posture under the Trump administration remains uncertain, though existing guidance and regulations remain in force.
- MIFID III SPECULATIVE: The call for MiFID III with binding AI provisions is currently academic (Oxford/SSRN paper, Feb 2026). No formal legislative proposal exists yet.
- EBA MEASURED RESPONSE: EBA's statement that no new guidelines are needed may evolve as AI adoption accelerates in banking — the 2026-2027 work programme includes further assessment activities.
- CHINA DATA LIMITED: PBOC's 'AI + Finance' framework details are less transparent than EU/US/UK regulatory publications. The 100% AI ethics review mandate is reported but detailed implementation mechanisms are less well-documented.
- IMPLEMENTATION GAPS: While the EU AI Act is law, many implementing acts and standards are still being developed (e.g., sandbox implementing act under consultation Dec 2025). Practical enforcement patterns will only emerge after Aug 2026.
- GLOBAL COORDINATION: FSB and Basel Committee frameworks are voluntary — actual adoption varies significantly across jurisdictions.

### 11. AI in Healthcare Regulation (FDA AI/ML Framework, EU MDR/AI Act for Medical Devices, Clinical Validation)

_Source: `AI_in_Healthcare_Regulation.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** AI in Healthcare Regulation (FDA AI/ML Framework, EU MDR/AI Act for Medical Devices, Clinical Validation)

**Type:** framework

**Jurisdiction:** US (FDA), EU (MDR + AI Act), global (WHO, IMDRF)

**Status:** in_force

**Effective Date:** Multi-layered: FDA Total Product Lifecycle (TPLC) framework ongoing, PCCP final guidance December 2024, TPLC draft guidance January 2025; EU MDR in force since May 2021, AI Act high-risk medical device provisions apply from August 2027 (may be further delayed per Digital Omnibus proposal); WHO LMM health guidance January 2024; GMLP principles October 2021

**Regulatory Philosophy:** sector_specific

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** AI in healthcare is regulated through overlapping sector-specific frameworks: in the US, the FDA regulates AI/ML-enabled medical devices under its Total Product Lifecycle (TPLC) approach with pathways including 510(k), De Novo, and PMA, plus the novel Predetermined Change Control Plan (PCCP) mechanism for iterative AI updates. In the EU, AI medical devices face dual regulation under the Medical Device Regulation (MDR/IVDR) and the AI Act, with most AI medical devices classified as high-risk under the AI Act Article 6(1). Internationally, the IMDRF coordinates harmonization while the WHO provides ethics guidance and the EMA-FDA issued joint principles for AI in the medicines lifecycle.

**Key Provisions:**

- FDA TPLC (Total Product Lifecycle) approach: AI devices are not static — continuous monitoring, evaluation, and updating required throughout use; draft guidance issued January 2025 covering model description, data lineage, performance claims, bias analysis, human-AI workflow, and monitoring
- Predetermined Change Control Plans (PCCPs): final FDA guidance issued December 2024 — manufacturers can pre-authorize planned modifications (retraining, new data, performance improvements) at time of initial submission; PCCP must include description of modifications, modification protocol, and impact assessment; if authorized, no new marketing submission needed for each change
- Three FDA regulatory pathways: 510(k) (96-97% of AI devices, requires substantial equivalence to predicate), De Novo (2-3%, novel Class II without predicate), PMA (<1%, highest-risk Class III with clinical trials)
- Good Machine Learning Practice (GMLP): 10 guiding principles jointly identified by FDA, Health Canada, and MHRA (October 2021) covering multi-disciplinary expertise, good software engineering, representative data, independent datasets, reference standards, good study design, human-AI team performance, clinical validity testing, user transparency, and monitoring
- EU MDR classification: AI software as medical device (SaMD) classified under MDR Annex VIII rules; Class IIa and above require Notified Body conformity assessment; medical devices integrating AI are typically high-risk under EU AI Act Article 6(1)
- EU AI Act interplay: MDCG 2025-6 guidance (June 2025) clarifies that sectoral conformity assessments under MDR/IVDR take precedence for high-risk AI systems — no standalone AI-only assessment needed, but AI Act substantive requirements (data quality, data governance, transparency, human oversight, record-keeping) must be addressed
- Clinical validation requirements: rigorous testing in real-world scenarios across diverse populations mandatory; FDA requires bias analysis/mitigation documentation; EU MDR requires clinical data from well-designed studies with representative patient populations
- Transparency and labeling: FDA recommends manufacturers update labeling when AI modifications are implemented under PCCP; EU AI Act requires transparency to users about AI system capabilities and limitations
- EMA-FDA joint 10 principles for AI in medicine development (published January 2026): human-centric risk-based approach, proportional validation, context of use definition, adherence to applicable standards, robust data governance, multidisciplinary expertise, transparent model development, lifecycle performance monitoring, and communication of AI limitations
- WHO ethics guidance for AI in health (updated January 2024 for Large Multimodal Models): over 40 recommendations covering diagnosis, clinical care, scientific research, data privacy, accountability, and equitable access
- Post-market surveillance: FDA requires ongoing monitoring of deployed AI devices; EU MDR requires post-market clinical follow-up (PMCF) and vigilance reporting

**Risk Classification:** US FDA: AI medical devices classified as Class I (exempt, self-certified), Class II (510(k) or De Novo pathway, moderate risk), or Class III (PMA pathway, highest risk). Over 96% of current AI devices are Class II cleared via 510(k). EU: MDR classification rules in Annex VIII determine risk class (Class I through Class III); AI devices classified as Class IIa and above require Notified Body involvement and therefore qualify as high-risk under the AI Act Article 6(1). Class I self-certified devices do NOT qualify as high-risk under the AI Act. The AI Act adds an additional layer of risk assessment on top of the MDR classification.

**Covered Entities:** US: manufacturers (domestic and foreign), sponsors, and any entity marketing AI/ML-enabled devices in the US market. EU: manufacturers, authorized representatives, importers, and notified bodies under MDR/IVDR; AI Act extends to AI providers, deployers, and distributors for AI-specific obligations. Globally: WHO guidance targets governments, technology companies, and healthcare providers.

**Sector Scope:** Healthcare and life sciences specifically — covers AI/ML-enabled Software as a Medical Device (SaMD), AI components embedded in hardware medical devices, clinical decision support (CDS) software (with specific FDA exemptions for certain CDS tools), AI in in vitro diagnostics (IVDs under IVDR), and AI in drug development (EMA-FDA joint principles). FDA's January 2026 guidance expanded exemptions for wellness software and certain wearable devices.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** US: FDA jurisdiction covers any AI medical device marketed in the United States, regardless of where the manufacturer is located; foreign manufacturers must register with the FDA and designate a US agent. EU: MDR and AI Act apply to any device placed on the EU market; non-EU manufacturers must appoint an authorized representative in the EU. The dual-regulation model (MDR + AI Act) means foreign manufacturers selling AI medical devices into the EU face both MDR conformity assessment and AI Act compliance obligations. IMDRF coordination efforts aim to reduce duplicative requirements across jurisdictions but fundamental differences persist.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** US FDA: enforcement tools include warning letters, import alerts, injunctions, seizures, consent decrees, civil monetary penalties, and criminal prosecution for fraudulent marketing of unapproved devices. In 2025, the FDA issued 19 warning letters citing Quality System Regulation violations by early September, surpassing the same-period total from 2024. EU: non-compliance with AI Act prohibited practices: up to EUR 35 million or 7% of worldwide annual turnover; non-compliance with high-risk AI requirements: up to EUR 15 million or 3% of worldwide turnover; MDR violations: Member State-level penalties including device withdrawal, market bans, and fines determined by national law. Additionally, under the new EU Product Liability Directive (in force December 2024), non-compliance with mandatory safety requirements creates a rebuttable presumption of defect triggering strict liability.

**Enforcement Body:** US: FDA Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH) and its Digital Health Center of Excellence (DHCoE, established 2017); EU: National competent authorities and Notified Bodies for MDR/IVDR; European AI Office for AI Act GPAI-specific obligations; Medical Device Coordination Group (MDCG) and joint AI Board (AIB) for guidance on MDR-AI Act interplay; EUDAMED database (not yet fully operational) for device registration and vigilance. Internationally: IMDRF provides harmonization framework; WHO GI-AI4H coordinates global governance standards.

**Compliance Timeline:** FDA: PCCP final guidance effective December 2024; TPLC draft guidance issued January 2025 (comment period followed by finalization expected 2025-2026); FDA January 2026 guidance reducing oversight of certain digital health products including AI-enabled wellness software. EU: MDR has been in force since May 2021; AI Act high-risk provisions for medical devices (Annex I products) apply from 2 August 2027; Digital Omnibus proposal (November 2025) may further extend this deadline as harmonised standards are behind schedule; MDCG 2025-6 joint guidance with AIB issued June 2025 for interim compliance clarification; MDR/IVDR simplification proposal adopted by Commission December 16, 2025. IMDRF: ongoing work on Machine Learning-enabled Medical Device definitions and GMLP adoption.

**Reporting Requirements:** FDA: premarket submissions (510(k), De Novo, PMA) must include AI-specific documentation per draft TPLC guidance (model description, data lineage, performance metrics, bias analysis, human-AI workflow, monitoring plan); PCCP documentation (modification description, protocol, impact assessment); post-market: Medical Device Reporting (MDR) for adverse events, annual reports, periodic quality system audits. EU: MDR requires EU Declaration of Conformity, technical documentation per Annex II/III, clinical evaluation report, PMCF plan and report, serious incident reporting within 15 days (or immediately for serious public health threats), trend reporting, periodic safety update reports (PSUR); AI Act adds: conformity assessment documentation, risk management system, data governance records, logging capabilities, transparency information, and registration in EU database.

**Liability Model:** product_liability (US and EU), with unresolved questions. US: Dual liability framework — product liability claims against manufacturers for defective AI devices, and medical malpractice claims against healthcare providers who use AI tools; the 2024 ALI Restatement of Medical Malpractice shifts toward a 'reasonable care' standard that may evolve to include AI tool use as standard of care. EU: Product Liability Directive (December 2024) creates rebuttable presumption of defect for non-compliant AI medical devices; the AI Liability Directive was WITHDRAWN in February 2025, leaving a gap in fault-based AI liability harmonization. Key open questions: liability apportionment between AI developer, device manufacturer, deploying hospital, and treating physician remains unclear, especially for 'black box' neural network models where error attribution is difficult.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** LARGE ENTERPRISES: Major medtech companies (e.g., GE Healthcare, Siemens Healthineers, Philips) dominate FDA AI device approvals and can absorb dual US/EU compliance costs; they benefit from PCCPs enabling faster iteration cycles; the 510(k) pathway's reliance on predicates advantages incumbents with existing cleared devices. STARTUPS AND SMEs: AI healthcare startups raised $10.7 billion in 2025 (up 24.4% from 2024), capturing 62% of digital health VC funding at an 83% premium over non-AI startups — demonstrating strong investor appetite despite regulatory burden; however, regulatory pathway represents one of the most significant early hurdles; FDA clearance timelines (median 142 days for 510(k)) and costs create cash flow challenges; EU dual-regulation (MDR + AI Act) is particularly onerous for startups lacking dedicated regulatory teams; estimated EU compliance costs of EUR 50,000-330,000+ per high-risk system can consume significant portions of early-stage funding. MARKET DYNAMICS: SaMD represents 62% of all AI/ML clearances in 2025; over 1,250 AI-enabled devices now authorized by FDA (up from 950 in August 2024); the compliance-as-a-service market for AI medical devices is growing rapidly, creating opportunities for RegTech startups. GEOGRAPHIC ARBITRAGE: The divergence between US deregulation (FDA reducing oversight in January 2026) and EU tightening (AI Act layered on MDR) may incentivize companies to prioritize US market entry before tackling EU requirements.

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed signals across jurisdictions. US: PROMOTING innovation through (1) PCCP mechanism enabling pre-authorized AI model updates without new submissions, (2) 510(k) pathway providing relatively fast clearance for iterative improvements, (3) Trump administration's January 2026 deregulation of certain digital health and wellness AI tools, (4) regulatory sandboxes proposed in July 2025 AI Action Plan. CONSTRAINING through (1) DOGE-fueled layoffs at FDA CDRH cutting ~15% of staff including AI reviewers (as of September 2025), potentially slowing review times, (2) uncertainty about regulatory stability across administrations. EU: CONSTRAINING through (1) dual MDR + AI Act compliance burden, (2) harmonised standards behind schedule, (3) notified body capacity constraints, (4) MDCG 2025-6 leaving some interplay questions unanswered. PROMOTING through (1) regulatory sandboxes mandatory in each Member State by August 2026, (2) Digital Omnibus extending timelines, (3) MDR/IVDR simplification proposal. GLOBALLY: IMDRF harmonization and EMA-FDA joint principles help reduce fragmentation but 'process control' vs 'comprehensive validation' paradigm conflicts persist.

**Global Influence:** Healthcare AI regulation is a key battleground for regulatory influence. The FDA's TPLC/PCCP approach is being studied globally as a model for adaptive AI regulation. The EU's dual MDR + AI Act framework, while more burdensome, may set the standard for safety-first jurisdictions. However, unlike GDPR's strong Brussels effect, AI medical device regulation faces competing models: (1) UK MHRA is pursuing an agile, pro-innovation approach with separate AI as a Medical Device (AIaMD) framework; (2) China's NMPA has approved hundreds of AI medical devices under its own framework influenced by but distinct from IMDRF; (3) South Korea's MFDS spearheaded IMDRF's ML device definitions; (4) Japan and South Korea are developing hybrid adaptive models. The EMA-FDA joint principles (January 2026) represent the most significant transatlantic convergence in AI health governance but remain non-binding. WHO's GI-AI4H is working to establish governance standards especially for low- and middle-income countries.

**Stakeholder Positions:** MEDTECH INDUSTRY: Generally supports FDA's PCCP approach as enabling innovation; concerned about EU dual-regulation burden — MedTechEurope has lobbied for Digital Omnibus concessions; large players like Siemens Healthineers and Philips can navigate both systems but prefer harmonization. AI STARTUPS: Welcome US deregulation signals but worry about FDA staff cuts reducing review capacity; view EU compliance as a significant market access barrier. HEALTHCARE PROVIDERS: American Hospital Association (AHA) submitted comments to FDA in December 2025 on AI-enabled device oversight, advocating for transparency and clinical validation requirements; physicians' organizations emphasize maintaining human oversight and liability clarity. PATIENT ADVOCATES: Concerned about deregulation reducing safety oversight, particularly given FDA staff cuts and DOGE influence; support transparency requirements and diverse clinical validation. REGULATORS: FDA Commissioner Marty Makary's stated priority of 'Silicon Valley speed' regulation raises concerns about adequate safety review; EU regulators acknowledge implementation challenges and delayed harmonised standards. CIVIL SOCIETY: Organizations like the Bipartisan Policy Center (US) call for clearer framework distinguishing FDA-regulated vs non-regulated health AI tools; concern that many AI health tools fall outside any regulatory framework.

**Deregulatory Signals:** Significant deregulatory activity in the US, moderate in the EU. US: (1) FDA January 2026 guidance reducing oversight of certain AI-enabled digital health products and wearables, explicitly following Trump administration deregulation directives; (2) Clinical Decision Support software exemptions expanded — AI matching patient data to treatment guidelines exempt if not replacing provider judgment; (3) FDA Commissioner Makary's stated goal of regulation at 'Silicon Valley speed'; (4) DOGE-driven FDA staffing cuts (~2,500 positions, 15% reduction by September 2025) including AI review personnel at CDRH, which de facto reduces enforcement capacity even where rules remain unchanged; (5) Trump July 2025 AI Action Plan encouraging regulatory sandboxes and Centers of Excellence. EU: (1) Digital Omnibus proposal (November 2025) extends AI Act high-risk compliance deadlines; (2) MDR/IVDR simplification proposal (December 2025) reduces some device classification burdens; (3) MDCG 2025-6 confirms MDR conformity assessment takes precedence, avoiding duplication with AI Act. Net trend: US moving toward lighter-touch/faster approval model while EU maintains comprehensive safety-first approach but with implementation delays and simplification concessions.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689) — classifies AI medical devices as high-risk under Article 6(1)/Annex I
- EU Medical Device Regulation (MDR, Regulation (EU) 2017/745) — primary EU medical device regulation, in force since May 2021
- EU In Vitro Diagnostic Regulation (IVDR, Regulation (EU) 2017/746) — covers AI in diagnostic devices
- EU Digital Omnibus on AI (November 2025 proposal) — extends compliance deadlines for medical device AI
- EU Product Liability Directive (Directive (EU) 2024/2853) — strict liability for defective AI medical devices
- GDPR (Regulation (EU) 2016/679) — applies to personal health data processed by AI medical devices
- US Executive Order 14110 on AI (Biden, October 2023) — included health AI provisions, partially rescinded by Trump
- Trump AI Executive Orders 2025 — deregulatory direction affecting FDA oversight
- HIPAA (US) — health data privacy framework intersecting with AI device data requirements
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework — voluntary US framework applicable to AI medical devices
- ISO/IEC 42001 — AI management system standard relevant to medical device manufacturers
- IEC 62304 — medical device software lifecycle standard, applicable to AI/ML SaMD
- ISO 14971 — risk management for medical devices, applied to AI-specific risks
- OECD AI Principles — referenced in international AI governance for health

**Key Actors:**

- FDA Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH) — primary US regulator for AI medical devices
- FDA Digital Health Center of Excellence (DHCoE) — coordinates AI/digital health policy
- FDA Commissioner Marty Makary — appointed by Trump administration, prioritizing deregulation and speed
- European Medicines Agency (EMA) — co-authored joint AI principles with FDA for medicines lifecycle
- Medical Device Coordination Group (MDCG) — issued 2025-6 guidance on AI Act-MDR interplay
- EU AI Board (AIB) — joint collaboration with MDCG on medical device AI guidance
- European AI Office — oversight of GPAI obligations intersecting with medical AI
- International Medical Device Regulators Forum (IMDRF) — leading harmonization on AI/ML device definitions and GMLP
- World Health Organization (WHO) — issued ethics and governance guidance for AI in health (2021, updated 2024)
- UK MHRA (Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency) — co-authored GMLP principles with FDA and Health Canada; developing separate AIaMD framework
- Health Canada — co-authored GMLP principles; partner in international harmonization
- South Korea MFDS — spearheaded IMDRF ML device key terms and definitions
- China NMPA (National Medical Products Administration) — approved hundreds of AI devices under domestic framework
- MedTechEurope — industry association lobbying for Digital Omnibus concessions and MDR simplification
- American Hospital Association (AHA) — submitted December 2025 comments to FDA on AI device oversight
- Major AI medtech companies: GE Healthcare, Siemens Healthineers, Philips, Aidoc, Viz.ai, Tempus, Butterfly Network

**Recent Developments:**

- December 2024: FDA publishes final PCCP guidance for AI-enabled device software functions, expanding scope from ML-only to all AI-enabled devices
- January 2025: FDA issues draft TPLC guidance for AI-enabled device software functions, recommending comprehensive submission documentation (model description, data lineage, bias analysis, human-AI workflow, monitoring)
- February 2025: DOGE-driven layoffs at FDA hit CDRH AI division, approximately 2,500 positions cut (15% of FDA workforce by September 2025), potentially slowing AI device review capacity
- June 2025: MDCG releases joint guidance (MDCG 2025-6) with AIB clarifying how EU AI Act applies to AI-enabled medical devices under MDR/IVDR — MDR conformity assessment takes precedence
- July 2025: Trump administration releases AI Action Plan encouraging regulatory sandboxes and AI Centers of Excellence for healthcare
- August 2025: FDA issues guidance on AI-enabled medical devices transparency, bias, and lifecycle oversight
- 2025 full year: 295 FDA AI/ML device clearances (record high), cumulative total exceeds 1,250; SaMD represents 62% of clearances; median clearance time 142 days
- November 2025: EU Digital Omnibus proposal extends AI Act high-risk compliance deadlines, acknowledges harmonised standards behind schedule; confirms single conformity assessment for MDR+AI Act
- December 2025: EU Commission adopts proposed revisions to MDR/IVDR simplification; AHA submits comments to FDA on AI-enabled device oversight
- January 2026: FDA announces sweeping reduction of oversight for certain AI-enabled digital health products and wearables, following Trump deregulation directives; clinical decision support exemptions expanded
- January 2026: EMA and FDA jointly publish 10 principles for AI in medicine development, covering the full lifecycle from early research to post-market monitoring
- February 2026: EU RAPS reports that Commission MDR/IVDR changes include software classification modifications impacting AI medical device regulation

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** AI healthcare regulation is a critical case study for AI entrepreneurs at the intersection of innovation and safety. KEY LESSONS: (1) REGULATORY PATHWAY AS BUSINESS STRATEGY — choosing between 510(k) (faster, cheaper, 96% of approvals) vs De Novo (novel devices, establishes new predicate) fundamentally shapes product roadmap and fundraising timeline; PCCP mechanism is a game-changer enabling pre-authorized iterative improvements without re-submission. (2) GEOGRAPHIC MARKET ENTRY SEQUENCING — US deregulation trend (FDA reducing oversight, faster reviews) vs EU dual-regulation burden (MDR + AI Act) creates a strategic choice: most AI medtech startups launch in the US first, then tackle EU compliance as a second phase; this divergence is a textbook example of regulatory arbitrage. (3) COMPLIANCE AS COMPETITIVE MOAT — despite high costs (EUR 50K-330K+ per high-risk system in EU), regulatory clearance creates significant barriers to entry; companies with cleared AI devices benefit from both patient/provider trust and legal market access that competitors must replicate. (4) FUNDING DYNAMICS — AI healthcare startups raised $10.7B in 2025 (24.4% increase), capturing 62% of digital health VC at an 83% premium over non-AI startups; investors view regulatory navigation as manageable given the market opportunity, but require clear regulatory strategy in due diligence. (5) BUILD vs BUY DECISION — the PCCP mechanism and 510(k) predicate system favor incumbent advantage, making 'buy' (partnership with cleared device companies) potentially more efficient for startups than 'build' (seeking own clearance). (6) EU SANDBOX OPPORTUNITY — mandatory regulatory sandboxes in each EU Member State by August 2026 create a path for resource-constrained startups to test AI medical devices under regulatory guidance. (7) TALENT AND TEAM — GMLP principles require multidisciplinary expertise (clinicians, data scientists, regulatory affairs); building this team early is essential for fundability. (8) LIABILITY AWARENESS — the unresolved liability question (product liability vs malpractice) means AI health entrepreneurs must carefully structure contractual risk allocation with hospital customers.

**Session Mapping:** Session 3 (Framing & Managing AI Projects — CRISP-DM, AI Canvas, Build vs Buy) and Session 5 (Ethics, Governance & Final Presentations — EU AI Act, bias, regulation)

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official FDA guidance documents, EU Commission legal texts, peer-reviewed publications in PMC/Nature/Frontiers, authoritative industry analyses from Innolitics, IntuitionLabs, and major law firms (King & Spalding, Ropes & Gray, DLA Piper), plus reporting from STAT News, MedTech Dive, and RAPS

**Cross Reference:** FDA device clearance data verified across Innolitics (295 clearances in 2025) and IntuitionLabs (1,250+ cumulative); EU regulatory timeline confirmed across multiple law firm analyses and official MDCG documents; PCCP guidance details consistent across FDA official document, Ropes & Gray, King & Spalding, and MedCrypt analyses; DOGE staffing cuts reported by STAT News and Fortune, corroborated by FDA inspection data from Hogan Lovells; VC funding data cross-referenced between Crunchbase, Menlo Ventures, and Fierce Healthcare

**Caveats:** RAPIDLY EVOLVING: (1) FDA staff cuts under DOGE may significantly change review capacity and timelines — the full impact is not yet clear as of February 2026; (2) EU Digital Omnibus and MDR/IVDR simplification proposals are still in legislative process and may be modified; (3) EU harmonised standards for AI Act high-risk requirements are behind schedule, creating implementation uncertainty; (4) Liability frameworks for AI medical devices remain unsettled in both the US and EU, with the EU AI Liability Directive withdrawal leaving a gap; (5) The 1,250+ FDA-authorized device figure comes from FDA's public database but the exact categorization methodology has been questioned by some researchers; (6) China's NMPA device approvals are harder to verify independently; (7) US regulatory direction is subject to political change with potential administration shifts

### 12. Algorithmic Impact Assessments (AIAs)

_Source: `Algorithmic_Impact_Assessments.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** Algorithmic Impact Assessments (AIAs)

**Type:** framework

**Jurisdiction:** Global (originating from Canada, adopted/adapted in EU, US, New Zealand, Brazil, and others)

**Status:** Mixed — mandatory in Canada (since 2019), mandatory under EU AI Act Article 27 (FRIA, effective August 2026), voluntary/emerging in US (federal bill proposed), mandatory at US state level (Colorado SB 24-205, NYC LL 144)

**Effective Date:** Canada: April 2019 (Directive on Automated Decision-Making). EU AI Act Article 27 (FRIA): August 2, 2026. Colorado AI Act: June 30, 2026. NYC Local Law 144: July 5, 2023. US Algorithmic Accountability Act of 2025: introduced June 2025, not yet enacted. Brazil AI Bill: approved by Senate December 2024, pending House review.

**Regulatory Philosophy:** comprehensive_horizontal (EU FRIA under AI Act) | patchwork_federalism (US state-level mandates) | voluntary_principles (OECD guidance, New Zealand Algorithm Charter) | sector_specific (NYC employment focus)

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** Algorithmic Impact Assessments (AIAs) are structured pre-deployment evaluation processes requiring organizations to identify, measure, and mitigate potential harms of automated decision-making systems before they are deployed. Canada pioneered the mandatory government AIA in 2019 under its Directive on Automated Decision-Making, using a 65-question risk assessment plus 41 mitigation questions to classify systems into four impact levels. The EU AI Act mandates Fundamental Rights Impact Assessments (FRIAs) under Article 27 for deployers of high-risk AI systems, effective August 2026, while US approaches remain fragmented across state laws (Colorado, NYC) and proposed federal legislation.

**Key Provisions:**

- Pre-deployment risk assessment: Organizations must evaluate AI systems before use, assessing potential harms across fairness, bias, privacy, and fundamental rights dimensions
- Impact level classification: Canada's AIA assigns systems to four levels (I-IV) based on risk severity, duration, reversibility, and data sensitivity, with escalating mitigation requirements
- Proportionate mitigation requirements: Higher impact levels trigger stricter obligations — Level II+ requires peer review, Level III-IV requires explanation capabilities, Level IV requires two or more expert reviewers
- Public transparency: Completed assessments must be published (Canada: Open Government Portal; NYC: employer websites; EU: notification to market surveillance authorities)
- Human oversight mandates: All frameworks require documentation of human oversight mechanisms and escalation procedures
- Ongoing monitoring and updates: Assessments must be repeated periodically (Colorado: annually and within 90 days of substantial modifications; EU FRIA: updated when underlying facts change)
- Stakeholder and affected community consultation: AI Now Institute framework and Ada Lovelace Institute templates require participatory engagement with affected populations
- Gender-Based Analysis Plus (GBA+): Canada's 2023 AIA update added intersectional analysis considering age, disability, ethnicity, economic status, geography, and sexual orientation
- Bias audit requirements: NYC Local Law 144 mandates annual independent bias audits of automated employment decision tools, testing for disparate impact by sex, ethnicity, and race
- Notification obligations: NYC requires candidate notification before AEDT use; EU requires deployer notification to market surveillance authorities with filled-out FRIA template

**Risk Classification:** Canada uses four impact levels (I-IV): Level I (little impact, minimal requirements), Level II (moderate impact, peer review required), Level III (high impact, expert consultation and explanation capability), Level IV (very high impact, robust peer review by multiple experts, full transparency). The EU AI Act uses a risk-based pyramid (unacceptable, high, limited, minimal risk) where FRIAs apply specifically to high-risk AI systems under Annex III. Colorado categorizes 'high-risk' AI systems based on consequential decision domains (employment, housing, credit, healthcare, education, insurance).

**Covered Entities:** Canada: federal government departments and agencies using automated decision systems. EU FRIA: public bodies and private entities providing public services, plus deployers of creditworthiness, insurance risk, and employment AI systems. Colorado: developers and deployers of high-risk AI systems. NYC LL 144: employers and employment agencies using AEDTs in New York City. Proposed US Algorithmic Accountability Act: large companies using automated decision systems for consequential decisions.

**Sector Scope:** Cross-sector in principle, but with particular focus on: public administration and government services (Canada), employment and hiring (NYC LL 144), credit scoring and insurance (EU FRIA), healthcare, housing, education, and criminal justice (Colorado AI Act, proposed US federal legislation). Canada's Directive exempts internal-only government systems and national security systems.

**Open Source Treatment:** Canada's AIA tool itself is open source, published under an open license on GitHub (canada-ca/aia-eia-js) for public sharing and reuse. The Inter-American Development Bank has adapted it for Latin American contexts. The assessment frameworks do not generally create special exemptions for open-source AI models — the focus is on the deployment context and decision impact rather than the model's licensing. However, the EU AI Act's broader open-source exemptions (reduced obligations for open-source providers) may interact with FRIA requirements, since FRIAs are deployer obligations, not provider obligations.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** EU FRIA: applies to any entity deploying high-risk AI systems within the EU, regardless of where the entity is headquartered (Brussels Effect). NYC LL 144: applies to any employer using AEDTs for employment decisions affecting NYC candidates/employees. Colorado: applies to deployers operating within Colorado. Canada's Directive is limited to federal government institutions. The proposed US Algorithmic Accountability Act would apply to large US companies meeting revenue and data thresholds, with potential implications for foreign companies operating in the US market.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** NYC LL 144: $500 civil penalty for first violation and each additional same-day violation; $500-$1,500 for each subsequent violation. Colorado AI Act: enforced by the Attorney General, with penalties under the Colorado Consumer Protection Act. EU AI Act: fines up to 35 million EUR or 7% of global annual turnover for prohibited practices, lower tiers for other violations. Canada: no direct fines under the Directive, but non-compliance may result in administrative consequences within the federal government. Proposed US Algorithmic Accountability Act: violations treated as unfair or deceptive acts under FTC Act.

**Enforcement Body:** Canada: Treasury Board of Canada Secretariat (policy owner). EU: National market surveillance authorities in each Member State, coordinated by the European AI Office. NYC: Department of Consumer and Worker Protection (DCWP). Colorado: State Attorney General. Proposed US federal: Federal Trade Commission (FTC).

**Compliance Timeline:** Canada: in force since April 2019, systems procured after April 2020 must comply, 4th review consultations closed January 2025. EU FRIA (Article 27): effective August 2, 2026, with standardized templates to be provided by the AI Office before that date. Colorado: effective June 30, 2026 (delayed from February 1, 2026), initial impact assessment due within 90 days, then annually. NYC LL 144: in force since July 5, 2023, annual bias audits required. Proposed US Algorithmic Accountability Act: if enacted, 12-24 month FTC rulemaking window expected.

**Reporting Requirements:** Canada: completed AIAs must be published on the Open Government Portal (22 published as of August 2024). EU FRIA: deployers must notify market surveillance authorities with completed assessment template. NYC LL 144: bias audit summary and date must be posted on employer website; candidate notification required before AEDT use. Colorado: impact assessment documentation must be maintained and provided to the Attorney General upon request within 90 days. Proposed US Algorithmic Accountability Act: impact assessments submitted to the FTC.

**Sme Provisions:** Limited explicit SME provisions in most AIA frameworks. EU AI Act includes general SME measures (regulatory sandboxes, reduced fees, simplified documentation for non-high-risk systems) but FRIAs apply equally to all deployers of covered high-risk systems regardless of size. The proposed US Algorithmic Accountability Act targets 'large companies' by setting revenue and data thresholds, effectively exempting small businesses. Canada's Directive only applies to federal government, so SME relevance is indirect (through procurement). NYC LL 144 applies to all employers regardless of size if they use AEDTs.

**Liability Model:** fault_based | none_specified — Most AIA frameworks emphasize compliance obligations rather than creating direct liability regimes. Colorado uses a 'reasonable care' standard (fault-based). NYC LL 144 imposes strict compliance penalties. The EU AI Act's liability framework is separate from FRIA obligations (see EU AI Liability Directive proposal). Canada's Directive operates within public administrative law rather than private liability.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:**

- For startups and SMEs: Compliance costs can be significant — research shows AI compliance can consume 25% of revenue for small companies, and a 200% increase in fixed compliance costs can turn a profitable startup into a money-losing one (operating margin from 13% to -7%). Solo advisors report spending 40% of hours on compliance, and individual DevOps engineers spend approximately 3 months per year on audit preparation.
- For large enterprises: AIAs are increasingly absorbed into existing compliance and governance frameworks (GRC, privacy impact assessments, ESG reporting). Companies like PwC, Deloitte, and Holistic AI offer AIA consulting services, creating a growing compliance industry.
- For AI vendors/developers: Must provide sufficient documentation and technical information for deployers to conduct their assessments. Colorado requires developers to publicly disclose risk summaries and report known discrimination risks to the Attorney General within 90 days.
- For public sector: Government agencies face the most mature requirements (Canada since 2019). The 22 published Canadian AIAs demonstrate the operational overhead of conducting thorough assessments across departments.
- Market access consideration: As FRIA requirements take effect in the EU (August 2026), companies selling AI systems to European public bodies or in covered sectors must ensure their systems are assessment-ready, potentially creating a 'documentation moat' favoring larger vendors.

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed evidence. Critics argue AIAs create compliance overhead that disproportionately burdens startups and may stifle innovation — over 60 European startup founders and investors signed open letters urging Brussels to delay implementation, with Mistral and Synthesia among prominent critics. However, proponents (OECD, Ada Lovelace Institute) argue that systematic risk assessment can improve product quality, reduce downstream liability, and build user trust. Canada's experience since 2019 suggests AIAs can coexist with continued government technology adoption. NYC LL 144's weak enforcement (only 18 out of 391 employers posted audit reports) suggests that poorly enforced AIAs may have limited innovation impact but also limited protective value.

**Global Influence:** Canada's AIA has been the most influential model internationally — its open-source tool has been adapted by the Inter-American Development Bank for Latin American governments and referenced by the OECD Observatory of Public Sector Innovation. The EU FRIA under Article 27 is expected to create a 'Brussels Effect' as companies globally prepare for compliance. The concept has influenced Brazil's AI Bill (algorithmic impact assessments for high-risk systems), New Zealand's Algorithm Charter, and the European Law Institute's Model Rules. The AI Now Institute's 2018 framework paper was foundational in establishing the intellectual case for mandatory AIAs.

**Stakeholder Positions:** Supporters: AI Now Institute (pioneered the concept in 2018), Ada Lovelace Institute (developed healthcare-specific templates), OECD (promotes as governance best practice), civil society organizations (Access Now, Algorithm Watch), academic researchers (Andrew Selbst at Harvard). Critics: European startup ecosystem (compliance burden concerns), some US business groups (oppose federal mandates), libertarian-leaning think tanks (view as innovation-stifling regulation). Mixed: NIST (provides voluntary risk management framework that can support AIAs but does not mandate them), Big Tech companies (publicly support responsible AI but lobby for flexibility in implementation).

**Deregulatory Signals:** US federal level: Trump's December 2025 Executive Order 'Ensuring a National Policy Framework for Artificial Intelligence' proposes preempting state AI laws inconsistent with federal deregulatory policy, which could undermine state-level AIA mandates like Colorado's. Colorado: implementation delayed from February 2026 to June 2026 after legislative impasse on amendments. NYC LL 144: weak enforcement by DCWP suggests de facto deregulation through non-enforcement — only 2 complaints received, minimal educational outreach conducted. However, new legislative proposals continue (Algorithmic Accountability Act of 2025, Eliminating Bias in Algorithmic Systems Act of 2026), indicating that the concept retains legislative support despite deregulatory headwinds.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (FRIA is Article 27 of the AI Act)
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework (EqualAI AIA tool aligns with NIST AI RMF Playbook)
- GDPR (Article 35 Data Protection Impact Assessment as conceptual precursor)
- Colorado AI Act SB 24-205
- US Algorithmic Accountability Act of 2025 (proposed)
- Canada Directive on Automated Decision-Making
- NYC Local Law 144
- Brazil AI Bill (PL 2338/2023)
- OECD AI Principles
- ISO/IEC 42001 (AI Management System standard)
- Trump EO on AI National Policy Framework (December 2025, state preemption implications)
- US State AI Laws Patchwork

**Key Actors:**

- Treasury Board of Canada Secretariat — developed and maintains the original mandatory AIA tool
- AI Now Institute (Dillon Reisman, Jason Schultz, Kate Crawford, Meredith Whittaker) — published foundational 2018 framework paper
- Andrew Selbst (Harvard JOLT) — influential academic work on institutional design of AIAs
- Ada Lovelace Institute — developed AIA user guide and healthcare-specific template
- EqualAI (Miriam Vogel, CEO) — created NIST-aligned AIA assessment tool
- European AI Office — responsible for providing standardized FRIA templates before August 2026
- Danish Institute for Human Rights — published FRIA guide for EU AI Act compliance
- OECD Observatory of Public Sector Innovation — catalogs and promotes AIA best practices
- World Privacy Forum (Pam Dixon) — published 'AI Governance on the Ground' research on Canada's AIA evolution
- Senator Ron Wyden (D-OR) — lead sponsor of US Algorithmic Accountability Act
- NYC Department of Consumer and Worker Protection (DCWP) — enforces Local Law 144
- Inter-American Development Bank — adapted Canada's AIA for Latin American use
- PwC, Deloitte, Holistic AI — major consulting firms offering AIA services

**Recent Developments:**

- June 2025: Algorithmic Accountability Act of 2025 introduced in US Senate (S. 2164) and House (H.R. 5511), directing FTC to require impact assessments of automated decision systems
- 2026: Eliminating Bias in Algorithmic Systems Act of 2026 introduced in US House (H.R. 7110), targeting bias in federal agency algorithmic systems
- January 2025: Canada closed consultations on 4th review of the Directive on Automated Decision-Making, proposing modifications based on 5+ years of operational experience
- August 2024: World Privacy Forum published 'AI Governance on the Ground' report documenting that 22 Canadian agencies had published AIAs, analyzing the evolution of the AIA process
- 2023-2024: Canada added Gender-Based Analysis Plus (GBA+) intersectional analysis to the AIA, requiring evaluation across age, disability, ethnicity, economic status, geography, language, race, religion, and sexual orientation
- December 2024: Brazilian Senate approved AI Bill requiring algorithmic impact assessments for high-risk systems, pending House review
- 2025: Colorado AI Act implementation delayed to June 30, 2026 after special legislative session failed to reach compromise on amendments
- December 2025: NYC State Comptroller audit found DCWP enforcement of LL 144 largely ineffective — only 1 out of 32 companies flagged for non-compliance by DCWP vs. 17 instances found by independent auditors
- 2024: Academic FAccT paper 'Null Compliance' documented that only 18 of 391 NYC employers posted required bias audit reports, revealing systemic enforcement failure
- August 2026 (upcoming): EU AI Act Article 27 FRIA obligations take effect for deployers of high-risk AI systems — European AI Office expected to provide standardized templates before deadline
- February 2024: NIST released AI RMF 2.0 update; July 2024: NIST published AI 600-1 Generative AI Profile, both informing AIA methodology
- December 2025: Trump Executive Order on AI National Policy Framework raises possibility of federal preemption of state-level AIA requirements

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:**

- Compliance cost planning: AI startups must budget for AIA compliance from the earliest stages — research shows compliance costs can consume 25% of revenue for small companies. Entrepreneurs should factor impact assessment costs into their unit economics and runway calculations.
- Market access strategy: As EU FRIA requirements take effect in August 2026, startups selling AI systems to European public bodies or in credit/insurance/employment sectors need 'assessment-ready' products with proper documentation. This creates both a barrier and a competitive moat for well-documented systems.
- Build vs. buy for compliance: Startups can choose between conducting AIAs in-house (using free tools like Canada's open-source AIA or EqualAI's template) or engaging consulting firms (PwC, Deloitte). The choice depends on risk level and available resources.
- Product differentiation through trustworthiness: Proactive impact assessment and transparency can become a selling point, especially for B2B and B2G (business-to-government) AI products where buyers increasingly require evidence of responsible AI practices.
- Geographic regulatory arbitrage: Different jurisdictions have different AIA requirements — a startup could initially target markets with lighter requirements, then expand to regulated markets as they mature. However, Trump's December 2025 EO potentially preempting state laws adds uncertainty to US state-level strategies.
- Employment AI is the regulatory frontier: NYC LL 144, Colorado's Act, and the proposed federal Algorithmic Accountability Act all heavily target employment/hiring AI — startups in HR tech and recruitment AI face the most immediate and concrete AIA obligations.
- Documentation as a product feature: Investors and enterprise customers increasingly ask about AI governance. Having completed AIAs demonstrates maturity and de-risks the product for buyers.
- Enforcement reality check: NYC's experience shows that enforcement can be weak (only 18/391 employers compliant), but entrepreneurs should not plan around non-enforcement — regulatory attention tends to increase over time, and reputational risk from non-compliance can be more damaging than fines.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary. Also relevant to Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) for the 'how to conduct an AIA' practical exercise, and Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) for compliance cost implications on unit economics.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official government sources (Canada.ca, Congress.gov, EU AI Act text), peer-reviewed academic research (FAccT 2024, Harvard JOLT), reputable policy organizations (AI Now Institute, Ada Lovelace Institute, OECD), and major law firms (Allen & Overy Shearman, Freshfields, Akin Gump).

**Cross Reference:**

- Canada AIA: Official Canada.ca documentation confirmed by OECD OPSI catalog, World Privacy Forum 2024 research, and Montreal AI Ethics Institute analysis
- EU FRIA Article 27: Confirmed across artificialintelligenceact.eu official text, multiple law firm analyses (A&O Shearman, Freshfields), Danish Institute for Human Rights guide, and Nemko Digital compliance guide
- NYC LL 144: Confirmed by official NYC.gov DCWP page, NYS Comptroller December 2025 audit, Deloitte analysis, and FAccT 2024 academic paper
- Colorado AI Act: Confirmed by official Colorado General Assembly page, multiple law firm analyses (Akin Gump, Clark Hill), and compliance guides (TrustArc, LogicGate)
- US Algorithmic Accountability Act: Confirmed by Congress.gov official text for both S. 2164 and H.R. 5511, GovTrack tracking
- Compliance cost data: Confirmed by PwC analysis, arxiv research paper (2301.13454), and multiple industry compliance guides

**Caveats:**

- The AIA landscape is evolving rapidly — the US Algorithmic Accountability Act of 2025 has not been enacted and may not pass in the current political environment given the Trump administration's deregulatory stance
- Trump's December 2025 Executive Order on AI National Policy Framework could preempt state-level AIA mandates (Colorado, potentially others), but the legal mechanism and scope of preemption remain unclear
- Colorado AI Act was already delayed once (February to June 2026) and may be further amended or delayed
- EU AI Act Article 27 FRIA templates have not yet been published by the European AI Office — practical implementation details remain uncertain
- NYC LL 144 enforcement has been widely criticized as ineffective — the December 2025 NYS Comptroller audit found systemic failures, and the law may be strengthened or replaced
- Brazil's AI Bill is still pending House approval and may be significantly amended
- Compliance cost figures cited are general AI compliance costs, not specific to AIA-only costs, which would be a subset
- The interaction between GDPR Article 35 DPIAs and EU AI Act Article 27 FRIAs creates potential for overlapping or conflicting assessment requirements — guidance on harmonization is still pending
