### 15. EU Artificial Intelligence Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689)

_Source: `EU_AI_Act.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** EU Artificial Intelligence Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689)

**Type:** legislation

**Jurisdiction:** EU (European Union, 27 Member States)

**Status:** in_force

**Effective Date:** Entered into force 1 August 2024; phased application: prohibited practices from 2 February 2025, GPAI obligations from 2 August 2025, most remaining rules from 2 August 2026, high-risk AI in regulated products from 2 August 2027

**Regulatory Philosophy:** comprehensive_horizontal

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The world's first comprehensive legal framework regulating artificial intelligence, adopting a risk-based approach that classifies AI systems into four tiers (unacceptable, high, limited, minimal risk) with proportionate obligations. It covers AI providers, deployers, importers, and distributors, with special rules for General-Purpose AI (GPAI) models including foundation models. The Act aims to ensure AI systems in the EU are safe, transparent, traceable, non-discriminatory, and environmentally friendly while fostering innovation.

**Key Provisions:**

- Risk-based classification system with four tiers: unacceptable (banned), high-risk (strict obligations), limited risk (transparency), and minimal risk (no restrictions)
- Eight prohibited AI practices including social scoring, subliminal manipulation, exploitation of vulnerabilities, real-time remote biometric identification in public spaces (with limited law enforcement exceptions), emotion recognition in workplaces/schools, untargeted scraping for facial recognition databases, and biometric categorisation inferring sensitive attributes
- High-risk AI systems must comply with risk management, data governance, technical documentation, transparency, human oversight, accuracy, robustness, and cybersecurity requirements
- Conformity assessment required for high-risk systems before market placement, with CE marking
- Transparency obligations for AI systems interacting with persons (chatbots must disclose AI nature), and for deepfakes/synthetic content (must be labelled)
- GPAI model obligations: technical documentation, copyright compliance policy, publicly available training data summary
- GPAI models with systemic risk (>10^25 FLOPs threshold): additional obligations including model evaluation, adversarial testing, incident reporting, cybersecurity protections
- Code of Practice for GPAI providers (final version published July 2025) as voluntary compliance mechanism
- AI literacy obligation: providers and deployers must ensure staff have sufficient AI competence (applicable since February 2025)
- Mandatory AI regulatory sandboxes: each Member State must establish at least one by August 2026
- Serious incident reporting to national authorities and AI Office without undue delay
- EU database registration for high-risk AI systems

**Risk Classification:** Four-tier risk framework: (1) Unacceptable risk - 8 banned practices violating fundamental rights; (2) High risk - safety components of regulated products (Annex I, e.g. medical devices, machinery) and stand-alone systems in 8 sensitive domains (Annex III: biometrics, critical infrastructure, education, employment, essential services, law enforcement, migration/border, justice/democracy); (3) Limited risk - transparency obligations for AI interacting with persons, deepfakes, emotion recognition, biometric categorisation; (4) Minimal risk - no mandatory obligations (e.g. spam filters, AI in video games). Separate regime for GPAI models with systemic risk threshold at 10^25 FLOPs of training compute.

**Covered Entities:** Providers (developers) of AI systems and GPAI models regardless of location if placed on EU market; deployers (users) established or located in the EU; importers and distributors; product manufacturers integrating AI; authorised representatives of non-EU providers. Excludes: AI for purely military/defence purposes, AI used exclusively for scientific R&D, free and open-source AI below systemic risk threshold (partial exemption), and AI systems released under free licenses before being put on the market.

**Sector Scope:** Horizontal regulation applying across all sectors, with specific high-risk designations for: biometrics, critical infrastructure (transport, energy, water, digital), education, employment and worker management, essential public/private services (social benefits, credit scoring, insurance, emergency services), law enforcement, migration/asylum/border control, and administration of justice/democratic processes. Exemptions: military/defence, scientific R&D (pre-market), personal non-professional use.

**Open Source Treatment:** Partial exemption for GPAI models released under qualifying free and open-source licenses (must allow access, use, modification, redistribution; weights, architecture, and usage info must be publicly available). Exempt from: technical documentation requirements (Art. 53(1a)-(1b)) and information provision to downstream providers. NOT exempt from: copyright compliance policy and training data summary publication. Critical limitation: open-source GPAI models classified as having systemic risk (>10^25 FLOPs) receive NO exemptions and must comply with all obligations. The July 2025 GPAI Guidelines clarified that monetisation of the model itself (not downstream services) may void the open-source exemption.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** Applies to entities outside the EU under three conditions (Article 2(1)): (a) providers placing AI systems or GPAI models on the EU market, regardless of establishment location; (b) deployers located in a third country where the AI system's output is used in the EU; (c) importers and distributors. Similar to GDPR's extraterritorial scope. Non-EU providers must appoint an authorised representative in the EU. The 'output-based' jurisdiction in Art. 2(1)(c) is particularly broad — if an AI system's output is used within the EU, even if both provider and deployer are outside the EU, the Act may apply.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** Three-tier fine structure: (1) Up to EUR 35 million or 7% of worldwide annual turnover (whichever is higher) for violations of prohibited AI practices; (2) Up to EUR 15 million or 3% of worldwide annual turnover for non-compliance with other obligations (high-risk requirements, GPAI rules, etc.); (3) Up to EUR 7.5 million or 1% of worldwide annual turnover for supplying incorrect/misleading information to authorities. For SMEs and startups, the lower of the two amounts (fixed or percentage) applies as a cap. For EU institutions/bodies: EUR 1.5 million for prohibited practices, EUR 750,000 for other violations. GPAI-specific fines under Art. 101: up to EUR 15 million or 3% of global turnover.

**Enforcement Body:** Dual-level enforcement: (1) National market surveillance authorities in each Member State handle AI system compliance; (2) European AI Office (within DG Connect, European Commission) has exclusive jurisdiction over GPAI model obligations. AI Office headed by Director Lucilla Sioli, with 140+ staff across 5 units. Additional governance: European AI Board (Member State representatives), Advisory Forum (stakeholders), Scientific Panel of independent experts.

**Compliance Timeline:** Phased over 3 years: 2 Feb 2025 - prohibited practices ban and AI literacy obligations apply; 2 Aug 2025 - GPAI model obligations, governance rules, and Code of Practice apply; 2 Aug 2026 - most remaining rules apply including high-risk AI systems under Annex III, transparency obligations, penalties regime (except GPAI penalties which started Aug 2025); 2 Aug 2027 - high-risk AI systems embedded in regulated products under Annex I (medical devices, machinery, etc.) and legacy GPAI models (placed on market before Aug 2025) must comply. NOTE: Digital Omnibus proposal (Nov 2025) may extend high-risk deadlines by up to 16 months pending adoption.

**Reporting Requirements:** High-risk AI systems: (1) Technical documentation demonstrating compliance before market placement; (2) EU declaration of conformity; (3) Registration in EU database; (4) Serious incident reporting to national authorities without undue delay; (5) Post-market monitoring system; (6) Quality management system documentation; (7) Logging and traceability capabilities. GPAI models: (1) Technical documentation including training/testing process info and evaluation results; (2) Sufficiently detailed training data summary (template published Aug 2025); (3) Copyright policy documentation; (4) Systemic risk models: adversarial testing results, incident reporting to AI Office. Deployers of high-risk AI: (1) Fundamental rights impact assessment for public bodies; (2) Monitoring obligations; (3) Data input record-keeping.

**Sme Provisions:** Dedicated provisions under Articles 57-62: (1) Priority access to AI regulatory sandboxes for SMEs and startups, provided free of charge with simplified procedures; (2) Proportionate conformity assessment fees based on size and development stage; (3) Commission commitment to regularly review and reduce compliance costs; (4) Member States must provide tailored guidance, training, and awareness activities; (5) Dedicated communication channels for SME questions; (6) Support for participation in standardisation processes; (7) Real-world testing within sandboxes with safe harbour from fines if following guidance in good faith; (8) Digital Omnibus proposal (Nov 2025) adds: simplified technical documentation for SMEs/SMCs, proportionate quality management obligations, and penalty mitigation for smaller entities.

**Liability Model:** product_liability (primary), with fault-based elements. The AI Act itself is primarily a product safety regulation (not a liability law), but non-compliance creates strong liability exposure through: (1) New Product Liability Directive (in force Dec 2024) — non-compliance with mandatory safety requirements creates a rebuttable presumption of defect, triggering strict liability; (2) The proposed AI Liability Directive (which would have introduced reversed burden of proof for fault-based claims) was WITHDRAWN by the Commission in February 2025 due to lack of consensus, leaving a regulatory gap in fault-based AI liability; (3) Existing national tort law regimes remain applicable. Net result: strict product liability via PLD for AI-as-product, but no harmonised fault-based liability framework for AI-as-service.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** Differential impact by company size: LARGE ENTERPRISES face significant but manageable compliance costs, most already have legal/compliance teams and can absorb the estimated EUR 52,000/year per high-risk AI system; many large US tech companies will comply voluntarily to maintain EU market access (de facto Brussels effect). STARTUPS AND SMEs face disproportionate burden — compliance costs estimated at $160,000-$330,000 per high-risk system, potentially consuming a significant share of early-stage funding; 18-50% of AI systems may qualify as high-risk (far exceeding the Commission's original 5-15% estimate). The compliance market itself is projected to reach EUR 17 billion by 2030, creating new business opportunities. Key concerns: (1) Classification uncertainty — unclear risk categorisation causes businesses to hesitate in AI adoption; (2) First-mover disadvantage — EU companies face compliance costs that non-EU competitors avoid; (3) Talent flight risk — founders warn of driving talent abroad; (4) Positive effects — compliance-as-a-service market, trust premium for EU AI companies, regulatory clarity enabling investment. The Digital Omnibus proposal (Nov 2025) attempts to address these concerns through extended timelines and simplified requirements for smaller entities.

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed evidence. CONSTRAINING factors: (1) 56 EU AI companies including Mistral AI and Aleph Alpha signed a July 2025 letter urging the Commission to 'pause and simplify' parts of the Act; (2) Compliance costs divert R&D spending; (3) Open letter from European CEOs requested a two-year 'clock-stop' citing negative impact on competitiveness; (4) Classification uncertainty creates regulatory chill — businesses hesitate to deploy AI. PROMOTING factors: (1) Regulatory sandboxes (mandatory in each Member State by Aug 2026) enable controlled experimentation; (2) Regulatory clarity may attract investment from risk-averse sectors; (3) EU AI companies may gain a trust and safety premium in global markets; (4) Apply AI Strategy (Oct 2025) and AI Continent Action Plan invest in European AI capabilities; (5) EUR 17B compliance market creates new innovation opportunities. Net assessment: short-term innovation drag likely, long-term effects depend on implementation quality and whether sandboxes/simplification measures prove effective.

**Global Influence:** The 'Brussels effect' for the AI Act is more limited than for GDPR. EVIDENCE OF INFLUENCE: (1) Brazil and Canada are drafting similar risk-based AI frameworks; (2) Large US tech companies will likely comply voluntarily for EU market access (de facto Brussels effect); (3) The GPAI Code of Practice may influence global AI safety norms; (4) Some scholars see it as a template for future international standards. LIMITATIONS: (1) Unlike GDPR, the AI Act faces competition from alternative regulatory models — UK, Japan, Singapore, Australia pursuing pro-innovation, less prescriptive approaches; (2) US under current administration is pursuing deregulation; (3) China has its own AI regulation model (state-control oriented); (4) Only 2 countries (Canada, Brazil) actively emulating the EU approach; (5) Brookings analysis: 'Europe alone will not be setting a comprehensive new international standard for AI'; (6) The EU itself is already walking back provisions via the Digital Omnibus, potentially undermining its regulatory authority.

**Stakeholder Positions:** INDUSTRY: Generally critical of complexity and costs. 56 EU AI companies (including Mistral AI, Aleph Alpha) signed July 2025 open letter urging pause/simplification. European CEO coalition requested two-year 'clock-stop'. Startup community warns of competitiveness loss and talent flight. France's position was heavily influenced by Mistral AI's lobbyist Cedric O (former Secretary of State for Digital Transition), who called the Act 'the death sentence of artificial intelligence in Europe'. CIVIL SOCIETY: Concerned that simplification efforts erode protections. CDT's Laura Cabrera: 'Postponing enforcement leaves people unprotected from harms of high-risk AI systems'. Article 19, Access Now, and other rights organisations argue the Code of Practice was watered down by industry lobbying. Corporate Europe Observatory documented Big Tech's outsized influence on negotiations. ACADEMIA: Mixed views — some scholars support the risk-based framework as proportionate, others warn of innovation drag. MEMBER STATES: France favoured lighter touch for GPAI (influenced by Mistral/national champions), Germany pushed for stronger SME protections, smaller states concerned about enforcement capacity.

**Deregulatory Signals:** Yes, significant deregulatory signals emerging since mid-2025. MECHANISMS: (1) Digital Omnibus proposal (Nov 2025) — extends high-risk AI deadlines by up to 16 months, adds SME/SMC simplifications, introduces EU-level regulatory sandbox, extends generative AI compliance deadline to Feb 2027; (2) Industry pressure — CEO letter requesting two-year 'clock-stop', open letters from 56 AI companies; (3) Commission rhetoric shift — emphasis on 'simplification' and 'competitiveness' (aligned with Draghi Report recommendations); (4) AI Liability Directive withdrawal (Feb 2025) — removed planned fault-based liability harmonisation; (5) Apply AI Strategy and AI Continent Action Plan (Oct 2025) signal pivot toward 'AI adoption' narrative. However, the core Act structure (risk tiers, prohibited practices) remains intact — the deregulatory signals primarily target implementation speed and compliance burden rather than fundamental architecture.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- GDPR (Regulation (EU) 2016/679) — data protection framework, AI systems must comply with GDPR for personal data processing
- Product Liability Directive (Directive (EU) 2024/2853) — non-compliance with AI Act safety requirements creates rebuttable presumption of defect
- AI Liability Directive — WITHDRAWN by Commission in February 2025, would have harmonised fault-based AI liability with reversed burden of proof
- General Product Safety Regulation (EU) 2023/988 — complements AI Act for consumer-facing AI products
- Cyber Resilience Act — cybersecurity requirements for digital products including AI
- Digital Services Act (DSA) — platform regulation, overlaps with AI Act on recommender systems and content moderation
- EU Copyright Directive (2019/790) — GPAI providers must comply with text and data mining rules
- EU AI Act Digital Omnibus (proposed Nov 2025) — simplification amendments currently in legislative process
- OECD AI Principles — international framework referenced in AI Act design
- EU AI Pact — voluntary initiative preceding mandatory AI Act obligations

**Key Actors:**

- European Commission (DG Connect) — proposed the Act (April 2021), oversees implementation
- European Parliament — co-legislator, key rapporteurs Brando Benifei (S&D, Italy) and Dragos Tudorache (Renew, Romania)
- Council of the EU — co-legislator, Spanish Presidency brokered final political agreement (Dec 2023)
- Thierry Breton — former Commissioner for Internal Market, championed the initial AI Act proposal
- Lucilla Sioli — Director of the European AI Office (140+ staff), leads GPAI enforcement
- Mistral AI — French AI startup, major lobbying force for lighter GPAI obligations through Cedric O
- Cedric O — Mistral AI lobbyist, former French Secretary of State for Digital Transition, influenced French negotiating position
- Mario Draghi — his competitiveness report reinforced narrative for regulatory simplification
- European AI Board — representatives from 27 Member States for cross-border coordination
- Advisory Forum — multi-stakeholder body (industry, civil society, academia) advising Commission
- Scientific Panel of Independent Experts — advises on GPAI model classification and systemic risk

**Recent Developments:**

- 2 Feb 2025: Prohibited AI practices ban and AI literacy obligations became applicable — first enforcement milestone
- 11 Feb 2025: European Commission withdrew the AI Liability Directive proposal, citing lack of consensus
- 2 Aug 2025: GPAI model obligations became applicable; rules for governance structures took effect; Code of Practice for GPAI published in final form (July 2025)
- 18 Jul 2025: Commission published draft Guidelines for GPAI model providers, clarifying definitions and scope
- Jul 2025: 56 EU AI companies including Mistral AI signed open letter urging Commission to simplify/pause parts of the Act
- Oct 2025: Commission launched Apply AI Strategy complementing AI Continent Action Plan, targeting AI adoption by SMEs
- 19 Nov 2025: European Commission published Digital Omnibus on AI proposal — proposes extending high-risk deadlines by up to 16 months, adding EU-level regulatory sandbox, simplifying SME obligations
- Jan 2026: Multiple Member States working on national AI sandbox frameworks ahead of Aug 2026 deadline
- Feedback period on Digital Omnibus open until 9 March 2026; legislative process in Council and Parliament ongoing
- As of Feb 2026: High-risk AI system rules (Annex III) scheduled for 2 Aug 2026 but may be delayed by Digital Omnibus adoption

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** Critical for any AI startup targeting European markets. KEY IMPLICATIONS: (1) COMPLIANCE COSTS — estimated EUR 52,000/year per high-risk AI system for conformity assessment, documentation, and monitoring; startups face $160,000-$330,000 total compliance costs, potentially requiring dedicated compliance hires or external consultants; (2) MARKET ACCESS — the Act's extraterritorial reach means even non-EU startups must comply if their AI outputs are used in the EU, creating a compliance barrier to entry but also a level playing field; (3) COMPETITIVE MOATS — compliance creates a trust premium and barrier to entry that benefits first-movers who invest in safety/transparency; EU-compliant AI products may command premium pricing in regulated sectors (healthcare, finance, HR); (4) BUSINESS MODEL IMPACT — some AI use cases are outright banned (social scoring, manipulative AI), others require heavy investment in human oversight and documentation; B2B AI companies must provide downstream deployers with sufficient information; (5) SANDBOX OPPORTUNITY — regulatory sandboxes offer startups a way to test innovative AI under regulatory guidance with safe harbour from fines; SMEs get priority access and fee waivers; (6) TIMING STRATEGY — the phased rollout and Digital Omnibus delays create a window for startups to prepare; understanding the timeline is a genuine competitive advantage; (7) OPEN SOURCE STRATEGY — releasing models under open licenses reduces some obligations but not all (copyright compliance still required), and systemic risk models get no exemptions regardless; (8) COMPLIANCE-AS-A-SERVICE — the EUR 17B compliance market creates startup opportunities in AI governance tools, auditing, documentation automation, and risk classification services.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary session for EU AI Act coverage. Also relevant to Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) for risk classification and compliance planning, and Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) for understanding regulatory impact on business model viability and market access.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official EU legal texts (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689), European Commission publications, European Parliament briefings, and analysis from major law firms (Orrick, DLA Piper, WilmerHale, Cooley). Timeline and penalty figures drawn directly from the Act's articles.

**Cross Reference:** European Commission official pages (digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu), artificialintelligenceact.eu (independent reference), EU AI Act Service Desk (ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu), European Parliament research service, Brookings Institution, CSIS, Carnegie Endowment, Corporate Europe Observatory, IAPP, Chatham House. Compliance cost estimates cross-referenced between CEPS, EU impact assessment, and industry surveys.

**Caveats:** The regulatory landscape is actively evolving: (1) The Digital Omnibus on AI (proposed Nov 2025) is in legislative process and could significantly alter implementation timelines and requirements — feedback period open until March 2026; (2) The AI Liability Directive withdrawal (Feb 2025) leaves a gap in fault-based liability that may be revisited; (3) GPAI systemic risk threshold (10^25 FLOPs) may be revised as compute capabilities evolve; (4) National implementation varies — Member States have discretion in penalty structures and sandbox design; (5) The Commission's GPAI Guidelines (July 2025) are non-binding and may be challenged; (6) Compliance cost estimates vary widely (5-50% of AI systems as high-risk depending on methodology); (7) Standardisation work by CEN/CENELEC for harmonised standards is ongoing and will materially affect compliance requirements.

### 16. EU AI Act Code of Practice for General-Purpose AI (GPAI)

_Source: `EU_AI_Act_Code_of_Practice_for_GPAI.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** EU AI Act Code of Practice for General-Purpose AI (GPAI)

**Type:** framework

**Jurisdiction:** EU

**Status:** voluntary

**Effective Date:** Published 10 July 2025; endorsed by AI Board and Commission on 1 August 2025; GPAI model obligations applicable from 2 August 2025

**Regulatory Philosophy:** comprehensive_horizontal

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The Code of Practice is a voluntary tool developed through a multi-stakeholder process involving nearly 1,000 participants, designed to help providers of general-purpose AI models demonstrate compliance with Articles 53 and 55 of the EU AI Act. It is structured in three chapters — Transparency, Copyright, and Safety & Security — and provides detailed measures and commitments that, if adhered to, grant a 'presumption of conformity' with the AI Act's GPAI obligations. Providers who sign the Code benefit from reduced administrative burden, increased legal certainty, and a collaborative approach with the AI Office during the first year.

**Key Provisions:**

- Transparency Chapter: Providers must complete a Model Documentation Form covering architecture, training methodologies, data provenance, computational resources, and energy consumption; documentation retained for at least 10 years; available to the AI Office and downstream providers on request within 14 days
- Copyright Chapter: Providers must establish a board-level copyright policy; only crawl lawfully accessible content; honor machine-readable rights reservations (robots.txt per RFC 9309); exclude sites flagged as persistent copyright infringers; deploy technical safeguards against copyright-infringing outputs; designate a contact point for rights holder complaints
- Safety & Security Chapter (systemic risk models only): Establish a Safety and Security Framework with pre-defined risk tiers and acceptance criteria; structured risk identification across 4 specified systemic risks (CBRN, loss of control, cyber offence, harmful manipulation); rigorous model evaluations including benchmarks, red teaming, and human uplift studies
- Serious incident reporting with tiered deadlines: 2 days for critical infrastructure disruption, 5 days for cybersecurity breaches, 10 days for death, 15 days for serious harm to health/rights/property/environment
- Safety and Security Model Reports required before market introduction, updated every 6 months for the most capable models, with mandatory external independent evaluator reports
- Organizational governance: clear assignment of systemic risk oversight, ownership, monitoring, and assurance roles; whistleblower protections; healthy risk culture promotion
- Cybersecurity controls: 256-bit encryption, zero-trust architecture, MFA, insider threat protections, red-teaming, bug bounty programs; security measures maintained until model parameters are publicly released or deleted
- Post-market monitoring: end-user feedback, bug bounties, community evaluations, code repository monitoring, watermarking and provenance tracking

**Risk Classification:** The Code applies the AI Act's GPAI risk tiers: (1) All GPAI models (training compute above 10^23 FLOPs) must comply with Transparency and Copyright chapters; (2) GPAI models with systemic risk (training compute above 10^25 FLOPs, or designated by Commission) must additionally comply with the Safety & Security chapter. Providers must define their own measurable risk tiers with pre-set acceptance thresholds and built-in safety margins.

**Covered Entities:** Providers of general-purpose AI models placed on the EU market; the Safety & Security chapter applies only to providers of GPAI models with systemic risk (e.g., GPT-4, Gemini 1.5 Pro, Grok 3, Claude 3.7 Sonnet)

**Sector Scope:** Cross-sector, horizontal — applies to all GPAI models regardless of downstream application domain

**Open Source Treatment:** Free and open-source GPAI models are exempt from the Transparency chapter's documentation obligations, provided they: (1) use a genuinely free and open-source license; (2) are not monetized through accessory support or professional services; and (3) publicly disclose model weights, architecture, and usage information. However, this exemption does NOT apply to GPAI models classified as having systemic risk — those must comply with all chapters regardless of open-source status. The Copyright chapter applies to all models including open-source.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** Yes, the Code applies to any provider placing a GPAI model on the EU market, regardless of where the provider is headquartered. Given the cost of training frontier AI models (>10^25 FLOPs), it is unlikely providers will develop different models for different jurisdictions, making the Code's requirements effectively global for the most advanced models.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** Non-compliance with GPAI-related AI Act provisions carries administrative fines of up to EUR 15 million or 3% of global annual turnover, whichever is higher. Fines apply from 2 August 2026 for models placed on the EU market after 2 August 2025, and from 2 August 2027 for models already on the market before 2 August 2025. The Code itself is voluntary, but compliance with the underlying AI Act is mandatory.

**Enforcement Body:** EU AI Office (primary enforcement for GPAI provisions), supported by the AI Board (composed of EU Member States) and national competent authorities

**Compliance Timeline:** AI Act entry into force: 1 August 2024. Prohibited AI systems + AI literacy: 2 February 2025. Code published: 10 July 2025. Code endorsed via adequacy decisions: 1 August 2025. GPAI rules applicable (new models): 2 August 2025. Enforcement actions begin: 2 August 2026. Legacy models compliance deadline: 2 August 2027. Full AI Act application (high-risk AI systems): 2 August 2026.

**Reporting Requirements:** Transparency: Model Documentation Form retained 10+ years, provided to AI Office on request. Safety: Safety and Security Model Report submitted before market introduction, updated every 6 months for most capable models. Framework notifications to AI Office within 5 business days. Serious incident reports within 2-15 days depending on severity. Additional documentation retained 10+ years. Final incident reports within 60 days of resolution. All documentation must be unredacted except where restricted by national security legislation.

**Sme Provisions:** SMEs and Small Mid-Cap Enterprises (SMCs) benefit from proportionate obligations: reduced detail levels in Safety and Security Model Reports; may seek AI Office assistance for post-market monitoring activities; an individual in the supervisory function may suffice for risk oversight instead of a dedicated committee; periodic supervisory assessment may replace full internal audit; SMEs may be exempt from regular reporting commitments to the AI Office per Article 56(5) while retaining the option to voluntarily comply.

**Liability Model:** none_specified

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** For large enterprises (frontier AI developers like OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, Microsoft): significant compliance investment required — Model Documentation Forms, Safety & Security Frameworks, Model Reports, external independent evaluations, cybersecurity infrastructure, 10-year documentation retention. However, signatories benefit from presumption of conformity and collaborative relationship with AI Office during the first year. For SMEs and startups: reduced obligations (proportionate documentation, AI Office assistance for monitoring), but the underlying technical requirements for safety evaluations, red-teaming, and cybersecurity may be prohibitively expensive for smaller firms developing systemic-risk models. Open-source developers receive meaningful exemptions from transparency documentation. Non-signatories face higher regulatory scrutiny and must demonstrate compliance through alternative means.

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed signals. Supporters argue the Code creates legal certainty and a level playing field that benefits responsible AI development. Critics — including CEOs of leading European companies who called for a two-year 'clock-stop' — argue it adds complexity and may slow European AI development. Google's Kent Walker stated it risks 'slowing down Europe's development and deployment of AI.' Meta refused to sign, calling it 'overreach that will stunt growth.' xAI signed only the Safety chapter, calling the copyright rules an overreach. The open-source exemptions were designed to avoid hampering open innovation, but the systemic-risk threshold means the most capable open models must still comply fully.

**Global Influence:** High potential Brussels effect. CSET Georgetown analysis suggests the Code represents 'the clearest articulation to date of how the EU expects frontier AI developers to test and secure their models' and 'may be an indication of the norms and practices that shape global expectations for frontier AI governance.' Because the Code applies at the model level and frontier models are too expensive to train separately for each jurisdiction, compliance is likely to become de facto global for signatories. The safety requirements go 'significantly beyond current best practices' according to CSET analysis. Chatham House notes the Code 'will be valuable for global governance' even if it has critics.

**Stakeholder Positions:** Signatories (26+ organizations): Amazon, Anthropic, Google, IBM, Microsoft, OpenAI, Aleph Alpha, Cohere, Mistral AI among others. Partial signatory: xAI (Safety & Security chapter only, refusing Transparency and Copyright). Notable refusal: Meta (Joel Kaplan called it 'overreach that will throttle frontier AI development in Europe'). No Chinese companies signed. Civil society concerns: CDT and others accused the drafting process of giving privileged access to US tech companies; some academics noted enforcement would require roughly triple the AI Office's mid-2025 staffing levels. European industry (e.g., SAP, Siemens CEOs) called for a two-year implementation pause. Legal scholars raised concerns about the drafting process lacking procedural safeguards and parliamentary oversight.

**Deregulatory Signals:** No active loosening. However, some political pressure exists: European CEOs called for a two-year 'clock-stop' on implementation, and there is political tension around the AI Act's impact on European competitiveness. The collaborative first-year approach (2 August 2025 to 2 August 2026) where the AI Office will not penalize signatories working in good faith could be seen as a soft delay. The Commission also published separate GPAI Guidelines in July 2025 to clarify scope, which narrowed some definitions (e.g., clarifying what counts as 'placing on the market').

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689) — parent legislation
- EU Copyright Directive (2019/790) — Article 4 text and data mining provisions referenced in Copyright chapter
- EU Whistleblowing Directive (2019) — referenced for whistleblower protections
- GPAI Guidelines (published 18 July 2025) — interpretive guidance on GPAI model definitions and scope
- Template for Summarizing Training Data in GPAI Models (published 24 July 2025)
- Code of Practice on Transparency of AI-Generated Content (first draft 17 December 2025) — separate code addressing marking/labelling obligations under AI Act
- GDPR — data protection obligations referenced throughout

**Key Actors:**

- European AI Office — facilitated drafting, chairs Signatory Taskforce, primary enforcement body
- AI Board — advisory body of EU Member States, endorsed Code via adequacy decision
- EU Scientific Panel of Independent Experts — advises AI Office on systemic risks
- 13 independent Chairs and Vice-Chairs — led the multi-stakeholder drafting process
- Mia Hoffmann (CSET Georgetown) — published influential analysis of Code's global impact potential
- Joel Kaplan (Meta) — public opposition to signing the Code
- Kent Walker (Google) — signed but warned of innovation slowdown
- OpenAI, Anthropic, Mistral AI — early signatories with public statements of support
- Center for Democracy and Technology (CDT) — civil society watchdog on the process
- Nearly 1,000 multi-stakeholder participants in the drafting process (GPAI providers, downstream providers, trade associations, academics, civil society)

**Recent Developments:**

- 10 July 2025: Final Code of Practice published after Closing Plenary on 3 July 2025
- 18 July 2025: European Commission published GPAI Guidelines clarifying key concepts (model definitions, lifecycle obligations, scope)
- 24 July 2025: Template for summarizing training data in GPAI models published (final version)
- 1 August 2025: AI Board and Commission endorsed Code via adequacy decisions
- 2 August 2025: GPAI model obligations became applicable for new models; 26+ organizations signed as initial signatories
- August 2025: Signatory Taskforce established, chaired by AI Office, to facilitate coherent application
- July 2025: xAI signed only Safety & Security chapter; Meta publicly refused to sign; European CEO coalition called for two-year implementation pause
- 17 December 2025: First draft of separate Code of Practice on transparency of AI-generated content published (marking and labelling)
- 2 August 2026 (upcoming): Full enforcement begins — Commission can impose fines for non-compliance; also marks applicability of high-risk AI system rules and transparency code for AI-generated content
- 2 August 2027: Deadline for legacy GPAI models (placed on market before 2 August 2025) to achieve compliance

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** Critical knowledge for any AI startup operating in or selling to the EU market. Key considerations: (1) COMPLIANCE COST — startups developing GPAI models above the 10^23 FLOPs threshold face mandatory transparency and copyright obligations; those above 10^25 FLOPs face the full Safety & Security regime including external evaluations and cybersecurity infrastructure, which can be extremely expensive; (2) MARKET ACCESS — signing the Code provides 'presumption of conformity' which reduces regulatory friction and signals trustworthiness to enterprise customers; not signing means higher scrutiny and need to demonstrate compliance through other means; (3) COMPETITIVE MOATS — compliance capability becomes a barrier to entry that benefits well-resourced incumbents; however, SME provisions and open-source exemptions partially level the playing field; (4) STRATEGIC DECISIONS — startups must decide whether to sign the Code (gaining legal certainty but accepting obligations) or develop alternative compliance frameworks; Meta's refusal shows large companies can take this path but at reputational cost; (5) OPEN SOURCE STRATEGY — releasing models under open-source licenses can reduce documentation burden, but systemic-risk models get no exemption; (6) BUILD vs BUY — using a GPAI model from a Code signatory (e.g., OpenAI, Google) as a downstream deployer may be simpler than developing your own model and bearing full provider obligations; (7) TIMELINE AWARENESS — enforcement begins August 2026, so startups have a defined window to prepare.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary. Also relevant to Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects — compliance as part of project planning) and Session 4 (AI business models & strategy — impact of regulation on unit economics and go-to-market).

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High

**Cross Reference:**

- European Commission official pages (digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu)
- EU AI Act community site (artificialintelligenceact.eu) — detailed overview published 30 July 2025
- CSET Georgetown analysis (Mia Hoffmann, 30 July 2025)
- Jones Day legal commentary (August 2025)
- Latham & Watkins analysis of GPAI obligations
- Skadden legal alert (August 2025)
- Chatham House analysis (August 2025)
- Code of Practice interactive website (code-of-practice.ai)
- Hugging Face open-source guide for GPAI providers

**Caveats:**

- The Code is voluntary — providers may choose alternative compliance paths, so its practical impact depends on adoption rates
- Enforcement starting August 2026 will reveal how the AI Office interprets the Code and what 'good faith efforts' mean in practice
- Uncertainty remains around modified GPAI models derived from pre-August 2025 originals: unclear whether the 2026 or 2027 enforcement deadline applies
- The AI Office's enforcement capacity is questioned — civil society estimates it needs roughly 3x its mid-2025 staffing to effectively monitor compliance
- Risk acceptance thresholds are defined by providers themselves, which CSET notes could allow commercial interests to override caution
- The separate Code of Practice on transparency of AI-generated content (first draft December 2025) may introduce additional obligations by mid-2026
- Political pressure for an implementation pause from European industry leaders could lead to adjustments
- Rapidly evolving field — new model releases, capability improvements, and geopolitical dynamics may require Code updates

### 17. European Artificial Intelligence Office (EU AI Office)

_Source: `EU_AI_Office.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** European Artificial Intelligence Office (EU AI Office)

**Type:** enforcement_body

**Jurisdiction:** EU

**Status:** in_force

**Effective Date:** Established by Commission Decision of 24 January 2024; published in Official Journal 14 February 2024; entered into force 21 February 2024; fully operational with unit appointments from 16 June 2024

**Regulatory Philosophy:** comprehensive_horizontal

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The EU AI Office is the central enforcement body for the EU AI Act, housed within the European Commission's Directorate-General for Communications Networks, Content and Technology (DG CONNECT). It holds exclusive supervisory and enforcement powers over providers of general-purpose AI (GPAI) models, coordinates uniform implementation of the AI Act across Member States, and fosters international cooperation on trustworthy AI. It also supports AI research, innovation, and policy coordination across the EU.

**Key Provisions:**

- Exclusive enforcement authority over providers of general-purpose AI (GPAI) models under the AI Act
- Power to request information and technical documentation from GPAI providers (Article 91 AI Act)
- Authority to conduct evaluations and tests on GPAI models, especially for systemic risk (Article 92 AI Act)
- Power to request risk mitigation measures or market recall of non-compliant models (Article 93 AI Act)
- Coordination of regulatory approach across EU Member States for uniform AI Act application
- Secretariat for the European AI Board, Scientific Panel of independent experts, and Advisory Forum
- Development and management of the General-Purpose AI Code of Practice (voluntary compliance tool)
- Support for AI regulatory sandboxes across Member States (each must establish at least one by August 2026)
- Coordination of GenAI4EU initiative to stimulate development of foundation models and applications
- International AI cooperation and representation of EU AI policy globally
- Publication of binding guidelines for GPAI providers on scope of obligations (published July 2025)
- Establishment of Signatory Taskforce for Code of Practice implementation monitoring

**Risk Classification:** The AI Office focuses specifically on GPAI models with two tiers: (1) all GPAI models (transparency, copyright compliance, training data summary obligations) and (2) GPAI models with systemic risk (additional safety evaluation, risk assessment, incident reporting, and cybersecurity obligations). Systemic risk is determined by compute thresholds (10^25 FLOPs) or Commission designation based on equivalent capability.

**Covered Entities:** Providers of general-purpose AI models (regardless of location if models are placed on the EU market), including both commercial and open-source providers above certain thresholds. Non-EU providers must appoint EU representatives.

**Sector Scope:** Cross-sector, horizontal scope covering all GPAI models regardless of downstream application domain. The AI Office does not directly enforce sector-specific high-risk AI system obligations (which fall to national competent authorities) but coordinates their uniform application.

**Open Source Treatment:** Open-source GPAI model providers benefit from reduced obligations under certain conditions: they must use a genuinely free and open-source license, not monetize the model through accessory support or professional services, and publicly disclose model weights, architecture, and usage information. Exempt open-source providers still must comply with copyright policy obligations and publish a training data summary. Critically, open-source exemptions do NOT apply to GPAI models classified as having systemic risk — these face the full set of obligations regardless of license type. Open-source models are generally exempt from the voluntary Code of Practice (except copyright chapter).

**Extraterritorial Reach:** The AI Office has significant extraterritorial reach. GPAI obligations apply regardless of provider location when models are placed on the EU market or their output is used within the EU. Non-EU providers (e.g., US-based companies like OpenAI, Meta, Google) must comply with all GPAI obligations and appoint authorized representatives within the EU. The Commission can impose fines on non-EU providers. This extraterritorial design is modeled on the GDPR's approach and intended to create a 'Brussels Effect' for global AI governance standards.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** For GPAI-specific violations (enforced by the AI Office from 2 August 2026): fines up to 3% of worldwide annual turnover or EUR 15 million, whichever is higher. For supplying incorrect, incomplete, or misleading information to the AI Office: up to 1% of global annual turnover or EUR 7.5 million. The broader AI Act penalty framework (enforced by national authorities for non-GPAI violations) includes: up to 7% of global annual turnover or EUR 35 million for prohibited AI practices, up to 3% or EUR 15 million for other obligations.

**Enforcement Body:** The EU AI Office itself, operating within DG CONNECT of the European Commission, is the sole enforcer for GPAI model obligations. For non-GPAI AI Act obligations, national competent authorities in each Member State are the primary enforcers, coordinated through the European AI Board.

**Compliance Timeline:** 24 January 2024: Commission Decision establishing the AI Office. 21 February 2024: Decision enters into force. 16 June 2024: Unit appointments effective, office becomes operational. 2 August 2025: GPAI model obligations enter into application (providers must comply; informal collaboration with AI Office expected). 10 July 2025: Final GPAI Code of Practice published. 2 August 2026: Commission enforcement powers for GPAI provisions fully applicable (fines can be levied). June 2026: Mandatory Commission review of whether AI Office has sufficient powers and resources. By August 2026: Each Member State must establish at least one AI regulatory sandbox.

**Reporting Requirements:** GPAI providers must maintain and make available technical documentation covering model training, testing, and evaluation processes. They must publish a sufficiently detailed summary of training data content (template provided by AI Office). Providers of GPAI models with systemic risk face additional obligations: perform model evaluations (including adversarial testing), assess and mitigate systemic risks, report serious incidents to the AI Office, and ensure adequate cybersecurity protections. The Code of Practice provides detailed compliance templates for transparency, copyright, and safety/security reporting.

**Sme Provisions:** The AI Office coordinates support for SMEs and startups through several mechanisms: (1) AI regulatory sandboxes (free access for SMEs, with national authorities only recovering fair and proportionate exceptional costs); (2) the EU Regulatory Sandboxes for AI project (EUSAiR), a Digital Europe-funded two-year program supporting sandbox implementation; (3) an AI innovation package launched January 2024 to support startups in developing compliant AI; (4) the Advisory Forum includes dedicated representation for startups and SMEs; (5) sandbox participation can serve as evidence of compliance, reducing documentation burden for SMEs.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** Large enterprises: Must invest in compliance teams, technical documentation, and risk management frameworks for GPAI models; companies like Meta, Google, OpenAI face direct AI Office scrutiny. Estimated compliance costs are significant but partially offset by the Code of Practice providing clear compliance pathways. SMEs and startups: Benefit from regulatory sandbox access (free of charge), reduced documentation via sandbox participation, and open-source exemptions for non-systemic-risk models. However, the compliance burden remains disproportionately heavy for smaller firms — the AI Office's limited staff (125-140 people) raises concerns about timely guidance and support. European AI companies: Face a competitive tension — compliance creates market access advantages (trusted AI label) but may slow development compared to less-regulated US and Chinese competitors. Non-EU tech companies: Must appoint EU representatives, comply extraterritorially, and face potential fines up to 3% of global revenue.

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed evidence. Proponents argue the AI Office promotes innovation through regulatory sandboxes, the GenAI4EU initiative, and clear compliance frameworks that build market trust. The open-source exemptions preserve the European open-source AI ecosystem (e.g., Mistral AI benefits from reduced obligations). Critics argue the AI Office is massively understaffed (only ~30 of 85 staff directly on AI Act implementation by some accounts), creating uncertainty and delays. The GPAI Code of Practice being voluntary provides flexibility but creates ambiguity. Industry groups like DigitalEurope have called for a 'stop-the-clock' intervention, warning that enforcement without finalized harmonized standards risks stalling innovation.

**Global Influence:** The AI Office is a key vector for the 'Brussels Effect' in AI governance. As the world's first dedicated AI enforcement body for a comprehensive horizontal AI law, it sets precedents for enforcement approaches worldwide. However, the Brussels Effect is increasingly uncertain: (1) the US under the Trump administration has moved toward deregulation (rescinding Biden's AI EO); (2) the UK pursues sector-specific rather than horizontal regulation; (3) companies may apply EU-compliant standards globally (watermarking, transparency) but resist full extraterritorial compliance. The Code of Practice has attracted signatories globally but notable holdouts (Meta refused to sign). The AI Office's investigation of X/Grok signals willingness to enforce against major US platforms, testing transatlantic regulatory tensions.

**Stakeholder Positions:** Supporters: European Parliament (drove ambitious scope), civil society groups (Access Now, AlgorithmWatch), academic community (scientific panel engagement), some industry players seeking regulatory clarity. Critics: DigitalEurope and industry associations (compliance burden, understaffing concerns, call for implementation delay); MEP Axel Voss (AI Office needs 200+ staff for compliance and safety units alone, far more than current levels); US government and US tech companies (view extraterritorial reach as overregulation); Meta (refused to sign Code of Practice, faces investigation); some Member States concerned about national authority coordination challenges.

**Deregulatory Signals:** Moderate deregulatory pressure exists. The Commission's November 2025 'Digital Omnibus on AI' proposal signals willingness to streamline implementation. The withdrawal of the AI Liability Directive in February 2025 removed a key complementary enforcement mechanism. Industry lobbying for a 'stop-the-clock' on implementation deadlines gained traction in late 2025. The Carnegie Endowment notes the EU is caught between deregulation impulses (to compete with the US and China) and maintaining its regulatory leadership brand. However, the AI Office itself continues expanding operations and launching investigations (Meta, X/Grok), suggesting enforcement commitment remains.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (parent legislation that the AI Office enforces)
- EU AI Act Code of Practice for GPAI (voluntary compliance tool managed by the AI Office)
- GDPR AI Intersections (data protection aspects of AI systems)
- EU Digital Omnibus on AI Nov 2025 (proposed simplification affecting AI Office scope)
- ISO/IEC 42001 (international AI management standard referenced in compliance frameworks)
- OECD AI Principles (international principles the EU AI framework aligns with)

**Key Actors:**

- Lucilla Sioli — Director of the EU AI Office (PhD Economics, European Commission civil servant since 1997, based in DG CONNECT)
- European Commission DG CONNECT — parent directorate-general housing the AI Office
- European Artificial Intelligence Board — Member State representatives coordinating national implementation, secretariat provided by AI Office
- Scientific Panel of Independent Experts — up to 60 experts, 2-year renewable terms, advising on systemic risks, model evaluation, and classification
- Advisory Forum — balanced stakeholder body including industry, startups, SMEs, academia, think tanks, civil society, plus permanent members ENISA, FRA, CENELEC, ETSI
- MEP Axel Voss — prominent critic of AI Office staffing levels
- DigitalEurope — industry association advocating for implementation delays
- Meta — refused to sign GPAI Code of Practice, subject of AI Office investigation
- X/Twitter (Grok) — subject of first AI Office investigation into alleged synthetic media violations
- Mistral AI — key European GPAI provider benefiting from open-source exemptions

**Recent Developments:**

- July 2025: Final General-Purpose AI Code of Practice published after multi-stakeholder drafting process with 1,000+ participants
- August 2025: GPAI model obligations become applicable; providers must comply from 2 August 2025
- August 2025: AI Office establishes Signatory Taskforce to support Code of Practice implementation
- Late 2025: AI Office launches investigation into Meta WhatsApp Business APIs (restricting rival AI providers)
- Late 2025/Early 2026: AI Office launches investigation into X/Grok chatbot for alleged synthetic media and illegal content violations
- November 2025: Commission proposes Digital Omnibus on AI, signaling potential streamlining of AI Act implementation
- February 2025: Commission withdraws AI Liability Directive proposal, leaving gap in fault-based AI liability framework
- January 2026: AI Office pivots from administrative setup to frontline regulatory enforcement body
- February 2026: First anniversary of prohibited AI practices enforcement (Article 5 prohibitions); Commission mandated to review Article 5 under Article 112
- February 2026: Expected publication of Commission guidance on high-risk AI system borderline cases
- Ongoing 2025-2026: Hiring challenges — AI Office struggles to fill technical positions, budget of EUR 46.5M considered insufficient compared to UK AISI (GBP 100M)
- June 2026 (upcoming): Mandatory Commission assessment of whether AI Office has sufficient powers and resources

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** The EU AI Office is the single most important regulatory body for any AI startup operating in or selling to the EU market. Key implications for entrepreneurs: (1) GPAI model providers face direct supervision — startups building foundation models must budget for compliance documentation, technical testing, and potential AI Office inquiries from day one. (2) Open-source strategy matters: releasing models under open-source licenses with published weights can significantly reduce compliance obligations (unless the model crosses systemic risk thresholds), making this a strategic business decision, not just a technical one. (3) Regulatory sandboxes offer free, structured environments for startups to develop and test AI products while generating compliance evidence — a competitive advantage for early participants. (4) The Code of Practice provides a clear compliance roadmap but is voluntary; startups must decide whether to sign and join the Signatory Taskforce (signals commitment, provides peer learning) or find alternative compliance paths. (5) Extraterritorial reach means non-EU startups targeting European customers cannot ignore the AI Office — they need EU representatives and compliance processes. (6) The compliance timeline creates a window: obligations apply from August 2025 but formal enforcement (with fines) begins August 2026, giving startups ~1 year to build compliance infrastructure. (7) Compliance costs are disproportionately burdensome for small teams, making the sandbox access, SME provisions, and open-source exemptions critical strategic tools. (8) First-mover advantage: startups that achieve early compliance can market 'EU AI Act compliant' as a trust signal, particularly valuable in B2B enterprise sales.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary. Also relevant to Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects — compliance as project constraint) and Session 4 (AI business models & strategy — regulatory moats and compliance costs in unit economics).

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High

**Cross Reference:**

- European Commission official AI Office page (digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/ai-office)
- artificialintelligenceact.eu comprehensive analysis and article-by-article coverage
- Wikipedia article on European Artificial Intelligence Office
- CSIS interview with Director Lucilla Sioli
- Latham & Watkins, Jones Day, DLA Piper, Arnold & Porter law firm analyses of GPAI obligations
- Carnegie Endowment for International Peace analysis of EU AI regulatory positioning
- UNESCO breakdown of EU AI governance authorities
- Hugging Face blog on open-source developer obligations under AI Act

**Caveats:**

- The AI Office is in rapid evolution — staff levels, budget, and organizational structure are changing throughout 2025-2026
- Formal enforcement powers for GPAI provisions do not begin until 2 August 2026; current enforcement is advisory/cooperative
- The Digital Omnibus on AI (November 2025) may significantly alter the AI Office's scope and implementation approach
- The mandatory June 2026 Commission review may upgrade AI Office powers and resources
- The withdrawal of the AI Liability Directive creates uncertainty about the broader liability framework complementing AI Office enforcement
- Staffing concerns are serious — the EUR 46.5M budget and ~125-140 staff may be insufficient for the scope of enforcement required
- The Brussels Effect for AI governance is increasingly contested, with US deregulation and industry pushback creating countervailing pressures
- Some reported details (e.g., Grok investigation) come from financial content aggregator sources that may not be fully verified

### 18. EU Digital Omnibus on AI (Digital Omnibus Package)

_Source: `EU_Digital_Omnibus_on_AI_Nov_2025.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** EU Digital Omnibus on AI (Digital Omnibus Package)

**Type:** legislation

**Jurisdiction:** EU

**Status:** proposed

**Effective Date:** Proposed 19 November 2025; if adopted, likely mid-2026 or later (subject to ordinary legislative procedure through European Parliament and Council trilogue)

**Regulatory Philosophy:** comprehensive_horizontal

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The Digital Omnibus on AI is a European Commission legislative proposal published on 19 November 2025 to simplify the implementation of the EU AI Act. It is part of the broader Digital Omnibus Package, which also includes a Digital Omnibus amending the GDPR, ePrivacy Directive, NIS2, and Data Act. The AI Omnibus aims to reduce administrative burdens by at least 25% for all businesses and 35% for SMEs by 2029, by deferring high-risk AI compliance deadlines, extending SME relief to small mid-caps, softening AI literacy obligations, streamlining conformity assessments, and centralizing oversight under the AI Office.

**Key Provisions:**

- Deferral of high-risk AI system obligations: Annex III systems delayed from August 2026 to December 2027 (max 16 months); Annex I systems delayed from August 2027 to August 2028 (max 24 months). Application is linked to Commission confirmation that harmonized standards and support measures are available, with 6 or 12 months lead time respectively.
- AI literacy obligation shifted: No longer a direct obligation on providers and deployers; transformed into a duty for the Commission and Member States to encourage providers/deployers to take AI literacy measures.
- Bias detection data processing: New Art. 4a allows providers and deployers of both high-risk and non-high-risk AI systems to process sensitive personal data for bias detection and correction, with the threshold lowered from 'strictly necessary' to 'necessary'.
- SME/SMC regime extension: Simplified quality management system compliance (Art. 17) extended from microenterprises only to all SMEs and small mid-cap companies (SMCs, defined as <750 employees and <EUR 150M revenue). SMCs also receive privileged treatment in fine calculations.
- Registration requirement removal: Providers of AI systems exempt from high-risk classification under Art. 6(3) no longer required to register in the EU database; only need to document a self-assessment.
- Transparency obligation delay: Art. 50(2) obligations for AI-generated content marking/watermarking for systems placed on market before August 2026 delayed by 6 months to February 2027.
- Centralized AI Office oversight: AI Office becomes exclusively competent for supervision and enforcement of AI systems based on GPAI models (where model and system share the same provider) and AI systems integrated into VLOPs/VLOSEs under the DSA.
- Pre-market conformity assessments: Commission or entrusted notified bodies can organize pre-market conformity assessments for high-risk systems under AI Office supervision.
- Sectoral conformity assessment priority: For dual-classified products (both Annex I.A and Annex III), the sectoral product law conformity assessment takes priority, with AI Act requirements integrated.
- 'Once-only' principle: Single application and assessment procedure for designation under both the AI Act and relevant sectoral legislation.
- Expanded sandbox use: Possibility of EU-level AI regulatory sandbox operated by the AI Office; extended real-world testing for high-risk AI systems under Annex I (previously limited to Annex III).
- Increased post-market monitoring flexibility: Removal of requirement for harmonized Commission template for post-market monitoring plans.

**Risk Classification:** The Omnibus does not change the fundamental risk-based classification structure of the AI Act (unacceptable/high/limited/minimal risk). It modifies how and when high-risk obligations are applied: deferring the application timeline, streamlining conformity assessments for dual-classified systems, and removing registration requirements for AI systems exempted from high-risk under Art. 6(3).

**Covered Entities:** Providers, deployers, importers, and distributors of AI systems operating in the EU market. Extended scope to small mid-cap companies (SMCs). The AI Office gains exclusive enforcement authority over GPAI-based systems and VLOP/VLOSE-integrated systems.

**Sector Scope:** Cross-sector horizontal regulation. Specific provisions address sectoral product legislation (medical devices, machinery via Annex I.A), critical infrastructure (Annex III), and platform regulation (VLOPs/VLOSEs under DSA). No new sector exemptions introduced.

**Open Source Treatment:** The Digital Omnibus on AI does not introduce specific new provisions regarding open-source AI models. The existing AI Act exemptions for open-source GPAI models remain unchanged: open-source GPAI providers are exempt from some documentation and representation obligations, but providers of open-source GPAI models with systemic risks remain subject to all obligations. The broader simplified compliance regime for SMEs and SMCs would benefit many open-source developers who fall within those categories.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** Maintains the AI Act's existing extraterritorial reach: the regulation applies to providers placing AI systems on the EU market or putting them into service in the EU, regardless of where they are established. Third-country providers of non-open-source GPAI models must still appoint an authorized representative in the EU. The Omnibus does not alter these provisions.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** The Omnibus does not change the AI Act's existing penalty tiers: Tier 1 (prohibited AI practices): up to EUR 35M or 7% of global annual turnover, whichever is higher. Tier 2 (high-risk system violations): up to EUR 15M or 3% of global annual turnover, whichever is higher. Tier 3 (misleading information): up to EUR 7.5M or 1% of global annual turnover, whichever is higher. The Omnibus extends the SME fine calculation method to SMCs: for SMEs and SMCs, fines use the lower (not higher) of the fixed amount or percentage threshold, significantly reducing effective penalties for smaller companies.

**Enforcement Body:** The AI Office within the European Commission gains exclusive supervisory and enforcement competence for GPAI-based AI systems and VLOP/VLOSE-integrated systems. National market surveillance authorities retain competence for other AI systems. Conformity assessment bodies handle pre-market assessments.

**Compliance Timeline:** Original AI Act deadlines are modified as follows: Prohibited AI practices: 2 February 2025 (unchanged). AI literacy: obligation shifted to Commission/Member States (no firm deadline for providers). High-risk AI systems (Annex III): deferred from 2 August 2026 to 2 December 2027 (backstop), or 6 months after Commission confirms support measures are available, whichever is earlier. High-risk AI systems (Annex I): deferred from 2 August 2027 to 2 August 2028 (backstop), or 12 months after Commission confirms support measures. Art. 50(2) transparency/marking for pre-August 2026 systems: deferred to 2 February 2027. Public authority compliance: 2 August 2030 (unchanged). Notified body AI Act designation: by 2 February 2028.

**Reporting Requirements:** The Omnibus retains existing AI Act reporting requirements for serious incidents by providers of high-risk AI systems. It removes the harmonized Commission template for post-market monitoring plans, allowing providers more flexibility. Self-assessment documentation replaces EU database registration for Art. 6(3) exempt systems. The broader Digital Omnibus Package introduces a single-entry EU portal for data breach reporting across GDPR, NIS2, DORA, and CER Directive.

**Sme Provisions:** Central pillar of the proposal. Key provisions: (1) Simplified quality management system compliance (Art. 17) extended from microenterprises to all SMEs and startups. (2) SME regulatory benefits (simplified technical documentation, sandbox access, support measures) extended to small mid-cap companies (SMCs: <750 employees, <EUR 150M revenue). (3) SMCs benefit from privileged fine calculation: lower of fixed amount or percentage threshold applies. (4) Quality management systems required proportionate to organization size. (5) Administrative burden reduction target of 35% for SMEs by 2029 (vs. 25% for all businesses). (6) Definitions of SMEs and SMCs aligned with previous Commission recommendations.

**Liability Model:** none_specified

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** The Omnibus significantly benefits businesses of all sizes, but most directly aids SMEs and small mid-caps. (1) For large enterprises: The 16-24 month deferral of high-risk AI deadlines provides crucial additional compliance time, especially given that harmonized standards from CEN-CENELEC are not expected before Q4 2026. However, the substantive requirements remain unchanged, and businesses report needing at least 12 months to comply with a single standard. (2) For SMEs and startups: The extension of simplified compliance to all SMEs (not just micro-enterprises), proportionate quality management requirements, and reduced fine exposure provide meaningful relief. The estimated EUR 6B+ in administrative cost savings across all businesses and public administrations by 2029 disproportionately benefits smaller firms. (3) For small mid-caps (SMCs): A new category of beneficiary. Entities with <750 employees and <EUR 150M revenue now qualify for regulatory flexibilities previously reserved for SMEs, including simplified documentation and privileged fine treatment. (4) For AI system integrators: Centralized AI Office oversight and the 'once-only' conformity assessment principle reduce duplicative compliance processes. (5) Criticism: Morrison Foerster analysis notes that the Omnibus 'offers only limited substantive reductions' and that 'the strict sunset date for the suspension is problematic, particularly if the development of harmonized standards faces further delays.'

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed assessment. Promotes innovation through: deferred compliance deadlines allowing more development time; expanded regulatory sandbox access including an EU-level sandbox; extended real-world testing to Annex I high-risk systems; relaxed AI literacy obligations reducing administrative overhead; legitimate interest basis for personal data in AI training (via the broader Data Omnibus). However, critics argue: the Omnibus fails to expand the research exemption to real-world testing; it does not clarify key definitions like 'provider'; the fundamental rights impact assessment obligation remains, creating overlap with GDPR DPIAs; and Member States can still require consent for AI data processing, undermining harmonization. The Draghi Report context is key: the EU identified an innovation gap vis-a-vis the US and China in advanced technologies, and the Omnibus is a direct response.

**Global Influence:** The Omnibus represents a notable shift in EU regulatory philosophy. While the EU pioneered comprehensive AI regulation with the AI Act (entering force August 2024), the Omnibus signals a recalibration toward competitiveness. This follows a broader international trend: the US Trump Administration adopted a deregulatory approach under its 'AI Arms Race' policy, and the UK passed its Data (Use and Access) Act 2024 with pragmatic simplifications. The 'Brussels Effect' dynamic is evolving: rather than other jurisdictions racing to match EU strictness, the EU is now partially retreating from its own regulatory ambitions. However, the Commission insists this is 'simplification, not softening of standards.' The GDPR-related changes in the broader Data Omnibus may influence other jurisdictions that modeled their data protection laws on the GDPR. The UK ICO's pragmatic approach may welcome similar simplifications.

**Stakeholder Positions:** Sharply divided. SUPPORTERS: DigitalEurope welcomed the delays and business wallet as crucial competitiveness steps. MEP Eva Maydell (EPP) framed it as industrial survival. The Commission projects EUR 6B+ savings. Many businesses welcome the AI Act delays given the absence of harmonized standards. CRITICS: NOYB (Max Schrems) called it 'the biggest attack on Europeans digital rights in years' and said it 'would mainly benefit Big Tech.' 127 civil society organizations signed a critical joint statement. BEUC warned that processing personal data for AI without consent gives large firms an advantage over European startups. MEP Alex Agius Saliba (S&D) said it 'could recklessly undermine the EU digital legal framework.' MEP Kim van Sparrentak (Greens-EFA) warned of 'rolling out the red carpet to a business model based on stolen data.' The EDPB and EDPS challenged the claim that changes are only minor modifications, noting the personal data definition change goes further than CJEU case law. CDT Europe raised concerns about weakened oversight.

**Deregulatory Signals:** Strong deregulatory signal, though the Commission frames it as 'simplification.' Specific mechanisms: (1) Deferral of high-risk compliance deadlines by 16-24 months. (2) Removal of AI literacy obligations from providers/deployers. (3) Removal of registration requirements for Art. 6(3) exempt systems. (4) Lowered threshold for sensitive data processing from 'strictly necessary' to 'necessary.' (5) Extended relief categories (SMCs). (6) Removal of harmonized post-market monitoring template. (7) The broader Data Omnibus adds GDPR simplifications (cookie consent reform, narrowed personal data definition, legitimate interest for AI training). The Digital Fitness Check consultation (open until 11 March 2026) signals further potential deregulation. The Omnibus is explicitly a response to the Draghi Report's competitiveness concerns and the perceived innovation gap with the US and China.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689)
- EU GDPR (Regulation 2016/679)
- ePrivacy Directive (2002/58/EC)
- NIS2 Directive (2022/2555)
- Data Act (Regulation 2023/2854)
- Digital Services Act (DSA)
- Digital Markets Act (DMA)
- Cyber Resilience Act
- Data Governance Act (to be repealed/consolidated)
- Regulation 2018/1139 (EASA aviation regulation, also amended by the AI Omnibus)

**Key Actors:** European Commission (proposer): Executive VP Henna Virkkunen (digital portfolio), Justice Commissioner Michael McGrath (GDPR aspects). Legislative co-legislators: European Parliament, Council of the EU. Key MEPs: Eva Maydell (EPP, supportive), Alex Agius Saliba (S&D, critical), Kim van Sparrentak (Greens-EFA, critical). Enforcement: AI Office (within Commission), national market surveillance authorities. Standardization: CEN-CENELEC Joint Technical Committee 21 (drafting harmonized AI standards). Advisory: EDPB, EDPS (both cautious about scope of changes). Civil society: NOYB/Max Schrems (strongly opposed), BEUC (consumer protection concerns), CDT Europe (AI governance concerns), 127 civil society organizations (joint critical statement). Industry: DigitalEurope (supportive). Contextual: Mario Draghi (author of the competitiveness report that motivated the Omnibus).

**Recent Developments:**

- 19 November 2025: European Commission published the Digital Omnibus Package, comprising the AI Omnibus (COM(2025) 836) and the Digital Omnibus (COM(2025) 837), along with the Data Union Strategy and European Business Wallets.
- November-December 2025: Eight-week feedback period opened; being extended daily until available in all EU languages (initially until 29 January 2026).
- December 2025: EDPB and EDPS challenged the scope of changes, particularly the personal data definition revision.
- 127 civil society organizations published joint criticism of the proposals.
- 11 March 2026 deadline: Digital Fitness Check consultation closes; this comprehensive 'stress test' of the Digital Rulebook may lead to further simplification proposals.
- 2 February 2026: Commission missed its own deadline for publishing Art. 6 guidance on high-risk AI systems classification.
- CEN-CENELEC indicated harmonized AI standards may not be available before Q4 2026.
- European Parliament and Council began ordinary legislative procedure review; adoption likely mid-2026 or later, with possibility of accelerated procedure.
- Commission also issued parallel guidance on serious incident reporting for high-risk AI and practical application of high-risk requirements.

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** Highly relevant for AI entrepreneurs in France and the EU. Key implications: (1) Compliance timing: The 16-24 month deferral of high-risk AI deadlines gives startups more runway to develop products before facing compliance obligations, but the requirements themselves are not reduced. (2) Cost reduction: The extension of simplified compliance to all SMEs and SMCs means most startups benefit from lighter quality management, simplified documentation, and proportionate requirements -- not just micro-enterprises. Fine exposure is also reduced (lower-of threshold). (3) Market access: The 'once-only' conformity assessment and centralized AI Office oversight simplify market entry for GPAI-based products. (4) Data advantage: The broader Data Omnibus's legitimate interest basis for AI training data gives EU startups more flexibility to train models on personal data, though critics warn this mainly benefits Big Tech. (5) Competitive moat: The regulatory sandbox expansion, including an EU-level sandbox, creates opportunities for startups to test high-risk AI in controlled environments. (6) Strategic risk: The Omnibus is still a proposal under legislative review; startups should not assume final rules will match the proposal. The ongoing Digital Fitness Check could bring further changes. (7) French/European context: The Omnibus responds to the Draghi Report on EU competitiveness and directly addresses concerns that EU regulation is driving innovation to the US and China. Mistral AI and similar EU AI companies benefit from the deferral and simplified compliance. (8) Discussion question: 'If you are building a high-risk AI system, should you delay compliance efforts or start now despite the proposed deferral?'

**Session Mapping:** 5

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High

**Cross Reference:** Information confirmed across multiple authoritative sources: Morrison Foerster client alert (1 Dec 2025), Skadden first impressions (Nov 2025), Crowell & Moring client alert (1 Dec 2025), IAPP analysis (9 Dec 2025), Sidley Austin analysis (Dec 2025), Cooley analysis (Nov 2025), Latham & Watkins analysis (Dec 2025), PWC analysis, official EC press release (IP/25/2718), and Kennedys Law analysis (Jan 2026). All sources are consistent on the core provisions. Official legislative text: COM(2025) 836 final (AI Omnibus) and COM(2025) 837 final (Data Omnibus).

**Caveats:** This is a Commission proposal under the ordinary legislative procedure; the final text will be negotiated by the European Parliament and Council and is likely to change substantially. The AI Act's underlying requirements are not simplified, only deferred. The harmonized standards from CEN-CENELEC are still under development. The Digital Fitness Check (open until 11 March 2026) could lead to further proposals. Some of the most impactful changes (GDPR personal data definition, legitimate interest for AI training) are in the separate Data Omnibus, not the AI Omnibus itself. Civil society opposition is strong and may influence Parliament's position. The Commission missed its own 2 February deadline for Art. 6 guidance, raising questions about implementation capacity.
