### 19. Foundation Model Governance (Cross-Jurisdictional Issue Area)

_Source: `Foundation_Model_Governance.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** Foundation Model Governance (Cross-Jurisdictional Issue Area)

**Type:** issue_area

**Jurisdiction:** Global (EU, US, UK, China, G7/OECD)

**Status:** Partially in_force (EU GPAI rules Aug 2025), partially enacted (California SB 53 Jan 2026), partially voluntary (Seoul Commitments, G7 Hiroshima Code)

**Effective Date:** EU GPAI obligations: 2 August 2025; EU enforcement powers: 2 August 2026; California SB 53: 1 January 2026; G7 Hiroshima Code: October 2023 (voluntary); Seoul Frontier AI Safety Commitments: May 2024 (voluntary); UK AISI evaluations: ongoing since November 2023

**Regulatory Philosophy:** Varies by jurisdiction: comprehensive_horizontal (EU AI Act GPAI tier), patchwork_federalism with innovation_first_light_touch (US federal under Trump, state-level SB 53 in California), sector_specific voluntary (UK pro-innovation approach with AISI evaluations), state_control (China mandatory algorithm registration and content labeling), voluntary_principles (G7 Hiroshima Process, Seoul Commitments, Frontier Model Forum)

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** Foundation model governance addresses the specific rules, safety testing requirements, incident reporting obligations, and transparency mandates that apply to the most capable general-purpose AI models (also called frontier models). The EU AI Act creates a two-tier system for GPAI models: all providers above 10^23 FLOPs must meet transparency and copyright obligations, while models above 10^25 FLOPs (systemic risk) face additional safety evaluation, adversarial testing, cybersecurity, and incident reporting requirements. California SB 53 targets frontier models above 10^26 FLOPs with safety framework publication and incident reporting. Internationally, the G7 Hiroshima Process and Seoul Summit Frontier AI Safety Commitments establish voluntary codes of conduct for organizations developing the most advanced AI systems.

**Key Provisions:**

- EU AI Act Article 53 — All GPAI model providers must: (a) maintain technical documentation, (b) provide information to downstream AI system providers, (c) comply with EU copyright law including text and data mining opt-out obligations, (d) publish a sufficiently detailed summary of training data content
- EU AI Act Article 51 — GPAI models trained with cumulative compute >10^25 FLOPs are presumed to pose systemic risk; providers must notify the Commission within 2 weeks of meeting/foreseeing this threshold; providers may contest classification with robust counter-evidence
- EU AI Act Article 55 — Systemic risk GPAI providers must: (a) perform state-of-the-art model evaluations via standardized protocols (benchmarks, red teaming, human uplift studies), (b) assess and mitigate systemic risks at Union level, (c) track, document, and report serious incidents to the AI Office without undue delay, (d) ensure adequate cybersecurity for model and physical infrastructure
- GPAI Code of Practice (July 2025) — Three chapters: (1) Transparency, (2) Copyright, (3) Safety & Security; signatories benefit from 'presumption of conformity' safe harbor; Safety chapter requires cutting-edge Safety and Security Framework before model release, covering evaluation triggers, risk categories, mitigation strategies, forecasting methods, and organizational responsibilities
- California SB 53 (TFAIA) — Frontier developers (models >10^26 FLOPs) must: (a) publish accessible safety framework incorporating national/international standards and catastrophic risk assessment, (b) report critical safety incidents to Office of Emergency Services within 15 days (24 hours if imminent risk of death/serious injury), (c) publish transparency reports at model launch, (d) provide whistleblower protections with anonymous internal reporting platform
- G7 Hiroshima Process (October 2023) — 11 guiding principles and voluntary Code of Conduct for organizations developing advanced AI, including: pre-deployment safety testing, red teaming, information sharing on capabilities and risks, cybersecurity investment, content provenance mechanisms, public reporting of capabilities and limitations
- Seoul Frontier AI Safety Commitments (May 2024) — 20 organizations (including Anthropic, Microsoft, NVIDIA, OpenAI) committed to: publishing safety frameworks, internal/external red-teaming for severe/novel threats, cybersecurity and insider threat safeguards, third-party vulnerability discovery, prioritizing societal risk research
- UK AI Security Institute (AISI) — Conducts voluntary pre-deployment evaluations of frontier AI systems across national security and public safety domains; has evaluated 30+ frontier systems since November 2023; no binding legal mandate but operates through voluntary agreements with developers
- China — Mandatory algorithm registration with CAC for generative AI services with public opinion/social mobilization capabilities; mandatory filing of LLMs; content labeling requirements (effective September 2025) for AI-generated/synthetic content; national security assessments for foundation models before public deployment

**Risk Classification:** EU AI Act creates a two-tier GPAI classification: (1) Standard GPAI models (>10^23 FLOPs) with transparency and copyright obligations, and (2) GPAI models with systemic risk (>10^25 FLOPs) with additional safety evaluation, adversarial testing, incident reporting, and cybersecurity requirements. California SB 53 uses a higher threshold (>10^26 FLOPs) to define 'frontier models.' The Hiroshima Process and Seoul Commitments use qualitative criteria ('most advanced AI systems,' 'frontier models') without a specific compute threshold. China does not use compute thresholds but requires registration for all generative AI services with public-facing capabilities.

**Covered Entities:** EU: providers of GPAI models placed on the EU market (regardless of where headquartered); California SB 53: frontier developers and 'large frontier developers' (annual revenue >$500M, approximately 5-8 companies currently); G7/Seoul: voluntary signatories (major AI labs); UK AISI: voluntary participants; China: all domestic providers of generative AI and algorithm-based services

**Sector Scope:** Cross-sector — foundation model governance applies horizontally across all sectors where GPAI models are deployed. EU AI Act GPAI rules apply regardless of downstream use case. SB 53 focuses specifically on model developers, not deployers. China's rules apply to all internet-facing generative AI services. Military/national security uses are generally excluded from EU AI Act but not from national security-related evaluations by UK AISI.

**Open Source Treatment:** EU AI Act Article 53(2) provides exemptions for open-source GPAI models meeting three conditions: (1) released under a free and open-source license allowing access, usage, modification, and distribution, (2) model parameters/weights/architecture publicly available, (3) not monetized. Exempt providers skip technical documentation and downstream provider information obligations but MUST still comply with copyright/training data summary requirements. CRITICAL EXCEPTION: open-source models classified as systemic risk (>10^25 FLOPs) receive NO exemptions — all Article 55 obligations apply regardless of open-source status. The GPAI Code of Practice offers simplified compliance pathways for SMEs. California SB 53 makes no open-source distinction — any model above 10^26 FLOPs is covered regardless of license.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** EU AI Act applies to all GPAI model providers placing models on the EU market, regardless of headquarters location — non-EU providers must appoint an EU-based authorized representative (except open-source exempt providers). This means US companies like OpenAI, Google, Meta, and Anthropic must comply with EU GPAI rules for models available in Europe. California SB 53 applies to any developer training frontier models, with enforcement by the California Attorney General. The G7 Hiroshima Process is voluntary but aims for global reach through OECD reporting framework (49 countries). China's regulations apply to services available to Chinese users.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** EU AI Act: fines up to 3% of global annual turnover or EUR 15 million (whichever is higher) for GPAI-specific violations; up to 7% or EUR 35 million for prohibited practices. California SB 53: civil penalties up to $1,000,000 per violation, enforced by California Attorney General; attorney's fees awarded to successful whistleblower plaintiffs. China: penalties include service suspension, fines, and potential criminal liability for serious violations. G7/Seoul/UK AISI: no binding penalties (voluntary frameworks).

**Enforcement Body:** EU: AI Office (within European Commission), operational since August 2025, responsible for GPAI model enforcement. California: California Attorney General and Office of Emergency Services (for incident reports). China: Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC), State Administration for Market Regulation (SAMR). UK: AI Security Institute (AISI) conducts evaluations; no dedicated AI regulatory body — sector regulators maintain oversight. G7/OECD: no enforcement body; OECD facilitates voluntary reporting.

**Compliance Timeline:** EU GPAI obligations: effective 2 August 2025 for new models; enforcement powers (fines) from 2 August 2026; legacy models (placed on market before Aug 2025) must comply by 2 August 2027; member states must establish AI regulatory sandboxes by 2 August 2026. California SB 53: effective 1 January 2026; large frontier developers must publish safety frameworks by January 2026. G7 Hiroshima reporting: first round of reports due 15 April 2025. Seoul Commitments: safety frameworks to be published within a year of signing (by May 2025). China content labeling: effective 1 September 2025.

**Reporting Requirements:** EU Article 55: providers of systemic risk GPAI must track, document, and report serious incidents to the AI Office 'without undue delay,' plus report possible corrective measures. EU Article 53: all GPAI providers must publish training data summaries and maintain technical documentation accessible to regulators. California SB 53: report critical safety incidents to OES within 15 days (24 hours for imminent death/injury risk); publish transparency reports at model launch; large frontier developers must publish safety frameworks. G7 Hiroshima: voluntary reporting via OECD framework. China: mandatory algorithm filing, LLM filing, and security assessments before public deployment.

**Sme Provisions:** EU AI Act explicitly requires the GPAI Code of Practice to allow 'simplified ways of compliance for SMEs, including start-ups, in order not to represent an excessive cost.' Specific provisions: (1) reduced training data disclosure — SMEs report top 5% of internet domains crawled (vs. 10% for large companies), (2) AI regulatory sandboxes must be established in each member state by August 2026 with priority access for SMEs/startups, (3) open-source exemption from documentation obligations reduces burden for small open-source model providers. California SB 53 implicitly protects smaller companies through its high 10^26 FLOPs threshold, which currently covers only 5-8 companies; additionally, only 'large frontier developers' (>$500M revenue) face the full set of obligations.

**Liability Model:** EU: The AI Liability Directive proposal was WITHDRAWN by the European Commission in February 2025. Instead, the revised Product Liability Directive (member state transposition deadline: 9 December 2026) extends strict product liability to software including AI systems — developers/providers are treated as manufacturers, liable without requiring proof of negligence. For GPAI models specifically, the AI Act establishes regulatory compliance obligations but does not create a direct private right of action. California SB 53: no private right of action for general violations, but whistleblower plaintiffs can recover attorney's fees; enforcement is through AG civil penalties. Overall: fault_based in most jurisdictions, with product_liability (strict) emerging in EU for defective AI products.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** Foundation model governance creates highly asymmetric impacts across company sizes: (1) LARGE LABS (OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, Meta, Mistral): face the heaviest obligations — EU systemic risk compliance (Article 55), California SB 53 full requirements, Seoul Commitments — requiring dedicated safety teams, red-teaming infrastructure, incident response systems, and legal/compliance staff across multiple jurisdictions; estimated compliance costs in millions of dollars annually. (2) MID-TIER MODEL PROVIDERS: face EU Article 53 transparency/documentation obligations if models exceed 10^23 FLOPs, but likely fall below systemic risk threshold — compliance is manageable but requires documentation infrastructure and copyright compliance processes. (3) STARTUPS AND SMEs: largely shielded by high compute thresholds and open-source exemptions; can leverage open-source models (Llama, Mistral 7B) to avoid GPAI provider obligations entirely; regulatory sandboxes provide testing environments; however, startups building on top of GPAI models may face downstream deployer obligations. (4) EUROPEAN vs. US COMPANIES: European AI companies (notably Mistral) face a dual challenge — compliance costs from EU GPAI rules while competing against US companies operating under lighter federal regulation; 56 EU-based AI companies signed a July 2025 letter warning compliance costs could stifle innovation. (5) OPEN-SOURCE ECOSYSTEM: EU open-source exemptions preserve the ability to release models freely, but systemic risk models (>10^25 FLOPs) like Llama receive no exemption, potentially chilling open release of the most capable models.

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed and contested. CONSTRAINING FACTORS: EU GPAI obligations add compliance overhead that may disadvantage European model developers; the 'stop the clock' campaign by 56 EU companies (including Mistral, Aleph Alpha) argued for a 2-year delay, suggesting innovation friction. Stanford's 2025 Foundation Model Transparency Index found overall transparency declining (average 40.69/100), with copyright litigation actively disincentivizing training data disclosure. PROMOTING FACTORS: The GPAI Code of Practice's 'presumption of conformity' provides legal certainty that may encourage investment; regulatory sandboxes create safe experimentation spaces; open-source exemptions preserve innovation pathways for smaller players; the EU's approach may create a 'race to the top' on safety, building consumer trust. The US under Trump is explicitly pursuing deregulation to maintain AI dominance, creating a regulatory arbitrage opportunity. California SB 53's focus on the largest models (10^26 FLOPs) limits innovation impact to a handful of frontier labs.

**Global Influence:** Strong 'Brussels effect' dynamics: EU GPAI rules are becoming the de facto global standard since major AI companies must comply to access the EU market. The GPAI Code of Practice is described as 'the world's first guidance for GPAI model compliance.' California SB 53 extends EU-style requirements to the US context for the first time. The G7 Hiroshima Process (49 countries) and Seoul Commitments (20 organizations) create multilateral alignment around similar principles. UK AISI has inspired a global network of AI Safety Institutes (US, Japan, Singapore, Canada, France, Korea). China's approach — mandatory registration with pre-deployment security reviews — represents an alternative governance model emphasizing state control over content and capabilities. The compute-threshold approach (10^23, 10^25, 10^26 FLOPs) pioneered by the EU and adopted by California is becoming an emerging global standard for scoping foundation model regulation.

**Stakeholder Positions:** INDUSTRY (split): Major AI labs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Microsoft) signed Seoul Commitments and largely accept GPAI framework — Mistral and OpenAI publicly backed EU GPAI Code of Practice in July 2025. However, 56 EU companies lobbied to delay implementation. Mistral AI and Aleph Alpha earlier lobbied to shift EU rules to focus on applications rather than models. CIVIL SOCIETY: Corporate Europe Observatory documented how European startups 'teamed up with Big Tech to gut the AI Act'; AI safety organizations (METR, CSET Georgetown) generally support robust evaluation requirements. GOVERNMENTS: France backed lighter GPAI rules (supporting Mistral), while other EU members pushed for stronger obligations; US under Trump prioritizes deregulation and innovation; UK maintains voluntary pro-innovation approach. ACADEMIA: Stanford HAI transparency research shows companies are becoming less transparent despite growing regulation; CSET Georgetown called the EU Code of Practice a potential 'new global standard' for AI safety.

**Deregulatory Signals:** US (federal): Trump revoked Biden's EO 14110 (January 2025), explicitly pursuing 'minimally burdensome' AI framework; December 2025 EO seeks federal preemption of state AI laws. EU: Commission rejected the 'stop the clock' campaign but withdrew the AI Liability Directive (February 2025), signaling pragmatism on enforcement; the GPAI Code of Practice is voluntary (though creates presumption of conformity). UK: explicitly chose not to create new AI legislation, relying on sector regulators and voluntary AISI evaluations. Japan and India maintain innovation-first approaches with minimal binding requirements for foundation models.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (parent legislation for GPAI rules)
- EU AI Act Code of Practice for GPAI (implementation mechanism)
- GDPR/AI Intersections (training data and privacy)
- AI Copyright/IP (training data copyright compliance)
- Trump AI Executive Orders 2025 (US deregulatory context)
- US Executive Order 14110 on AI (Biden, rescinded)
- California SB 53 / US State AI Laws Patchwork
- China AI Regulations (alternative governance model)
- OECD AI Principles (international voluntary framework)
- ISO/IEC 42001 (AI management system standard)
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework (US voluntary standard)
- International AI Safety Report 2026 (Bengio, evidence base)
- AI Safety Institutes Network (evaluation infrastructure)
- Responsible AI Frameworks (industry self-governance)
- EU Digital Omnibus on AI (November 2025 adjustments)

**Key Actors:**

- European AI Office (Lucilla Sioli, Director) — EU enforcement body for GPAI rules, operational since August 2025
- European Commission DG CONNECT — published GPAI Guidelines and endorsed Code of Practice
- OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Anthropic, Meta, Microsoft, Mistral AI — major GPAI model providers subject to systemic risk obligations
- Frontier Model Forum — industry consortium (founded by Anthropic, Google, Microsoft, OpenAI) coordinating voluntary safety commitments
- UK AI Security Institute (AISI) — conducts frontier model evaluations, evaluated 30+ systems
- OECD — manages G7 Hiroshima Process reporting framework
- Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) — Chinese AI regulatory authority
- California Attorney General — enforcement authority for SB 53
- Stanford HAI / CRFM — publishes Foundation Model Transparency Index
- METR (Model Evaluation & Threat Research) — tracks frontier AI safety policies
- Cédric O / Arthur Mensch (Mistral AI) — led EU lobbying to lighten GPAI obligations
- Yoshua Bengio — led International AI Safety Report providing evidence base for governance

**Recent Developments:**

- July 10, 2025: European AI Office published final GPAI Code of Practice with three chapters (Transparency, Copyright, Safety & Security)
- August 1, 2025: European Commission and AI Board endorsed the Code via adequacy decisions
- August 2, 2025: EU AI Act GPAI model obligations became applicable for new models; AI Office became operational for enforcement
- July 2025: 56 EU-based AI companies (including Mistral, Aleph Alpha) signed open letter urging Commission to 'stop the clock' for 2 years on GPAI obligations — rejected by Commission
- July 2025: European Commission published GPAI Guidelines clarifying scope of obligations, including 10^23 FLOPs threshold interpretation and open-source exemption conditions
- September 29, 2025: California Governor Newsom signed SB 53 (TFAIA) into law — first US state frontier AI law with safety framework and incident reporting requirements
- January 1, 2026: California SB 53 took effect, requiring frontier developers to publish safety frameworks and establish incident reporting systems
- December 2025: Stanford published 2025 Foundation Model Transparency Index showing average transparency score of 40.69/100 — decline from previous year
- February 2025: European Commission withdrew AI Liability Directive proposal in 2025 Work Programme, leaving Product Liability Directive as primary liability mechanism
- December 11, 2025: Trump issued EO on 'Ensuring a National Policy Framework for AI' seeking federal preemption of state AI laws — potentially conflicting with California SB 53
- February 2025: OECD launched Hiroshima Process reporting framework, first reports due April 15, 2025
- May 2024: Seoul AI Safety Summit produced Frontier AI Safety Commitments signed by 20 organizations
- March/April 2025: China released new AI content labeling measures and three national security standards for generative AI (effective late 2025)
- UK AISI rebranded to AI Security Institute and published Frontier AI Trends Report based on evaluations of 30+ frontier systems

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** Foundation model governance is critical for AI entrepreneurs at every stage: (1) BUILD vs. BUY DECISION: Startups using open-source models below 10^25 FLOPs as deployers avoid GPAI provider obligations, while those training custom large models face significant compliance costs — this fundamentally shapes the build-vs-buy calculus. (2) MARKET ACCESS: EU GPAI compliance is effectively mandatory for accessing the 450M-consumer EU market; entrepreneurs must budget for documentation, training data summaries, and potential copyright compliance from day one. (3) COMPLIANCE COST ASYMMETRY: The regulatory landscape strongly favors application-layer startups over model-training startups — estimated compliance costs for systemic risk models run into millions annually (safety teams, red-teaming, legal), while downstream deployers face lighter obligations. (4) OPEN-SOURCE STRATEGY: The EU open-source exemption creates a viable path for startups to release models without full GPAI compliance, but this exemption vanishes at systemic risk scale — entrepreneurs must plan their scaling trajectory with this cliff in mind. (5) GEOGRAPHIC ARBITRAGE: US federal deregulation under Trump vs. EU comprehensive regulation creates strategic choices about where to headquarter and which markets to prioritize; California SB 53 complicates the US picture. (6) SANDBOX OPPORTUNITIES: EU regulatory sandboxes (mandatory in each member state by August 2026) offer startups controlled environments to test AI products with reduced compliance burden. (7) COMPETITIVE MOATS: Early compliance with GPAI rules can become a competitive advantage — 'presumption of conformity' from Code of Practice adherence provides legal certainty that non-compliant competitors lack. (8) TRANSPARENCY AS DIFFERENTIATOR: With Stanford showing average transparency at 40.69/100, startups that voluntarily exceed transparency requirements may build trust advantages.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary; Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) — secondary (build-vs-buy implications, GPAI compliance in project planning); Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) — secondary (regulatory compliance costs, geographic strategy, competitive moats from safety)

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official EU legislation text (Articles 51, 53, 55), European Commission publications, signed California legislation (SB 53), government-published Bletchley Declaration and Seoul Commitments, law firm analyses (Latham & Watkins, WilmerHale, White & Case, Clifford Chance), Stanford HAI research, CSET Georgetown analysis, and OECD official communications

**Cross Reference:**

- EU AI Act official text and artificialintelligenceact.eu analysis corroborate GPAI obligations and thresholds
- Multiple law firm analyses (Latham & Watkins, WilmerHale, Clifford Chance, Baker Botts, White & Case) confirm SB 53 requirements and penalties
- European Commission digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu confirms GPAI Code of Practice structure and timeline
- RAND Corporation analysis confirms GPAI classification framework
- CSET Georgetown and Stibbe analysis confirm Code of Practice details
- Stanford CRFM Foundation Model Transparency Index (December 2025) confirms transparency decline
- Future of Privacy Forum and Brookings Institution confirm SB 53 scope and threshold details
- GOV.UK confirms UK AISI mandate and Bletchley Declaration details

**Caveats:**

- EU GPAI enforcement is in its earliest phase — the AI Office is taking a collaborative approach in year one (2025-2026), and actual enforcement patterns remain to be established
- The 10^25 FLOPs threshold for systemic risk is acknowledged as a rough proxy; the Commission may adjust or supplement it as the field evolves
- Trump's December 2025 EO seeking federal preemption of state AI laws could potentially conflict with California SB 53 — legal challenges are likely but unresolved as of February 2026
- The AI Liability Directive withdrawal leaves a gap in EU AI liability framework — the Product Liability Directive covers products but not services, creating potential gaps for AI-as-a-service
- China's regulatory landscape is evolving rapidly with multiple overlapping measures; exact enforcement patterns are less transparent than EU/US
- The voluntary nature of G7/Seoul/UK commitments means compliance varies significantly across signatories — METR tracks actual implementation
- Compute thresholds will become outdated as training efficiency improves — a model with equivalent capabilities may be trained below thresholds in future
- The GPAI Code of Practice provides 'presumption of conformity' but this has not yet been tested in enforcement proceedings

### 20. General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) — AI Intersections (Regulation (EU) 2016/679)

_Source: `GDPR_AI_Intersections.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) — AI Intersections (Regulation (EU) 2016/679)

**Type:** legislation

**Jurisdiction:** EU (European Union, 27 Member States + EEA)

**Status:** in_force

**Effective Date:** Adopted 14 April 2016; entered into force 24 May 2016; applicable from 25 May 2018. AI-specific guidance ongoing: EDPB Opinion 28/2024 on AI models (December 2024), CNIL AI recommendations (February and June 2025), Digital Omnibus GDPR amendments proposed November 2025 (Article 88c on legitimate interest for AI training).

**Regulatory Philosophy:** comprehensive_horizontal

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The GDPR is the EU's comprehensive data protection regulation that, while not AI-specific, applies extensively to AI systems that process personal data. It governs how personal data can be collected, stored, and used for AI training, profiling, and automated decision-making. Key AI-relevant provisions include the legal basis requirements for training data (Articles 6 and 9), rights related to automated decision-making (Article 22), transparency obligations (Articles 13-15), the right to erasure (Article 17), and data protection impact assessments (Article 35). Since 2024, the EDPB and national DPAs have issued substantial AI-specific guidance clarifying how these provisions apply to foundation models, LLMs, and generative AI systems.

**Key Provisions:**

- Legal basis for AI training data: Article 6(1)(f) legitimate interest is the primary legal basis for AI model training on personal data, as confirmed by EDPB Opinion 28/2024 (December 2024) and the CNIL (June 2025). Requires three-step balancing test: (i) legitimate interest exists, (ii) processing is necessary, (iii) data subject rights do not override. The Digital Omnibus (November 2025) proposes new Article 88c explicitly codifying legitimate interest for AI training with safeguards.
- Automated decision-making: Article 22 grants data subjects the right not to be subject to decisions based solely on automated processing (including profiling) that produce legal effects or similarly significantly affect them. Exceptions: explicit consent, contractual necessity, or authorisation by law. When exceptions apply, the controller must implement suitable safeguards including the right to obtain human intervention, express a point of view, and contest the decision.
- Transparency and right to explanation: Articles 13(2)(f), 14(2)(g), and 15(1)(h) require controllers to provide 'meaningful information about the logic involved, as well as the significance and envisaged consequences' of automated decision-making. Recital 71 (non-binding) suggests a right to explanation of individual decisions. Debated in legal scholarship — GDPR provides information rights rather than a full 'right to explanation' of AI reasoning.
- Right to erasure and AI models: Article 17 grants data subjects the right to request deletion of personal data. Creates a fundamental tension with AI models where personal data is embedded in model weights. The EDPB acknowledges that if an AI model has been duly anonymised post-training, erasure from the model may not be required. Machine unlearning remains technically immature and is not mandated.
- Data minimisation and purpose limitation: Article 5(1)(b) (purpose limitation) and Article 5(1)(c) (data minimisation) apply to AI training. Data collected for one purpose cannot be repurposed for AI training without a compatible new purpose. The CNIL clarifies that data minimisation does not prohibit large training datasets, but requires selecting and filtering data to avoid unnecessary personal data processing.
- Data Protection Impact Assessment (DPIA): Article 35 mandates DPIAs for processing 'likely to result in a high risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.' AI systems involving systematic profiling, large-scale processing of special category data, or automated decision-making with legal effects virtually always trigger the DPIA requirement. All high-risk AI systems under the EU AI Act also presumptively require a DPIA.
- Special category data: Article 9 prohibits processing of biometric data, health data, racial/ethnic origin, political opinions, etc. unless strict exceptions apply (explicit consent, substantial public interest, etc.). AI facial recognition systems using biometric data for identification are subject to both Article 9 GDPR and the AI Act's prohibited practices provisions.
- Web scraping for AI training: The CNIL (June 2025) confirmed that scraping publicly available data for AI training can be lawful under legitimate interest, but requires: respecting robots.txt and ai.txt signals, implementing filters to exclude unnecessary personal and sensitive data, continuous relevance review, and prompt deletion of irrelevant data. Companies must not scrape from sites that have explicitly objected.
- Right to object to AI training: Article 21 gives data subjects the right to object to processing based on legitimate interest, including for AI training. Meta's 2025 implementation of opt-out mechanisms for EU users (contested by noyb) illustrates the practical challenges. The CNIL recommends making objection mechanisms accessible and effective.
- AI model anonymisation: EDPB Opinion 28/2024 states that AI models may be considered anonymous (and thus outside GDPR scope) if it is very unlikely that (1) individuals can be directly or indirectly identified from the model and (2) personal data can be extracted through queries. Assessment is case-by-case; methods include evaluating memorisation risks, regurgitation testing, and differential privacy techniques.

**Risk Classification:** The GDPR itself does not use a risk classification system for AI. However, it operates on a risk-proportionate basis: high-risk processing (automated decision-making with legal effects, large-scale profiling, biometric identification) triggers enhanced obligations including DPIAs, prior consultation with DPAs, and stricter transparency requirements. The AI Act's risk tiers (unacceptable, high, limited, minimal) operate alongside GDPR — high-risk AI systems under the AI Act almost always also trigger GDPR's enhanced requirements.

**Covered Entities:** All data controllers and processors that process personal data of individuals in the EU, regardless of establishment location. For AI specifically: AI model providers (as data controllers for training data processing), AI deployers (as controllers or processors depending on the context), companies scraping web data for AI training, cloud providers hosting AI models processing personal data. Both the training phase (data collection, preparation, model training) and deployment phase (inference, outputs containing personal data) are covered.

**Sector Scope:** Horizontal regulation applying across all sectors. No sector exemptions for AI. Particularly impactful for AI in: healthcare (Article 9 special category data), financial services (credit scoring as automated decision-making under Article 22), employment (profiling and automated hiring decisions), law enforcement (facial recognition, predictive policing), advertising (behavioural profiling), and education. Scientific research benefits from a broader lawful processing framework under Article 89.

**Open Source Treatment:** The GDPR draws no distinction between open-source and proprietary AI models. All models that process personal data must comply equally, regardless of licensing. The EDPB Opinion 28/2024 applies the same anonymisation, legitimate interest, and lawfulness tests to all AI models. This contrasts with the EU AI Act, which provides partial exemptions for open-source GPAI models. A practical consequence: open-source AI model developers who release weights publicly face additional exposure, as third-party researchers can more easily test for personal data memorisation and regurgitation, potentially triggering Article 17 erasure requests or DPA investigations.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** Extensive extraterritorial scope under Article 3. The GDPR applies to: (1) any organisation established in the EU processing personal data, regardless of where the processing occurs (Article 3(1)); (2) non-EU organisations processing data of EU individuals when offering goods/services to them or monitoring their behaviour within the EU (Article 3(2)). For AI: a US or Chinese AI company that scrapes personal data of EU residents for model training, or deploys an AI system that processes EU personal data, must comply with the GDPR regardless of where the company or its servers are located. Non-EU controllers must appoint an EU representative (Article 27). This is the original model for extraterritorial data regulation that the AI Act later emulated.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** Two-tier fine structure: (1) Up to EUR 20 million or 4% of total worldwide annual turnover (whichever is higher) for violations of data processing principles (Articles 5, 6, 7, 9), data subject rights (Articles 12-22), and international data transfers (Articles 44-49). (2) Up to EUR 10 million or 2% of worldwide annual turnover for violations of controller/processor obligations, certification requirements, and monitoring body obligations. AI-specific enforcement examples: Clearview AI fined over EUR 90 million cumulatively across EU/UK jurisdictions for GDPR violations related to facial recognition database; OpenAI fined EUR 15 million by Italian Garante (December 2024) for ChatGPT GDPR violations including lack of legal basis, transparency failures, and inadequate age verification; Meta fined EUR 1.2 billion by Irish DPC (2023) for US data transfers. The Digital Omnibus proposes SME penalty relief: fines capped at the lower of the fixed amount or percentage for organisations under 750 employees.

**Enforcement Body:** National Data Protection Authorities (DPAs) in each Member State, coordinated through the European Data Protection Board (EDPB). Key DPAs active on AI: CNIL (France, most detailed AI guidance), Garante (Italy, first GenAI fine against OpenAI), Irish DPC (lead authority for US tech companies under one-stop-shop mechanism: Meta, Google, Microsoft, Apple), Hamburg DPA (Germany, active on AI enforcement). The EDPB provides binding opinions and coordinates cross-border enforcement through the consistency mechanism.

**Compliance Timeline:** GDPR has been fully applicable since 25 May 2018 — all AI-relevant provisions are already in force. Key AI-specific guidance timeline: Dec 2024 — EDPB Opinion 28/2024 on AI models and personal data; Feb 2025 — CNIL publishes AI transparency and data subject rights recommendations; Jun 2025 — CNIL finalises legitimate interest guidance for AI training; May 2025 — OLG Cologne rules Meta AI training lawful under legitimate interest; Nov 2025 — Digital Omnibus proposes Article 88c (legitimate interest for AI training) and SME relief; 2026 — EDPB coordinated enforcement action on transparency (Articles 12-14), directly relevant to AI systems; Mid-to-late 2026 — Digital Omnibus GDPR amendments could be adopted via ordinary legislative procedure.

**Reporting Requirements:** DPIA required before any high-risk AI processing begins (Article 35), with prior consultation of the DPA if residual high risks cannot be mitigated (Article 36). Data breach notification to the supervisory authority within 72 hours (Article 33) and to affected data subjects without undue delay if high risk (Article 34). Records of processing activities must document all AI-related personal data processing (Article 30). Transparency notices must inform data subjects about automated decision-making, profiling, and AI training use of their data (Articles 13-14). The CNIL recommends layered privacy notices for AI systems: essential information upfront, detailed technical explanations linked separately, including diagrams of how data flows through training, model, and outputs.

**Sme Provisions:** The GDPR provides limited SME-specific relief: exemption from Records of Processing Activities (ROPA) for organisations with fewer than 250 employees, unless processing is likely to result in a risk to rights and freedoms (Article 30(5)) — since most AI processing involves such risk, this exemption rarely applies in practice. The Digital Omnibus (November 2025) proposes expanding this: ROPA exemption threshold raised from 250 to 750 employees, and the trigger changed from 'any risk' to 'high risk.' The Commission targets a 25% compliance cost reduction for all companies and 35% for SMEs. However, there is no SME exemption from DPIA requirements, data subject rights, or the fundamental principles of lawful processing — AI startups face the same core obligations as large enterprises.

**Liability Model:** fault_based (primary). Unlike the EU AI Act which works through product safety/strict liability, GDPR liability is primarily fault-based: data controllers and processors are liable for damage caused by GDPR-non-compliant processing (Article 82). Controllers bear the burden of demonstrating compliance (accountability principle, Article 5(2)). Processors are liable only for damage caused by processing outside lawful instructions or in breach of processor-specific obligations. Joint controllership (Article 26) is common in AI value chains where multiple parties determine purposes and means of processing. The withdrawn AI Liability Directive (February 2025) would have introduced reversed burden of proof for AI-related claims, but its withdrawal leaves GDPR's Article 82 as the primary civil liability route for AI data protection harms.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** The GDPR's impact on AI businesses operates at multiple levels: TRAINING DATA ACQUISITION — the most fundamental impact. Companies cannot freely scrape personal data for AI training. Web scraping requires legitimate interest assessment, safeguards, and respecting opt-out mechanisms. This creates a significant cost and process burden: Meta invested months negotiating with the Irish DPC and EDPB before proceeding with EU data training (2024-2025), and still faces noyb class action threats. STARTUPS face disproportionate burden: GDPR compliance for AI startups costs $20,000-$100,000+ in initial implementation, with ongoing costs for DPIAs, DPO appointment, and data subject rights management. Many EU AI startups report avoiding personal data entirely or limiting EU market exposure. LARGE ENTERPRISES benefit from established legal/compliance teams and can absorb costs. US Big Tech companies (Meta, Google, OpenAI) face sustained enforcement pressure but have resources to comply. COMPETITIVE ASYMMETRY — EU companies face GDPR constraints that US and Chinese competitors largely avoid in their home markets, creating a first-mover disadvantage in data-intensive AI development. However, GDPR compliance can create a TRUST PREMIUM — B2B AI companies demonstrating GDPR compliance gain credibility with enterprise customers, particularly in regulated sectors (healthcare, finance). DATA PARTNERSHIPS — GDPR's legitimate interest framework for AI training (clarified 2024-2025) provides more legal certainty than previously existed, potentially unlocking more data for European AI development.

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed but increasingly negative in perception. CONSTRAINING: (1) Northeastern University researcher found measurable decline in AI innovation output in EU versus comparable jurisdictions post-GDPR; (2) European AI venture capital investment lagged behind US, with GDPR compliance costs cited as contributing factor; (3) Enterprise AI sales cycles 30% longer in EU than US due to GDPR compliance requirements; (4) Purpose limitation and data minimisation principles structurally limit the 'data maximalism' approach that drove US AI development; (5) Right to erasure creates ongoing legal uncertainty for model weights containing personal data. PROMOTING: (1) EDPB and CNIL guidance (2024-2025) provided clearer rules for AI training under legitimate interest, reducing regulatory uncertainty; (2) Privacy-preserving AI techniques (federated learning, differential privacy, synthetic data) became a competitive advantage for EU companies; (3) Digital Omnibus proposal (November 2025) signals political will to accommodate AI within GDPR; (4) OLG Cologne ruling (May 2025) backing Meta's legitimate interest for AI training provided judicial validation. NET ASSESSMENT: GDPR imposes real costs on AI innovation, but the 2024-2025 wave of guidance and the Digital Omnibus represent a significant course correction. The question is whether it comes fast enough to prevent European AI companies from falling further behind.

**Global Influence:** The GDPR's 'Brussels effect' on AI governance is substantial and arguably more impactful than the AI Act's. DIRECT INFLUENCE: (1) California's CCPA/CPRA, Brazil's LGPD, Japan's APPI, South Korea's PIPA, India's DPDP Act — over 150 countries have adopted GDPR-inspired data protection laws, all of which constrain AI training data use; (2) GDPR's extraterritorial reach means US and Chinese AI companies de facto comply when processing EU data, creating global compliance standards; (3) The legitimate interest framework for AI training is being watched globally as a model for balancing innovation and privacy. LIMITATIONS: (1) US has no federal equivalent, and current administration favours deregulation; (2) China's PIPL, while inspired by GDPR, provides state exemptions that undermine the privacy-first approach; (3) Some jurisdictions (Singapore, UAE) explicitly design lighter data protection regimes to attract AI investment. The Digital Omnibus GDPR amendments will be closely watched as a test of whether the EU can maintain its regulatory model while accommodating AI development pressures.

**Stakeholder Positions:** INDUSTRY: Generally views GDPR as a significant obstacle to AI development. Meta, Google, and OpenAI have all faced enforcement actions. 56 EU AI companies signed a July 2025 letter urging simplification. Industry broadly welcomed the EDPB's December 2024 opinion on legitimate interest for AI training as providing needed clarity, and the Digital Omnibus Article 88c as a positive step. However, industry considers the proposal insufficient — many wanted a true safe harbour for AI training, not just a codification of the existing balancing test. CIVIL SOCIETY: noyb (Max Schrems' organisation) is the most active critic, filing complaints against Meta's AI training across 11 DPAs and threatening class action lawsuits. Access Now, EDRi, and Corporate Europe Observatory argue the Digital Omnibus amounts to 'Big Tech writing its own rules' and that weakening GDPR for AI threatens fundamental rights. The EDPB-EDPS Joint Opinion on the Digital Omnibus (January 2026) expressed concern about adequate safeguards. REGULATORS: DPAs are split — CNIL has taken a pragmatic, guidance-first approach to AI, while the Italian Garante has been enforcement-heavy (OpenAI fine, ChatGPT temporary ban). The Irish DPC occupies a central but contested role as lead authority for US Big Tech. ACADEMIA: Legal scholars debate whether Article 22's automated decision-making protections are adequate for modern AI, and whether the 'right to explanation' in Recital 71 has binding force.

**Deregulatory Signals:** Yes, significant and accelerating since late 2025. KEY MECHANISMS: (1) Digital Omnibus (November 2025) proposes Article 88c explicitly codifying legitimate interest as a legal basis for AI training — the first amendment to GDPR's core provisions since its adoption; (2) SME ROPA exemption expansion from 250 to 750 employees; (3) Proposed limited exemption for special category data that inadvertently appears in AI training datasets where removal would require 'disproportionate effort'; (4) Commission rhetoric shifting from 'data protection first' to 'innovation and competitiveness' aligned with the Draghi Report's recommendations; (5) OLG Cologne ruling (May 2025) signalling judicial receptivity to AI training under legitimate interest. COUNTERBALANCES: The Digital Omnibus has received significant pushback from civil society, the European Parliament's LIBE Committee, and some Member States including Malta. The EDPB-EDPS Joint Opinion (January 2026) urged caution. The core GDPR architecture (principles, rights, DPIAs) remains unchanged — deregulatory signals primarily target reducing friction for AI-specific use cases within the existing framework rather than dismantling protections.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689) — complementary framework; AI Act governs AI system safety/risk while GDPR governs personal data processing within those systems. Dual compliance required for AI systems processing personal data.
- Digital Omnibus on AI (proposed November 2025) — amends both AI Act and GDPR simultaneously, including Article 88c codifying legitimate interest for AI training.
- EU Copyright Directive (2019/790) — text and data mining provisions (Articles 3-4) interact with GDPR for AI training datasets containing copyrighted content that also includes personal data.
- Product Liability Directive (Directive (EU) 2024/2853) — GDPR non-compliance in AI systems may constitute evidence of product defect under the new PLD.
- ePrivacy Directive (2002/58/EC, as amended) — governs electronic communications data that may feed into AI systems; Digital Omnibus proposes aligning its rules with GDPR amendments.
- OECD AI Principles — GDPR's transparency and accountability principles align with OECD recommendations on responsible AI.
- Council of Europe Framework Convention on AI (2024) — first binding international AI treaty, references GDPR-style data protection as a foundation.
- UK Data Protection Act 2018 / UK GDPR — post-Brexit parallel regime; UK ICO has issued extensive AI-specific GDPR guidance, operating independently from EDPB.

**Key Actors:**

- European Data Protection Board (EDPB) — issued Opinion 28/2024 on AI models (December 2024), the most authoritative guidance on GDPR-AI intersection. Coordinates 2026 enforcement action on transparency.
- CNIL (France) — most active DPA on AI guidance: published comprehensive recommendations on AI system development (2024), transparency/data subject rights for AI (February 2025), and legitimate interest for AI training (June 2025). De facto standard-setter for GDPR-AI compliance.
- Italian Garante per la Protezione dei Dati Personali — first DPA to take enforcement action against a GenAI company: temporarily banned ChatGPT (March 2023), fined OpenAI EUR 15 million (December 2024). Most aggressive AI enforcement posture.
- Irish Data Protection Commission (DPC) — lead supervisory authority for US Big Tech (Meta, Google, Apple, Microsoft) under GDPR's one-stop-shop mechanism. Approved Meta's EU AI training plan (May 2025). Ordered X/Twitter to suspend Grok AI training on EU user data (2024).
- noyb (None of Your Business) — Max Schrems' privacy advocacy organisation. Filed complaints across 11 DPAs against Meta's AI training. Sent cease-and-desist letter threatening class action (May 2025). Key civil society actor driving enforcement.
- Meta Platforms — central test case for GDPR-AI intersection. Paused EU AI training June 2024, resumed May 2025 after DPC approval. OLG Cologne ruling (May 2025) validated its legitimate interest approach.
- OpenAI — received first GenAI GDPR fine (EUR 15M, Italy). Faces ongoing investigations across multiple EU jurisdictions. Ordered to conduct public information campaign on data rights.
- Higher Regional Court of Cologne (OLG Köln) — issued landmark ruling (May 2025) confirming Meta's use of public user data for AI training is lawful under legitimate interest, applying CJEU three-part test.
- European Commission (DG Justice) — proposed Digital Omnibus GDPR amendments for AI (November 2025), signalling willingness to adapt GDPR framework for AI era.
- Hamburg DPA — active on AI enforcement; one of the more innovation-friendly German DPAs in interpreting GDPR for AI.

**Recent Developments:**

- 17 December 2024: EDPB adopts Opinion 28/2024 on AI models and personal data — establishes framework for when AI models can be considered anonymous, confirms legitimate interest as valid legal basis for AI training with three-step balancing test, clarifies that unlawful training data taints deployment unless model is duly anonymised.
- 20 December 2024: Italian Garante fines OpenAI EUR 15 million for ChatGPT GDPR violations — lack of legal basis for training data, transparency failures, inadequate age verification. First GDPR fine against a generative AI company. OpenAI appeals, calling fine 'disproportionate.'
- 7 February 2025: CNIL publishes two AI recommendations: 'AI: Informing Data Subjects' and 'AI: Complying and Facilitating Individuals' Rights' — advocates layered transparency notices, accessible objection mechanisms, and disclosure of memorisation/regurgitation risks.
- May 2025: Irish DPC approves Meta's plan to train AI on EU user data starting 27 May 2025, after Meta provides updated transparency notices, longer notice periods, in-app objection forms, and de-identification measures.
- May 2025: noyb sends Meta cease-and-desist letter, urges 11 DPAs to stop Meta's AI training, threatens European class action lawsuit. Argues opt-out is insufficient — GDPR requires opt-in consent.
- 23 May 2025: Higher Regional Court of Cologne (OLG Köln) rules that Meta's use of public social media data for AI training is lawful under Article 6(1)(f) legitimate interest — landmark judicial validation of EDPB's December 2024 guidance.
- 17 June 2025: CNIL publishes final recommendations on legitimate interest as legal basis for AI training — details three-step balancing test, safeguards required, and web scraping conditions.
- 14 October 2025: EDPB announces 2026 coordinated enforcement action will focus on transparency obligations (Articles 12-14 GDPR) — directly relevant to AI system transparency.
- 19 November 2025: European Commission publishes Digital Omnibus proposal including GDPR amendments: Article 88c codifies legitimate interest for AI training, expands SME ROPA exemption to 750 employees, proposes limited exemption for incidental special category data in AI datasets. Feedback period open until March 2026.
- January 2026: EDPB-EDPS publish Joint Opinion 1/2026 on the Digital Omnibus AI proposal, expressing concerns about adequacy of proposed safeguards for personal data in AI training. Calls for stronger protections.
- January 2026: Corporate Europe Observatory publishes report 'Article by article, how Big Tech shaped the EU's roll-back of digital rights' — documents industry lobbying influence on Digital Omnibus GDPR amendments.
- February 2026: Multiple DPAs across Europe investigating DeepSeek for GDPR compliance following its January 2025 release — demonstrating proactive enforcement posture toward new AI entrants.

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** The GDPR-AI intersection is among the most critical compliance areas for any AI startup targeting European markets. KEY IMPLICATIONS: (1) DATA STRATEGY IS BUSINESS STRATEGY — the legal basis for training data acquisition is a foundational business decision. Startups must choose between legitimate interest (cheaper but legally contested, as the Meta/noyb dispute shows), consent (expensive and impractical at scale), or synthetic/anonymised data (technically challenging but compliance-safe). This choice shapes the entire business model. (2) COMPLIANCE COSTS — GDPR compliance for AI startups runs $20,000-$100,000+ initially, plus ongoing costs for DPIAs, DPO, data subject rights handling, and transparency infrastructure. A DPIA alone can cost EUR 10,000-50,000 for complex AI systems. These costs are proportionally much higher for startups than for established enterprises. (3) RIGHT TO ERASURE RISK — if a data subject requests erasure and their data was used in training, the startup may face costly retraining or untested machine unlearning approaches. Designing privacy-by-default architecture from day one is essential. (4) COMPETITIVE MOATS FROM COMPLIANCE — GDPR-compliant AI companies gain a trust premium in B2B sales, especially in regulated sectors (healthcare, finance, HR). European enterprises increasingly require GDPR compliance documentation from AI vendors. This creates a barrier to entry that benefits first-movers who invest in privacy engineering. (5) OPT-OUT INFRASTRUCTURE — any AI system training on user data must provide accessible opt-out mechanisms (Article 21). The Meta/noyb dispute shows this is an active enforcement area. Designing opt-out from the start avoids costly retrofitting. (6) DUAL COMPLIANCE — AI startups must navigate both GDPR and the EU AI Act simultaneously. High-risk AI systems face DPIA requirements under GDPR and conformity assessment under the AI Act. Understanding the interaction between these frameworks is a genuine competitive advantage. (7) DIGITAL OMNIBUS OPPORTUNITY — the proposed Article 88c legitimate interest codification, if adopted (expected mid-to-late 2026), would provide clearer legal footing for AI training on personal data. Startups should design compliance architectures that can benefit from this clarification once adopted. (8) GEOGRAPHIC STRATEGY — some EU startups deliberately limit initial deployment outside the EU to avoid GDPR complexity, then expand once compliance infrastructure is mature. Others position GDPR compliance as a selling point for global enterprise sales.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary session for GDPR-AI intersection coverage, alongside the EU AI Act. Also relevant to Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) for data strategy decisions and DPIA requirements, and Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) for understanding how GDPR shapes data acquisition costs, competitive moats, and market access. Can provide concrete case studies (OpenAI/Garante, Meta/noyb, Clearview AI) for ethical discussion in Session 5.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official EDPB opinions (Opinion 28/2024), CNIL published recommendations, court rulings (OLG Cologne), DPA enforcement decisions (Garante/OpenAI), and analysis from major international law firms (Skadden, DLA Piper, Freshfields, Wilson Sonsini, Hogan Lovells, Baker McKenzie). Fine amounts and enforcement actions verified through official DPA press releases and EUR-Lex legal texts.

**Cross Reference:** EDPB official publications (edpb.europa.eu), CNIL official site (cnil.fr), Irish DPC press releases (dataprotection.ie), Italian Garante decisions, EUR-Lex (eur-lex.europa.eu), IAPP analysis (iapp.org), noyb complaint filings (noyb.eu), Future of Privacy Forum reports (fpf.org), ICO guidance (ico.org.uk), Corporate Europe Observatory, European Parliament Research Service, Library of Congress Global Legal Monitor, TechPolicy.Press.

**Caveats:** The GDPR-AI regulatory landscape is in its most active period of evolution: (1) The Digital Omnibus proposal (November 2025) could fundamentally change GDPR's application to AI training through Article 88c — adoption timeline uncertain (mid-to-late 2026), and significant pushback from EDPB-EDPS, civil society, and some Member States may alter the final text substantially; (2) The noyb vs Meta dispute over AI training opt-out is ongoing and could produce binding precedent or trigger a CJEU referral; (3) The EDPB Opinion 28/2024 is guidance, not law — DPAs retain discretion in individual cases, and divergent national enforcement approaches persist (compare CNIL's guidance-first approach with Italy's enforcement-first approach); (4) The question of whether AI model weights constitute personal data remains unresolved — the EDPB's case-by-case anonymisation test leaves significant uncertainty; (5) Machine unlearning technology is immature, creating a practical gap between Article 17 erasure rights and technical feasibility; (6) Multiple DPAs are investigating DeepSeek and other new AI models, and enforcement outcomes may further shift the landscape in 2026.
