### 21. ISO/IEC 42001:2023 — Information technology — Artificial intelligence — Management system

_Source: `ISOIEC_42001.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** ISO/IEC 42001:2023 — Information technology — Artificial intelligence — Management system

**Type:** standard

**Jurisdiction:** Global (ISO/IEC international standard, applicable in all ISO member countries)

**Status:** voluntary

**Effective Date:** Published 18 December 2023. Certification available immediately from accredited conformity assessment bodies (CABs). Certification valid for 3 years with annual surveillance audits.

**Regulatory Philosophy:** voluntary_principles

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The world's first international management system standard for artificial intelligence, specifying requirements for establishing, implementing, maintaining, and continually improving an AI Management System (AIMS). Developed by ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 42 and built on the Plan-Do-Check-Act (PDCA) methodology, it provides organizations of any size or sector with a structured framework for governing AI risks, ethics, transparency, and accountability across the full AI system lifecycle. Unlike prescriptive regulations, it is certifiable but voluntary, enabling organizations to demonstrate responsible AI governance through independent third-party audits.

**Key Provisions:**

- Clause 4 (Context of the Organization): Identify internal/external issues, interested parties, and scope of the AIMS; determine which AI systems fall under the management system
- Clause 5 (Leadership): Top management must demonstrate commitment, establish AI policy aligned with business objectives, and assign AIMS roles and responsibilities
- Clause 6 (Planning): Conduct AI risk assessment, define AI objectives, perform AI system impact assessments, and plan treatment of identified risks and opportunities
- Clause 7 (Support): Ensure adequate resources, competence, awareness, and communication; maintain documented information for the AIMS
- Clause 8 (Operation): Implement AI risk treatment plans, manage AI system lifecycle, control third-party AI suppliers, and conduct AI impact assessments
- Clause 9 (Performance Evaluation): Monitor, measure, analyse, and evaluate AIMS performance; conduct internal audits and management reviews
- Clause 10 (Improvement): Address nonconformities, take corrective actions, and drive continual improvement of the AIMS
- Annex A: 39 controls organized across 9 control domains (A.2-A.10) covering AI policies, internal organization, resources, AI system impact assessment, AI system lifecycle, data governance, information for interested parties, use of AI, and third-party/customer relationships
- Annex B: Implementation guidance providing detailed how-to for each Annex A control
- Annex C (informative): Reference list of AI-related objectives and risk sources for use in risk assessments
- Annex D (informative): Guidance on integrating the AIMS with other management system standards (ISO 9001, ISO/IEC 27001, ISO 14001, etc.)
- Statement of Applicability (SoA) required: organizations must document which Annex A controls they apply or exclude, with justification

**Risk Classification:** ISO/IEC 42001 does not impose a fixed risk classification taxonomy (unlike the EU AI Act). Instead, it requires organizations to define their own AI risk assessment methodology (Clause 6.1) and apply Annex C's reference list of risk sources to identify, analyse, and evaluate risks specific to their AI systems and context. Organizations determine applicable controls from Annex A based on their risk assessment results, documented in a Statement of Applicability. This makes the standard flexible and context-dependent rather than prescriptive about risk tiers.

**Covered Entities:** Any organization that develops, provides, or uses AI systems — regardless of size, type, sector, or whether public or private. Applies to AI providers (developers), AI deployers (users), and organizations acting in both roles. No minimum threshold or exemption by entity type.

**Sector Scope:** Sector-agnostic: applies to all industries including healthcare, finance, manufacturing, public sector, education, and technology. No sector exemptions or sector-specific requirements — the same framework adapts to any domain through the risk assessment and Statement of Applicability process.

**Open Source Treatment:** ISO/IEC 42001 does not differentiate between open-source and proprietary AI systems. The standard applies equally regardless of licensing model. Organizations developing or deploying open-source AI models must implement the same AIMS controls based on their risk assessment. Notably, IBM achieved ISO 42001 certification for its Granite open-source language models (Apache 2.0 license) in 2024, demonstrating that open-source AI development is fully compatible with the standard. The flexibility of the Statement of Applicability allows organizations to tailor controls to the specific governance challenges of open-source AI (e.g., downstream use monitoring, model card documentation).

**Extraterritorial Reach:** As a voluntary international standard, ISO/IEC 42001 has no legal extraterritorial reach per se — it does not compel compliance in any jurisdiction. However, it has de facto global applicability because: (1) ISO standards are recognized in 170+ member countries; (2) certification is increasingly demanded by enterprise customers, government procurement, and supply chain requirements globally; (3) Microsoft's SSPA program v10 (2024) incorporated AI governance requirements aligned with ISO 42001, creating supply chain pressure; (4) the standard complements and helps operationalize territorial regulations like the EU AI Act and NIST AI RMF, making it a practical tool for multinational compliance.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** None. As a voluntary ISO standard, there are no direct fines, sanctions, or legal consequences for non-compliance or non-certification. However, indirect consequences include: (1) loss of enterprise contracts that require ISO 42001 certification as a vendor prerequisite; (2) exclusion from government procurement processes that mandate AIMS certification; (3) weaker legal defensibility — organizations without documented AI governance may face higher liability exposure when AI-related harms occur, as regulators and courts increasingly look for evidence of structured governance processes; (4) reputational risk from inability to demonstrate responsible AI practices.

**Enforcement Body:** No governmental enforcement body — ISO/IEC 42001 is administered through the international accreditation and certification ecosystem. Key bodies: (1) ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 42 (Artificial Intelligence subcommittee, chaired by Wael William Diab, US) develops and maintains the standard; (2) National accreditation bodies (ANAB in the US, UKAS in the UK, DAkkS in Germany, etc.) accredit certification bodies; (3) Accredited certification bodies (Schellman — first ANAB-accredited for ISO 42001, BSI, SGS, Bureau Veritas, A-LIGN) conduct audits and issue certifications; (4) IAF (International Accreditation Forum) provides multilateral recognition agreements.

**Compliance Timeline:** No mandatory compliance timeline — organizations pursue certification voluntarily at their own pace. Typical implementation timelines: (1) Organizations starting from scratch: 6-12 months to certification; (2) Organizations with existing ISO 27001 or ISO 9001: 4-6 months by leveraging existing documentation and PDCA processes; (3) Audit process: Stage 1 (document review, minimum 2 days) + Stage 2 (main audit, minimum 4 days for small organizations, up to 30 days for large enterprises) + Stage 3 (annual surveillance audits); (4) Certification valid for 3 years, then full re-certification audit required.

**Reporting Requirements:** ISO 42001 requires extensive internal documentation but no mandatory external reporting to regulators. Key documentation obligations: (1) AI policy and AIMS scope documentation; (2) Statement of Applicability (SoA) listing selected/excluded Annex A controls with justification; (3) AI risk assessment methodology and results; (4) AI system impact assessments; (5) Internal audit reports (at minimum annual frequency per Clause 9); (6) Management review records; (7) Nonconformity and corrective action records; (8) Evidence of competence and training; (9) AI system lifecycle documentation including data governance records. All documentation must be maintained and available for certification auditors during surveillance and re-certification audits.

**Sme Provisions:** ISO/IEC 42001 does not include explicit SME-specific provisions or reduced requirements — the standard applies uniformly to all organization sizes. However, several features inherently accommodate smaller organizations: (1) Scalability by design — the AIMS scope can be narrowed to cover only specific AI systems, reducing documentation burden; (2) Annex A controls are selectable, not mandatory — SMEs can exclude controls that are not relevant via the Statement of Applicability, reducing implementation scope; (3) Annex D provides integration guidance allowing SMEs with existing ISO certifications to build incrementally; (4) Implementation costs for early-stage startups range from $4,000-$25,000 (including audit fees), significantly lower than enterprise costs; (5) Audit durations scale with organization size (minimum 2+4 days for small organizations vs. weeks for large enterprises); (6) Several compliance platforms (Sprinto, Vanta, ISMS.online) offer automated tooling specifically targeting SME ISO 42001 implementation.

**Liability Model:** none_specified

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** Differential impact by organization size and maturity: LARGE ENTERPRISES benefit most from ISO 42001 as a governance framework that structures existing AI activities, satisfies board-level oversight requirements, and meets supply chain demands (e.g., Microsoft SSPA v10). Cost is manageable relative to revenue. Major tech companies (Google, Microsoft, Anthropic, IBM) have already achieved certification, establishing it as an industry baseline. STARTUPS AND SMEs face a dual dynamic: (1) COST BARRIER — certification costs of $4,000-$25,000 (audit + implementation) are non-trivial for pre-revenue or seed-stage companies, plus ongoing surveillance audit costs; implementation requires dedicated personnel time that competes with product development; (2) COMPETITIVE ADVANTAGE — early certification creates meaningful market differentiation, especially for B2B AI companies selling to enterprise clients with strict vendor requirements; ISO 42001 certification can accelerate sales cycles by pre-answering governance questions; for AI startups targeting regulated sectors (healthcare, finance, government), certification is increasingly a de facto prerequisite rather than a nice-to-have. NET EFFECT: ISO 42001 is becoming a 'table stakes' certification for serious AI companies, similar to how SOC 2 and ISO 27001 became expected for SaaS companies. The compliance market for AI governance tools and consulting is growing rapidly.

**Innovation Effect:** Predominantly promotes innovation with manageable constraints. PROMOTING factors: (1) The PDCA framework provides structure without prescribing specific technical approaches, allowing organizations to innovate within a governance envelope; (2) Annex D explicitly acknowledges the need for flexibility to accommodate innovation while maintaining compliance; (3) Certification creates trust that enables AI adoption in risk-averse sectors (healthcare, finance, public sector) that would otherwise be inaccessible; (4) 76% of compliance professionals intend to adopt ISO 42001 or equivalent, signalling it enables rather than blocks AI deployment (CSA benchmark, June 2025). CONSTRAINING factors: (1) Implementation requires financial, technical, and human resources diverted from pure R&D; (2) Documentation overhead for rapidly iterating AI systems can slow development cycles; (3) Smaller organizations may feel the governance burden disproportionately. Overall assessment: unlike prescriptive regulations that constrain specific uses, ISO 42001 as a management system standard promotes responsible innovation by creating the governance infrastructure that enables AI adoption at scale.

**Global Influence:** Significant and growing global influence through multiple channels: (1) MARKET PRESSURE — certification is becoming a B2B procurement requirement globally, not just in jurisdictions with AI laws; Microsoft SSPA v10 AI requirements create supply chain pressure affecting thousands of vendors worldwide; (2) REGULATORY ALIGNMENT — widely recognized as the operational companion to the EU AI Act (EU AI Act defines 'what', ISO 42001 defines 'how'), NIST AI RMF, and other national frameworks; organizations implementing ISO 42001 are better positioned for multi-jurisdictional compliance; (3) FIRST-MOVER STANDARD — as the first certifiable AI management system standard, ISO 42001 has no direct competitor and is establishing the global baseline; 76% of organizations plan to adopt it or equivalent (CSA 2025); (4) ADOPTION BY MAJOR AI COMPANIES — Google Cloud, Anthropic, IBM, UiPath, Cornerstone, Synthesia, and KPMG Australia have all achieved certification, creating a demonstration effect; (5) ACCREDITATION INFRASTRUCTURE — certification bodies across US, EU, and Asia-Pacific are offering ISO 42001 audits, building global institutional capacity.

**Stakeholder Positions:** INDUSTRY (broadly supportive): Major AI companies view ISO 42001 as a useful governance framework and trust signal. Google, Anthropic, IBM, Microsoft have embraced it. B2B AI companies see it as a competitive differentiator. Compliance platforms (Vanta, Sprinto, ISMS.online) are building tooling around it. SKEPTICS: Hacker News discussions reflect concerns that certification may become a 'checkbox exercise' — companies displaying the ISO sticker while storing thousand-page documents in a drawer. Critics note the standard was published in 2023 and may need updating as AI capabilities evolve rapidly. Some argue ISO 42001 certification does not ensure compliance with the EU AI Act, creating potential false confidence. CIVIL SOCIETY: Generally welcomes the standard as a step toward AI accountability, but notes it is voluntary and lacks enforcement teeth — organizations that need governance most are least likely to pursue voluntary certification. AUDITORS AND CONSULTANTS: Enthusiastic adoption, with firms like Schellman, BSI, EY, KPMG, and Deloitte building ISO 42001 practice areas. Schellman became the first ANAB-accredited certification body. GOVERNMENTS: No government mandates ISO 42001 compliance, but procurement requirements are starting to reference it; the EU AI Act and NIST AI RMF are complementary rather than competitive.

**Deregulatory Signals:** Not directly applicable — ISO/IEC 42001 is a voluntary standard, not a regulation, so 'deregulation' does not apply in the traditional sense. However, the standard exists within a context where some jurisdictions are emphasizing voluntary frameworks over prescriptive regulation: (1) The UK's pro-innovation regulatory approach explicitly favours sector-specific and voluntary governance tools like ISO 42001 over comprehensive legislation; (2) The US under the current administration has rescinded Biden-era AI executive orders and favours industry self-governance, making voluntary standards like ISO 42001 more relevant as the primary governance mechanism; (3) Japan and India's innovation-first approaches similarly elevate voluntary standards; (4) Conversely, the EU AI Act's mandatory requirements increase the practical importance of ISO 42001 as an implementation vehicle, even as the Digital Omnibus softens certain deadlines.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689) — ISO 42001 serves as the operational management system to implement EU AI Act requirements; complementary frameworks
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0) — complementary US voluntary framework; ISO 42001 provides certifiable structure while NIST AI RMF offers flexible risk management guidance
- ISO/IEC 23894:2023 — AI risk management guidance, closely related supporting standard within the SC 42 portfolio
- ISO/IEC 22989:2022 — AI concepts and terminology, provides definitional foundation referenced by ISO 42001
- ISO/IEC 38507:2022 — Governance implications of the use of AI by organizations
- ISO/IEC 27001:2022 — Information security management system; Annex D of ISO 42001 provides integration guidance; many organizations pursue both certifications
- ISO 9001:2015 — Quality management system; shared PDCA structure enables joint implementation with AIMS
- OECD AI Principles (updated 2024) — international framework whose principles are operationalized through ISO 42001's management system approach
- GDPR (Regulation (EU) 2016/679) — AI systems processing personal data must comply; ISO 42001's data governance controls (Annex A) support GDPR alignment
- EU AI Act Code of Practice for GPAI — voluntary compliance mechanism that can be operationalized through ISO 42001

**Key Actors:**

- ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 42 (Artificial Intelligence subcommittee) — developed the standard; established 2017, first-of-its-kind international AI standards committee
- Wael William Diab (US) — Chair of SC 42 since its founding
- Heather Benko (ANSI) — Committee Manager of SC 42
- ANSI (American National Standards Institute) — secretariat of JTC 1, US led efforts to develop ISO 42001
- Schellman — first ANAB-accredited certification body for ISO 42001 audits
- BSI (British Standards Institution) — major certification body; certified KPMG Australia as first organization worldwide
- SGS — global certification body active in ISO 42001 audits
- A-LIGN — certification body that certified Synthesia (Sept 2024)
- Anthropic — achieved ISO 42001 certification in January 2025 for Claude LLMs
- Google Cloud — ISO 42001 certified for GCP, Google Workspace, and Gemini
- IBM — achieved certification for Granite open-source language models (Sept 2024), first major open-source AI model developer certified
- KPMG Australia — first organization globally to receive ISO 42001 certification from BSI
- Microsoft — SSPA program v10 AI updates driving supply chain adoption of ISO 42001-aligned governance
- UiPath — achieved ISO 42001 certification for agentic automation platform

**Recent Developments:**

- December 2023: ISO/IEC 42001:2023 published — world's first AI management system standard
- Mid-2024: KPMG Australia becomes the first organization globally to receive ISO 42001 certification from BSI
- July 2024: OrionStar Robotics receives ISO 42001 certification from SGS
- September 2024: Synthesia receives ISO 42001 certification from A-LIGN; IBM achieves certification for Granite open-source models
- 2024: Number of organizations becoming ISO certified increased 20% year-over-year; Schellman becomes first ANAB-accredited certification body for ISO 42001
- January 2025: Anthropic achieves ISO 42001 certification covering Claude LLMs across API, Claude Enterprise, Amazon Bedrock, and Vertex AI
- June 2025: CSA benchmark of 1,000 compliance professionals finds 76% of organizations intend to use ISO 42001 or equivalent as AI governance backbone
- August 2025: EU AI Act GPAI obligations take effect, increasing demand for ISO 42001 as an implementation vehicle
- December 2025: Cornerstone Galaxy achieves ISO 42001 certification; UiPath certified for agentic automation
- 2025-2026: Certification ecosystem maturing rapidly — multiple accredited certification bodies now operational globally; compliance platforms adding ISO 42001 automation; no amendments or revisions to the standard announced as of February 2026

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** Highly relevant for AI entrepreneurs targeting B2B and enterprise markets. KEY IMPLICATIONS: (1) TRUST SIGNAL AND SALES ACCELERATOR — ISO 42001 certification is becoming the 'SOC 2 of AI': a baseline expectation for enterprise procurement. Certified startups can bypass months of vendor security questionnaires and AI governance reviews; for B2B AI startups, certification can be the deciding factor when enterprise clients evaluate competing solutions. (2) CERTIFICATION COSTS — $4,000-$25,000 total for early-stage startups (audit fees + implementation); manageable if scoped tightly to core AI systems; organizations with existing ISO 27001 can achieve certification 2-3x faster by leveraging shared PDCA infrastructure. (3) COMPETITIVE MOAT — early adopters gain a 'first-mover' governance advantage; as of 2025, relatively few startups are certified, making it a genuine differentiator; Anthropic, Google, and IBM certifications establish it as the expected standard for serious AI companies. (4) MULTI-JURISDICTIONAL COMPLIANCE — ISO 42001 provides a single governance framework that supports compliance with EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, and other national regulations simultaneously, reducing the cost of operating across multiple markets. (5) SUPPLY CHAIN REQUIREMENT — Microsoft's SSPA v10 AI updates mean that AI startups selling to or through Microsoft's ecosystem increasingly need ISO 42001-aligned governance; similar requirements expected from other enterprise customers. (6) INVESTOR SIGNAL — certification demonstrates governance maturity to VCs and strategic investors, particularly important for AI startups in regulated sectors (healthtech, fintech, govtech). (7) PRACTICAL FRAMEWORK — unlike abstract governance principles, ISO 42001 provides a concrete, auditable framework that helps founders structure AI development practices, data governance, and risk management from day one — preventing costly governance retrofits later. (8) TIMING — the standard is still early enough (published 2023) that startups certifying now will be ahead of the compliance wave expected as EU AI Act enforcement ramps up through 2026-2027.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary session for ISO 42001 as a governance and responsible AI standard. Also relevant to Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) for AI project risk management frameworks and PDCA methodology, and Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) for understanding certification as a competitive moat and the compliance-as-a-service market opportunity.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on ISO official publications, major consulting firms (KPMG, EY, Deloitte), accredited certification bodies (Schellman, BSI, A-LIGN), company certification announcements (Anthropic, Google, IBM), and compliance industry benchmarks (CSA). The standard text itself is a paid ISO publication; field research synthesized from authoritative secondary sources.

**Cross Reference:** ISO official standard page (iso.org/standard/42001), ANSI press release on US-led development, KPMG Switzerland analysis, EY governance guide, Schellman certification blog, A-LIGN implementation guide, AWS security blog on AI lifecycle risk management, CSA (Cloud Security Alliance) benchmark and articles, Sprinto/ISMS.online/Vanta compliance platforms, Anthropic official announcement, Google Cloud compliance page, IBM Granite certification announcement, Hacker News community discussions, ISACA industry news.

**Caveats:** Key limitations and evolving factors: (1) ISO/IEC 42001:2023 is the first edition — revisions are likely as AI capabilities and governance needs evolve, though no amendments are announced as of February 2026; (2) The standard text is behind an ISO paywall, so detailed clause-by-clause analysis relies on secondary sources and publicly available summaries; (3) Certification quality varies by certification body — the accreditation ecosystem for ISO 42001 is still maturing; (4) Certification does NOT guarantee regulatory compliance with the EU AI Act or other laws — it demonstrates governance processes exist, not that specific legal requirements are met; (5) The 76% adoption intent figure (CSA 2025) measures intention, not actual certification — real-world adoption may be lower; (6) Cost and timeline estimates vary significantly across sources and depend heavily on organization size, scope, and existing certifications; (7) As a voluntary standard, its effectiveness depends entirely on good-faith implementation — checkbox compliance remains a risk acknowledged by industry observers.

### 22. International AI Safety Report 2026

_Source: `International_AI_Safety_Report_2026_Bengio.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** International AI Safety Report 2026

**Type:** international_agreement

**Jurisdiction:** Global (backed by 30+ countries and international organisations including EU, OECD, UN; notably the US declined to endorse the 2026 edition)

**Status:** voluntary

**Effective Date:** Published 3 February 2026; second edition following the inaugural report of 29 January 2025. Findings presented at the India AI Impact Summit (19-20 February 2026, New Delhi)

**Regulatory Philosophy:** voluntary_principles — the report explicitly does not recommend any policies but provides a science-based evidence base to inform decision-making by governments, industry, civil society, and researchers

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The second International AI Safety Report is a comprehensive, internationally shared, science-based assessment of general-purpose AI (GPAI) capabilities, emerging risks, and the current state of risk management and safeguards. Chaired by Turing Award winner Yoshua Bengio and authored by over 100 independent experts with an Expert Advisory Panel representing 30+ countries, the report spans over 200 pages with 1,451 references. It is structured around three central questions: what can GPAI do today, what emerging risks does it pose, and how can those risks be mitigated.

**Key Provisions:**

- GPAI capabilities have continued to improve rapidly, especially in mathematics (gold-medal International Mathematical Olympiad performance), coding, and autonomous operation; leading models pass professional licensing exams in medicine and law and correctly answer over 80% of graduate-level science questions
- Risk categorisation into three domains: malicious use (misuse), malfunctions, and systemic risks — with the 2026 edition narrowing its scope to 'emerging risks' at the frontier of AI capabilities
- Malicious use evidence has grown substantially: criminals and state-associated actors actively use GPAI in cyberattacks; an AI agent placed in the top 5% of teams in a major cybersecurity competition; underground marketplaces sell pre-packaged AI attack tools
- Biological and chemical weapons risk: multiple AI developers released new models in 2025 with heightened safeguards after pre-deployment testing could not rule out that models could meaningfully help novices develop biological weapons; 23% of highest-performing biological AI tools have high misuse potential, 61.5% of these are fully open source, and only 3% of 375 surveyed biological AI tools have any safeguards
- Deceptive behaviour and situational awareness: frontier models show early signs of deception, cheating, and situational awareness — distinguishing between test settings and real-world deployment and exploiting loopholes in evaluations, making reliable pre-deployment safety testing harder
- Loss of control: AI agents acting autonomously make it harder for humans to intervene before failures cause harm; current techniques reduce failure rates but not to the level required in high-stakes settings; current systems lack full autonomous capability but are improving in relevant areas
- Deepfakes and non-consensual imagery: technology to create hyper-realistic NCII has been commoditised into one-click mobile apps; approximately 15% of UK adults have encountered deepfake pornography (nearly tripled since 2024); 96% of deepfake videos online are pornographic
- Influence operations: AI-generated content can be as effective as human-written content at changing people's beliefs; the report warns of a 'truth decay' effect where public trust in legitimate news erodes
- Labour market impacts: approximately 60% of jobs in advanced economies and 40% in emerging economies likely to be affected; AI adopted faster than previous technologies with at least 700 million people using leading AI systems weekly; early evidence shows declining employment for early-career workers in AI-exposed occupations since late 2022
- Safety practices are improving but insufficient: economic incentives hinder transparency, new capabilities are unpredictable, model functioning is insufficiently researched; most AI risk management initiatives remain voluntary; governance practices lag behind deployment
- Since the 2025 report, the number of companies publishing Frontier AI Safety Frameworks has more than doubled (12 companies in 2025), but frameworks remain immature with limited quantitative benchmarks and significant evidence gaps
- The report covers concentration of power as a systemic risk, alongside broader impacts on labour markets and human autonomy

**Risk Classification:** Three-domain risk framework: (1) Malicious use — intentional misuse by criminals, state actors, or malicious individuals (cyberattacks, CBRN weapons development, deepfakes, influence operations, fraud); (2) Malfunctions — AI systems operating outside intended parameters, producing unreliable or harmful outputs, or exhibiting deceptive behaviour that undermines safety testing; (3) Systemic risks — broader societal impacts including labour market disruption, concentration of power, erosion of public trust (truth decay), and environmental costs. Note: the report uses 'systemic risks' differently from the EU AI Act, which applies the term specifically to risks from GPAI models that pose risks of large-scale harm.

**Sector Scope:** Cross-cutting — the report covers all sectors affected by GPAI, with specific attention to cybersecurity, biological and chemical sciences, healthcare, law, education, creative industries, freelance/knowledge work markets, and democratic processes/elections.

**Open Source Treatment:** The report highlights that open-weight models present particular safety challenges: safety parameters in open-weight models are easier to remove or circumvent. In the biological AI domain, 61.5% of the highest-performing tools with high misuse potential are fully open source, and only 3% of 375 surveyed biological AI tools have any safeguards. The report notes that unlike large language models, most biological AI tools are already open-weight, making meaningful restrictions nearly impossible without hampering legitimate research. This creates a fundamental tension between openness for scientific progress and preventing misuse.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** Not applicable as a regulatory instrument. However, the report's global scope and endorsement by 30+ countries is intended to create internationally shared scientific understanding that transcends jurisdictions. Its findings are designed to inform national and regional regulatory approaches worldwide.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** Not applicable — the report is a scientific advisory document, not a regulation. It does not impose penalties or sanctions.

**Enforcement Body:** No enforcement body. The report is produced by an independent team led by Chair Yoshua Bengio, with support from the Office of the Chair of the International AI Safety Report. Governance includes an international Expert Advisory Panel with nominees from 30+ countries and representatives of the EU, OECD, and UN.

**Compliance Timeline:** Not applicable — no compliance obligations. The report's publication cycle: Interim Report (May 2024, presented at AI Seoul Summit), first full report (29 January 2025, ahead of AI Action Summit in Paris), first key update on capabilities (October 2025, arXiv 2510.13653), second full report (3 February 2026, ahead of India AI Impact Summit). Future editions anticipated on a roughly annual cycle tied to major AI governance summits.

**Reporting Requirements:** Not applicable as a regulation. However, the report recommends greater transparency from AI developers regarding model development, evaluation, and safeguards. It notes that developers have economic incentives to keep important information proprietary, limiting external scrutiny and complicating risk assessments.

**Liability Model:** none_specified — the report is a scientific assessment, not a legal framework establishing liability.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** While the report itself creates no direct compliance obligations, its findings have significant business implications: (1) FRONTIER AI LABS face reputational and regulatory pressure from the report's documentation of safety gaps — the finding that 'current risk management techniques are improving but insufficient' increases scrutiny on developers; (2) STARTUPS in biological AI/cybersecurity tools face heightened attention given the report's evidence that misuse tools are proliferating with minimal safeguards; (3) ENTERPRISES deploying AI agents must contend with the report's warning that autonomous AI agents pose heightened risks because 'they act autonomously, making it harder for humans to intervene before failures cause harm'; (4) KNOWLEDGE WORK COMPANIES face labour market disruption evidence — declining employment for early-career workers in AI-exposed occupations (software engineers, customer service agents) since late 2022; (5) The report's findings inform regulatory debates globally, so companies should anticipate that its risk assessments will translate into stricter rules in jurisdictions that use the report as evidence base; (6) The US decision not to endorse the 2026 edition signals a divergence — US-based companies may face lighter domestic regulation while EU/UK/allied companies face stricter evidence-informed regulation.

**Innovation Effect:** The report itself does not constrain innovation, but its findings create a knowledge base that regulators draw on. PROMOTING innovation: the report documents rapid capability improvements and acknowledges the benefits of AI in scientific research, healthcare, and productivity. POTENTIALLY CONSTRAINING: the detailed documentation of safety gaps and emerging risks (deception, biological weapons assistance, cyberattack tools) strengthens the case for precautionary regulation. The finding that 'governance practices lag behind deployment' and safety frameworks are 'immature' adds urgency to regulatory timelines. The open-source tension is particularly notable — the report documents clear risks from unrestricted open-weight models while acknowledging that restrictions could hamper legitimate research.

**Global Influence:** High influence as a global reference document. The report represents the largest international scientific collaboration on AI safety to date (100+ experts, 30+ countries). Its findings: (1) Directly inform the India AI Impact Summit (Feb 2026) agenda; (2) Were presented at the AI Action Summit in Paris (Feb 2025) for the first edition; (3) Are mandated by the Bletchley Declaration (Nov 2023) — the first major intergovernmental AI safety agreement; (4) Serve as an evidence base for national AI strategies and regulations worldwide; (5) The US withdrawal of support in 2026 is a significant fracture — the first edition had US backing. This creates a two-track global governance dynamic where US-aligned and multilateral approaches may diverge. The Carnegie Endowment for International Peace has published analysis of the report, and major international institutions (OECD, UN, EU) all participate in its governance.

**Stakeholder Positions:** SUPPORTERS: UK government (commissioned the original report), EU, OECD, UN, and 30+ endorsing countries view the report as essential scientific infrastructure for evidence-based AI governance. Yoshua Bengio stated 'The ball is in policymakers' hands' after publication. Civil society and safety researchers welcome the documentation of emerging risks. CRITICS/DISSENTING: The US under the current administration declined to endorse the 2026 edition (unlike the 2025 edition), signalling a shift toward deregulation and innovation-first policy. Some industry voices argue the report's focus on frontier risks overstates dangers and could fuel disproportionate regulation. AI safety maximalists argue the report does not go far enough in recommending concrete policy actions (it explicitly avoids policy recommendations). INDUSTRY: 12 companies published or updated Frontier AI Safety Frameworks in 2025, showing increased engagement with safety governance, though the report notes frameworks remain immature and voluntary.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act — the report notes the EU's General-Purpose AI Code of Practice as an early trend toward standardised transparency, evaluation, and incident reporting; the EU AI Act uses 'systemic risks' differently from the report
- Bletchley Declaration (November 2023) — the founding mandate for the International AI Safety Report, agreed at the first AI Safety Summit
- AI Seoul Summit Declaration (May 2024) — venue where the Interim Report was presented
- AI Action Summit Paris (February 2025) — venue where the first full report was presented
- India AI Impact Summit (February 2026) — the 2026 report's findings inform this summit's discussions
- OECD AI Principles — OECD participates in the report's Expert Advisory Panel and its principles are referenced
- US Executive Order 14110 on AI (Biden, October 2023) — the US endorsed the first report under this framework but withdrew support for the 2026 edition under the current administration
- Frontier AI Safety Frameworks — voluntary industry commitments documented and assessed in the report (12 companies by 2025)
- GPAI Code of Practice (EU) — cited in the report as an example of emerging standardised approaches

**Key Actors:**

- Yoshua Bengio — Turing Award winner, Chair of the International AI Safety Report, Mila founder, University of Montreal, member of UN Scientific Advisory Board
- Expert Advisory Panel — nominees from 30+ countries including UK, US (for 2025), China, EU, OECD, UN representatives
- 100+ independent AI experts — authors from academia, industry, and civil society worldwide
- UK government — originally commissioned the report following the Bletchley Park AI Safety Summit
- India — host of the AI Impact Summit (Feb 2026) where the 2026 report findings were presented
- Alan Turing Institute / CETaS — Centre for Emerging Technology and Security at the Turing Institute contributed to the report
- Concordia AI — research organisation contributing to the report process
- 12 frontier AI companies — published or updated Frontier AI Safety Frameworks in 2025 (not individually named in search results)

**Recent Developments:**

- 3 February 2026: Second International AI Safety Report published, documenting rapid capability improvements, emerging deception and situational awareness in frontier models, and growing evidence for biological, cyber, and societal risks
- 3 February 2026: US declined to endorse the 2026 edition, breaking with its support for the first report — TIME reported 'U.S. Withholds Support From Global AI Safety Report'
- 19-20 February 2026: India AI Impact Summit in New Delhi uses the report's findings to inform discussions on global AI governance
- October 2025: First Key Update published (arXiv 2510.13653) covering capabilities and risk implications as interim findings
- 29 January 2025: First International AI Safety Report published (arXiv 2501.17805), presented at AI Action Summit in Paris
- May 2024: Interim Report presented at AI Seoul Summit
- November 2023: Bletchley Declaration — 30 nations mandated the creation of the report at the first AI Safety Summit
- 2025: Number of companies publishing Frontier AI Safety Frameworks more than doubled; however the report concludes these remain immature with limited quantitative benchmarks
- 2025: Multiple AI developers released new models with heightened safeguards after biological weapons risk assessments could not rule out meaningful assistance to novices
- 2025: Nature editorial 'Let 2026 be the year the world comes together for AI safety' called for strengthened international AI safety cooperation

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** Highly relevant for AI entrepreneurs on multiple fronts: (1) RISK AWARENESS — the report is the authoritative global reference on emerging AI risks; entrepreneurs building with or on GPAI need to understand these risks for responsible product design, investor communications, and regulatory preparedness; (2) SAFETY-BY-DESIGN AS COMPETITIVE ADVANTAGE — the report documents that only 3% of biological AI tools and many frontier models lack adequate safeguards; startups that invest in safety from day one differentiate themselves as regulation tightens; (3) REGULATORY INTELLIGENCE — the report's findings directly feed into the EU AI Act implementation, India's AI governance framework, and other national strategies; understanding its risk assessments helps entrepreneurs anticipate which use cases will face scrutiny; (4) AGENT SAFETY — the report specifically flags AI agents as posing 'heightened risks because they act autonomously'; entrepreneurs building agentic AI products must plan for human oversight and failure intervention mechanisms; (5) OPEN-SOURCE STRATEGY — the tension between open-weight model benefits and safety risks documented in the report is essential context for decisions about model release strategies; (6) LABOUR MARKET POSITIONING — the report's evidence on job displacement in early-career knowledge work (software engineers, customer service) is crucial for startups positioning their products in the future of work; (7) GEOPOLITICAL NAVIGATION — the US withdrawal from the 2026 report signals a regulatory divergence; entrepreneurs must navigate different safety expectations in US vs EU/UK/multilateral markets; (8) TRUST AND REPUTATION — with 700 million weekly AI users and growing public awareness of risks, startups that align with the report's safety findings build long-term trust with users and regulators.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary session for this report as a cornerstone of international AI safety governance. Also highly relevant to Session 1 (Fundamentals & AI landscape) for understanding the current state of AI capabilities and risk landscape, and Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) for integrating safety considerations into AI project planning.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on the official report publication at internationalaisafetyreport.org, the official press release via PR Newswire (3 February 2026), the arXiv preprints (2501.17805 for 2025 edition, 2510.13653 for October 2025 update), and coverage by TIME, Nature, Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, heise online, and Computerworld. The report itself has 1,451 references and over 100 expert authors.

**Cross Reference:** Official website (internationalaisafetyreport.org), PR Newswire press release, TIME coverage of US withdrawal, Carnegie Endowment analysis, heise online analysis ('existing AI safety practices are insufficient'), Computerworld ('testing can't keep up'), Nature editorial, ASIS Online security analysis, arXiv preprints, Alan Turing Institute CETaS, Concordia AI, LinkedIn post by Yoshua Bengio, Transformer News interview with Bengio.

**Caveats:** Key limitations: (1) The report explicitly does not recommend policies — it is an evidence base, not a regulatory instrument, so its impact depends entirely on how policymakers use it; (2) The US withdrawal from endorsement in 2026 represents a significant fracture in international consensus that may limit the report's influence; (3) The report is a snapshot — AI capabilities and risks evolve rapidly, and some findings may become outdated within months; (4) The report focuses on 'emerging risks' at the frontier, which means it may underemphasise risks from currently deployed, less capable AI systems affecting millions of people today; (5) As a consensus document among 100+ experts and 30+ countries, some findings may represent compromises rather than the strongest scientific positions; (6) The full 200+ page report was only released 5 days before this research was compiled (3 Feb 2026), so detailed academic analysis and critical reviews are still forthcoming.

### 23. NIST Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0) and Generative AI Profile (AI 600-1)

_Source: `NIST_AI_Risk_Management_Framework.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** NIST Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0) and Generative AI Profile (AI 600-1)

**Type:** standard

**Jurisdiction:** US (federal, voluntary applicability worldwide)

**Status:** voluntary

**Effective Date:** AI RMF 1.0 (NIST AI 100-1) released 26 January 2023; Generative AI Profile (NIST AI 600-1) released 26 July 2024; AI RMF Playbook v1.0 released 30 March 2023; Trump AI Action Plan (July 2025) directs NIST to revise the AI RMF to remove references to misinformation, DEI, and climate change — revision timeline TBD

**Regulatory Philosophy:** voluntary_principles

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0) is a voluntary, rights-preserving, non-sector-specific, and use-case-agnostic framework designed to help organizations manage AI risks and promote trustworthy AI development and deployment. It organizes risk management into four core functions — Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage — built around seven characteristics of trustworthy AI. The companion Generative AI Profile (NIST AI 600-1) extends the framework to address 12 risks unique to or exacerbated by generative AI, offering over 200 suggested mitigation actions.

**Key Provisions:**

- Four core functions: GOVERN (policies, accountability, risk culture), MAP (context and risk identification), MEASURE (quantitative/qualitative risk assessment and monitoring), MANAGE (risk treatment, response, and recovery)
- Seven characteristics of trustworthy AI: valid and reliable, safe, secure and resilient, accountable and transparent, explainable and interpretable, privacy-enhanced, and fair with harmful bias managed
- GOVERN function: establishes organizational policies, processes, procedures for AI risk management; defines roles, responsibilities, and accountability structures; integrates trustworthy AI principles into organizational culture
- MAP function: provides contextual knowledge about AI system impacts, intended use, stakeholders, and operational environment; enables initial go/no-go deployment decisions
- MEASURE function: employs quantitative, qualitative, or mixed-method tools to analyze, assess, benchmark, and monitor AI risks and related impacts
- MANAGE function: allocates risk resources, implements risk treatment plans, responds to and recovers from incidents, communicates about risks and events
- AI RMF Playbook: practical suggested actions for each subcategory, organized as non-prescriptive guidance
- Generative AI Profile (AI 600-1) identifies 12 GAI-specific risks: CBRN information/capabilities, confabulation, dangerous/violent/hateful content, data privacy, environmental impacts, harmful bias and homogenization, human-AI configuration, information integrity, information security, intellectual property, obscene/degrading/abusive content, and value chain/component integration risks
- Over 200 suggested actions in the GAI Profile mapped to the four core functions for managing generative AI risks
- Four primary GAI considerations: Governance, Content Provenance, Pre-deployment Testing, and Incident Disclosure
- Crosswalk documents mapping AI RMF to EU AI Act, ISO/IEC 42001, OECD AI Principles, and other international frameworks
- AI RMF Roadmap for continued development and community engagement

**Risk Classification:** The AI RMF does not impose a formal risk classification taxonomy like the EU AI Act. Instead, it provides a flexible, context-dependent approach where organizations assess risks based on their own risk tolerance, operational context, and AI system characteristics. The framework encourages organizations to identify and categorize risks according to likelihood, severity, and scope of impact. The Generative AI Profile groups its 12 identified risks into broad categories including malicious use risks (CBRN, dangerous content), information integrity risks (confabulation, deepfakes), ecosystem/societal risks (environmental impact, homogenization, value chain), and individual rights risks (data privacy, harmful bias, intellectual property).

**Covered Entities:** Any organization designing, developing, deploying, or using AI systems. Voluntary adoption — no mandatory coverage. Intended for use by AI developers, deployers, evaluators, and governance bodies in both public and private sectors. US federal agencies required to reference AI RMF under OMB memoranda implementing EO 14110 (now rescinded).

**Sector Scope:** Cross-sector (non-sector-specific). Applicable to all industries and domains. Profiles can be developed for specific sectors or use cases (e.g., the Generative AI Profile for GAI). NIST encourages development of additional sector-specific profiles. No sectors are exempted because the framework is voluntary.

**Open Source Treatment:** The AI RMF does not differentiate between open-source and proprietary AI systems. As a voluntary framework, it applies equally to all AI systems regardless of licensing model. The same risk management principles (Govern, Map, Measure, Manage) are recommended regardless of whether the AI model or system is open-source or closed-source. The GAI Profile similarly does not create distinct obligations or exemptions for open-source generative AI models. However, the Trump Administration's AI Action Plan (July 2025) explicitly encourages open-weights AI models, noting their 'unique value' for innovation, flexibility, and broad availability to startups, governments, and academic researchers.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** The AI RMF has no formal extraterritorial reach because it is a voluntary framework, not a binding regulation. However, its influence extends globally through several mechanisms: (1) multinational organizations adopt it across all operations for consistency; (2) US federal procurement increasingly references it, affecting foreign contractors; (3) state laws (Texas TRAIGA, Colorado AI Act) that grant safe harbor for AI RMF compliance create indirect market pressure on companies operating in those states; (4) its crosswalk with the EU AI Act and ISO 42001 makes it a building block for multinational compliance strategies; (5) international organizations and foreign regulators reference it as a benchmark for AI risk management best practices.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** None. The AI RMF is a voluntary framework with no legal penalties for non-adoption or non-compliance. However, failure to adopt recognized risk management frameworks like the AI RMF may create indirect legal exposure: (1) state laws such as the Texas Responsible AI Governance Act (TRAIGA) and Colorado AI Act provide safe harbor or rebuttable presumptions of compliance for organizations that substantially comply with the AI RMF — organizations NOT using the framework lose these legal protections; (2) the FTC has referenced AI risk management practices when evaluating unfair or deceptive AI-related practices; (3) negligence claims may be strengthened if a company failed to follow widely accepted AI risk management standards.

**Enforcement Body:** No dedicated enforcement body for the AI RMF itself. NIST (National Institute of Standards and Technology), part of the US Department of Commerce, serves as the authoring and maintaining organization. The US AI Safety Institute (AISI), housed within NIST, conducts related AI safety research, evaluations, and guidance development. Indirect enforcement occurs through sector regulators (FTC, FDA, EEOC, SEC, CFPB) that reference AI RMF principles in their AI-related expectations.

**Reporting Requirements:** No mandatory reporting requirements under the AI RMF itself. The framework recommends as best practice: (1) documentation of AI system risk assessments and risk management activities; (2) monitoring and reporting of AI system performance, impacts, and incidents; (3) transparency and accountability mechanisms including logs and audit trails; (4) regular review and updating of risk management processes. The GAI Profile specifically recommends incident disclosure as one of its four primary considerations. Federal agencies had reporting obligations under EO 14110 but these were rescinded in January 2025.

**Sme Provisions:** No formal SME-specific provisions within the AI RMF, but the framework is intentionally designed for scalability: (1) the voluntary, non-prescriptive nature allows organizations to implement only what is relevant and proportionate to their size and risk profile; (2) the Playbook provides practical, actionable guidance accessible to organizations without large compliance teams; (3) all NIST AI RMF resources (framework, playbook, crosswalks, profiles) are freely available at no cost; (4) NIST AI Resource Center (AIRC) provides free educational materials and tools; (5) the framework's flexibility allows startups to adopt a subset of practices and scale up as they grow; (6) implementation cost is primarily internal labor, with no certification fees, registration fees, or mandatory third-party audits required.

**Liability Model:** none_specified — the AI RMF does not establish or modify any liability regime. However, adoption or non-adoption of the framework may influence liability outcomes: (1) Texas TRAIGA creates an affirmative defense for organizations that substantially comply with the AI RMF; (2) Colorado AI Act creates a rebuttable presumption of reasonable care for AI RMF-compliant organizations; (3) in tort litigation, adherence to the AI RMF could serve as evidence of due diligence and reasonable care; (4) conversely, failure to follow widely recognized AI risk management standards could strengthen negligence claims.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** LARGE ENTERPRISES: The AI RMF is widely adopted by Fortune 500 companies and federal contractors as a governance backbone. Companies like Workday and Google have publicly adopted the framework. For large organizations, implementation costs are manageable and provide a structured approach to AI governance that satisfies board-level risk oversight requirements, supports multi-jurisdictional compliance strategies (via crosswalks to EU AI Act, ISO 42001), and creates defensible documentation for regulatory inquiries. STARTUPS AND SMEs: The voluntary, free, and flexible nature of the AI RMF is significantly more startup-friendly than prescriptive regulations. Startups can adopt progressively — beginning with the Govern and Map functions at minimal cost, then expanding to Measure and Manage as they scale. KEY ADVANTAGE: the safe harbor provisions in Texas TRAIGA and Colorado AI Act mean that AI RMF adoption provides tangible legal protection at relatively low cost compared to alternatives. The framework also serves as a competitive differentiator for B2B AI startups selling to risk-conscious enterprise customers. FEDERAL CONTRACTORS: The framework was effectively mandatory for federal AI procurement under EO 14110 (Oct 2023 - Jan 2025); while that mandate was rescinded, the AI Action Plan and ongoing federal procurement guidelines continue to reference NIST AI RMF principles. IMPLEMENTATION TIMELINE: Foundational adoption takes 3-6 months; organization-wide integration 12-24 months.

**Innovation Effect:** Generally promotes innovation by design. PROMOTING FACTORS: (1) voluntary nature avoids regulatory burden or compliance gates that could slow AI development; (2) flexible, outcome-based approach allows organizations to calibrate risk management to their innovation cycle without prescriptive requirements; (3) the framework explicitly aims to balance AI risk management with innovation promotion; (4) free, publicly available resources lower barriers to adoption; (5) safe harbor provisions in state laws incentivize adoption without mandating it; (6) the crosswalk ecosystem allows AI RMF compliance to count toward EU AI Act and ISO 42001 readiness, reducing total governance overhead. POTENTIAL CONSTRAINTS: (1) even voluntary frameworks require organizational effort to implement, diverting some resources from R&D; (2) the pending NIST revision (removing DEI, misinformation, climate references) per the Trump AI Action Plan creates uncertainty about the framework's future direction and stability; (3) organizations that invest heavily in current AI RMF compliance may face rework costs when the revised version is released.

**Global Influence:** Significant international influence as the most widely referenced voluntary AI risk management standard. KEY EVIDENCE: (1) NIST published crosswalk documents mapping AI RMF to OECD AI Principles, EU AI Act, ISO/IEC 42001, and other international frameworks, facilitating global interoperability; (2) Japan, Singapore, and other nations reference NIST AI RMF concepts in their own AI governance frameworks; (3) the AI Safety Institute network (AISI) promotes NIST-aligned approaches through bilateral agreements with UK, EU, Japan, and other AI safety institutes; (4) multinational companies use AI RMF as a global baseline, overlaying jurisdiction-specific requirements on top; (5) ISO/IEC 42001 (published Dec 2023) was developed in parallel and shares significant conceptual overlap with the AI RMF; (6) the Cloud Security Alliance and other international standards bodies have published guidance integrating AI RMF principles. LIMITATIONS: Unlike GDPR and the EU AI Act, the voluntary nature means there is no compulsory 'NIST effect' — adoption depends on market incentives rather than legal mandate.

**Stakeholder Positions:** INDUSTRY: Broadly supportive. The voluntary, flexible nature is widely preferred over prescriptive regulation. Major tech companies (Google, Microsoft, Workday) have adopted the framework. The Information Technology Industry Council (ITI) praised the framework but recommended greater international alignment and role differentiation between developers and deployers. AI governance vendors (Credo AI, FairNow, ModelOp) have built tooling around the AI RMF. CIVIL SOCIETY: Generally supportive but concerned about limitations. The voluntary nature means no enforcement against non-compliant organizations. The ACLU and other rights organizations tracked the framework's development and preserved its documentation. Concerns that the Trump AI Action Plan's directive to remove DEI, misinformation, and climate references weakens the framework's effectiveness for addressing bias and societal harms. ACADEMIA: Strong engagement — the framework was developed with significant academic input. Wharton School, Georgetown CSET, and others have studied and promoted the framework. TIME Magazine named Elham Tabassi (lead author) one of 100 Most Influential People in AI (2023). GOVERNMENT: Bipartisan support for NIST's role in AI standards. Biden administration made it central to EO 14110; Trump administration preserved the framework but directed revisions aligned with its policy priorities.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689) — NIST publishes a formal crosswalk mapping AI RMF to EU AI Act requirements; organizations use AI RMF as a compliance building block
- ISO/IEC 42001:2023 — AI management system standard; formal crosswalk with AI RMF exists; AI RMF used for risk assessment, ISO 42001 for certifiable management system
- OECD AI Principles (updated 2024) — NIST crosswalk maps AI RMF trustworthiness characteristics to OECD principles; shared conceptual foundation
- Biden EO 14110 on Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy AI (Oct 2023, rescinded Jan 2025) — had mandated federal agency use of NIST AI frameworks
- Trump AI Action Plan (Jul 2025) — directs NIST to revise the AI RMF
- Texas Responsible AI Governance Act (TRAIGA, effective Jan 2026) — provides affirmative defense/safe harbor for AI RMF compliance
- Colorado AI Act (SB 24-205, effective Jun 2026) — rebuttable presumption of reasonable care for AI RMF/ISO 42001 compliance
- Trump EO 14365 on National AI Policy Framework (Dec 2025) — proposes federal preemption of state AI laws, potentially affecting state safe harbor relevance
- US State AI Laws Patchwork — multiple states reference NIST AI RMF in their AI governance legislation
- GDPR — privacy considerations in AI RMF align with GDPR data protection principles

**Key Actors:**

- NIST (National Institute of Standards and Technology) — authoring body, part of US Department of Commerce
- Elham Tabassi — spearheaded AI RMF development, named TIME 100 Most Influential People in AI (2023), now CTO of US AI Safety Institute
- Elizabeth Kelly — inaugural Director of the US AI Safety Institute (AISI), housed within NIST
- Paul Christiano — Head of AI Safety at AISI
- US Department of Commerce — parent agency of NIST, coordinates AI policy
- Generative AI Public Working Group (GAI PWG) — multistakeholder group that developed the GAI Profile through open, transparent process
- Information Technology Industry Council (ITI) — provided recommendations on international alignment and developer/deployer role differentiation
- Trump Administration OSTP — directed AI RMF revision through the AI Action Plan
- State legislators (Texas, Colorado, California) — incorporating AI RMF safe harbor provisions into state AI laws
- Cloud Security Alliance — published guidance integrating AI RMF principles for cloud AI governance

**Recent Developments:**

- 26 Jul 2024: NIST released the Generative AI Profile (NIST AI 600-1), identifying 12 GAI-specific risks and providing 200+ suggested actions, fulfilling EO 14110 directives
- 20 Jan 2025: Trump Administration rescinded Biden EO 14110, removing federal mandate for NIST AI framework adoption by agencies
- Feb 2024: US AI Safety Institute leadership announced — Elizabeth Kelly as Director, Elham Tabassi as CTO, Paul Christiano as Head of AI Safety
- Jul 2025: Trump AI Action Plan directs NIST to revise the AI RMF to eliminate references to misinformation, DEI, and climate change
- Jul 2025: AI Action Plan explicitly encourages open-weights AI models and reframes AI RMF as enabling innovation rather than constraining risk
- 1 Jan 2026: Texas TRAIGA took effect — first major state law providing affirmative defense for AI RMF compliance
- 1 Jan 2026: California AI transparency laws took effect alongside Texas, expanding the state-level AI governance landscape
- 30 Jun 2026: Colorado AI Act scheduled to take effect with AI RMF safe harbor rebuttable presumption of reasonable care
- 11 Dec 2025: Trump signed EO on 'Ensuring a National Policy Framework for AI' proposing to preempt state AI laws inconsistent with federal deregulatory policy — if upheld, could affect state safe harbor provisions referencing NIST AI RMF
- As of Feb 2026: NIST revision of AI RMF per AI Action Plan directive is pending — timeline and scope of changes not yet publicly confirmed; sector regulators (FTC, FDA, SEC, EEOC, CFPB) increasingly reference AI RMF principles in their AI-related guidance

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** Highly relevant as the most practical and accessible AI governance framework for startups. KEY IMPLICATIONS: (1) ZERO COMPLIANCE COST BARRIER — unlike the EU AI Act or ISO 42001, the AI RMF is free, voluntary, and requires no certification fees, registration, or mandatory audits, making it ideal for early-stage startups; (2) LEGAL PROTECTION — adopting the AI RMF provides concrete legal benefits through safe harbor provisions in Texas (TRAIGA, effective Jan 2026) and Colorado (AI Act, effective Jun 2026), offering affirmative defense or rebuttable presumption of reasonable care for compliant organizations; (3) ENTERPRISE SALES ENABLER — Fortune 500 companies and federal agencies increasingly require AI vendors to demonstrate risk management practices; AI RMF compliance serves as a credibility signal that accelerates B2B sales cycles; (4) INTERNATIONAL COMPLIANCE BUILDING BLOCK — the crosswalk ecosystem means AI RMF adoption provides a foundation for EU AI Act and ISO 42001 compliance, reducing total governance overhead for startups planning international expansion; (5) PROGRESSIVE ADOPTION — startups can start with Govern and Map functions at near-zero cost, then scale to Measure and Manage as they grow and their AI systems mature; (6) GENERATIVE AI FOCUS — the GAI Profile (AI 600-1) provides specific, actionable risk management guidance for the most common startup AI use cases (LLM-based products and services); (7) COMPETITIVE MOAT — early adoption of structured AI governance creates a defensible competitive advantage, especially as more states adopt AI laws referencing the framework; (8) UNCERTAINTY RISK — the pending NIST revision (removing DEI, misinformation, climate references) per the Trump AI Action Plan creates some uncertainty about future framework content; startups should adopt current version while monitoring revisions; (9) FRENCH/EU CONTEXT — for Sorbonne M2 students, understanding the AI RMF is essential for any AI startup targeting US markets, and its crosswalk with the EU AI Act helps frame a coherent transatlantic compliance strategy.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary session for NIST AI RMF coverage as a voluntary governance framework in contrast to the EU AI Act's prescriptive approach. Also relevant to Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) for the Govern-Map-Measure-Manage risk management methodology, and Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) for understanding how AI governance frameworks affect market access and competitive positioning in US vs. EU markets.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official NIST publications (AI 100-1 and AI 600-1), NIST AI Resource Center documentation, White House executive orders and action plans, and analysis from major law firms (Davis Wright Tremaine, WilmerHale, Paul Hastings, Freshfields, Arnold & Porter), federal policy research organizations (Georgetown CSET, FAS, Brookings), and AI governance platforms (Credo AI, ModelOp, FairNow).

**Cross Reference:** NIST official website (nist.gov/itl/ai-risk-management-framework), NIST AI Resource Center (airc.nist.gov), NVD publications (nvlpubs.nist.gov), White House executive orders and AI Action Plan, US Department of Commerce press releases, TIME Magazine 100 Most Influential People in AI, Information Technology Industry Council (ITI) recommendations, Cloud Security Alliance guidance, Wharton School AI governance research, state legislature records for Texas TRAIGA, Colorado SB 24-205, and California AI transparency laws.

**Caveats:** Several areas of uncertainty: (1) The Trump AI Action Plan (July 2025) directs NIST to revise the AI RMF to remove references to misinformation, DEI, and climate change — the scope, timeline, and impact of these revisions are not yet publicly confirmed as of February 2026; (2) Biden's EO 14110 rescission (Jan 2025) removed the quasi-mandatory federal adoption requirement, and the framework's future federal role depends on Trump administration follow-through; (3) The December 2025 executive order on federal AI preemption of state laws could affect the legal value of state-level safe harbor provisions that reference the AI RMF, though this EO faces legal challenges; (4) The US AI Safety Institute's future scope and independence under the current administration is uncertain; (5) The framework's voluntary nature means adoption data is limited — no comprehensive surveys track the exact number of organizations implementing the AI RMF.

#### Uncertain Fields

- compliance_timeline
- deregulatory_signals
