### 24. OECD Recommendation of the Council on Artificial Intelligence (OECD AI Principles)

_Source: `OECD_AI_Principles_updated.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** OECD Recommendation of the Council on Artificial Intelligence (OECD AI Principles)

**Type:** international_agreement

**Jurisdiction:** Global (47 adherents including all 38 OECD members plus 9 non-member countries and the EU)

**Status:** amended

**Effective Date:** Originally adopted 22 May 2019; first revision 8 November 2023 (updated AI system definition); second revision 3 May 2024 (substantive principles update at OECD Ministerial Council Meeting)

**Regulatory Philosophy:** voluntary_principles

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The OECD AI Principles are the first intergovernmental standard on AI, providing a voluntary but highly influential framework for trustworthy AI governance. Composed of five values-based principles for AI actors and five policy recommendations for governments, they were adopted in 2019, became the basis for the G20 AI Principles, and were substantially updated in May 2024 to address generative AI, information integrity, environmental sustainability, and intellectual property challenges.

**Key Provisions:**

- SECTION 1 — Five Values-Based Principles for Responsible Stewardship of Trustworthy AI:
- Principle 1.1 — Inclusive Growth, Sustainable Development and Well-being: AI should benefit people and the planet by driving inclusive growth, advancing sustainable development, and enhancing well-being. The 2024 update added explicit reference to environmental sustainability.
- Principle 1.2 — Human-Centred Values and Fairness: AI actors should respect human rights, democratic values, diversity, fairness, and social justice. The 2024 update expanded this to include addressing misinformation and disinformation amplified by AI while respecting freedom of expression, and added provisions on bias.
- Principle 1.3 — Transparency and Explainability: AI actors should commit to transparency and responsible disclosure about AI systems, enabling people to understand AI-based outcomes and challenge them. The 2024 update clarified what information AI actors should provide regarding AI systems.
- Principle 1.4 — Robustness, Security and Safety: AI systems should function appropriately and not pose unreasonable safety risks. The 2024 update added provisions for mechanisms to override, repair, and/or decommission AI systems that risk causing undue harm or exhibit undesired behaviour, and added a provision on information integrity. Traceability and risk management provisions were relocated from this principle to Principle 1.5.
- Principle 1.5 — Accountability: AI actors should be accountable for the proper functioning of AI systems. The 2024 update incorporated traceability provisions (relocated from 1.4) and expanded risk management to emphasise cooperation between AI stakeholders and address specific risks including labour rights and intellectual property rights. Provisions now also address uses outside of intended purpose, intentional misuse, or unintentional misuse.
- SECTION 2 — Five Policy Recommendations for Governments:
- Recommendation 2.1 — Investing in AI Research and Development: Governments should consider long-term public investment and encourage private investment in R&D, open science, and open-source tools/datasets to spur innovation in trustworthy AI.
- Recommendation 2.2 — Fostering an Inclusive AI-Enabling Ecosystem: Governments should foster a digital ecosystem including data, computing infrastructure, and mechanisms for sharing AI knowledge and expertise.
- Recommendation 2.3 — Shaping an Enabling Interoperable Governance and Policy Environment: Governments should promote interoperable governance frameworks, building on the OECD's AI definitions adopted across jurisdictions (EU AI Act, US legislation, Council of Europe).
- Recommendation 2.4 — Building Human Capacity and Preparing for Labour Market Transformation: Governments should empower people with skills to adapt to AI-driven changes, support worker transitions, and ensure fair labour practices.
- Recommendation 2.5 — International Cooperation for Trustworthy AI: Governments should cooperate internationally to share information, develop interoperable governance approaches, and advance responsible AI. The 2024 merger of GPAI into OECD strengthens this dimension.

**Risk Classification:** The OECD AI Principles do not establish a formal risk classification system (unlike the EU AI Act's tiered risk approach). Instead, they apply horizontally across all AI systems and recommend proportionate risk management throughout the AI system lifecycle. The framework defines the AI system lifecycle phases: (i) design, data and models; (ii) verification and validation; (iii) deployment; (iv) operation and monitoring.

**Covered Entities:** All 'AI actors' broadly defined: those who play an active role in the AI system lifecycle, including organizations and individuals that develop, deploy, or operate AI systems. Applies to both public and private sector entities.

**Sector Scope:** Cross-sectoral and horizontal — applies to AI across all sectors and domains without exemptions. No sector-specific carve-outs.

**Open Source Treatment:** The OECD AI Principles do not contain specific provisions differentiating open-source AI from proprietary AI. The principles apply equally regardless of the AI system's licensing model. However, Recommendation 2.1 explicitly encourages government investment in 'open-source tools and open datasets that are representative and respect privacy and data protection to support an environment for AI research and development that is free of harmful bias and to improve interoperability and use of standards.' Open-source is thus framed positively as a tool for advancing trustworthy AI, but no reduced obligations or specific exemptions are provided.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** As a non-binding OECD Recommendation, the principles do not have formal extraterritorial reach in a legal sense. However, their practical influence is extraterritorial: with 47 adherents representing over 80% of global GDP, and adoption by the G20, the principles function as the de facto global baseline for AI governance. The OECD's AI definitions (AI system, AI system lifecycle) have been formally adopted in the EU AI Act, the US National AI Initiative Act, the Council of Europe AI Convention, and Japanese AI guidelines, creating indirect extraterritorial standardization.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** None. As a non-binding OECD Recommendation, the framework does not confer enforcement powers or give rise to any penalties for non-compliance. Adherents are expected to implement the principles through their own national legislation and policy frameworks, where penalties (if any) are determined at the national level.

**Enforcement Body:** No dedicated enforcement body. The OECD Secretariat (specifically the AI and Emerging Digital Technologies Division, which oversees the OECD.AI Policy Observatory) monitors implementation and publishes reviews, but does not enforce compliance. National implementation is left to each adherent's domestic authorities.

**Compliance Timeline:** No formal compliance deadlines (voluntary framework). Key milestones: original adoption May 2019; AI definition update November 2023; substantive principles revision May 2024; AI Policy Toolkit Self-Assessment Tool under development January 2025 to March 2026; ongoing implementation reviews through the OECD.AI Policy Observatory.

**Reporting Requirements:** No mandatory reporting. The OECD encourages voluntary self-reporting by adherents on their national AI policies through the OECD.AI Policy Observatory database, which currently tracks over 1,000 policy initiatives from 70+ countries. The OECD publishes national AI reviews (e.g., Germany and Egypt in 2024) and an AI Incidents Monitor. A 'common reporting framework for AI incidents' was proposed in an OECD policy paper published February 2025.

**Sme Provisions:** The Recommendation specifically calls for fostering an 'inclusive AI-enabling ecosystem' with attention to SMEs. Recommendation 2.2 encourages governments to support SMEs' access to data, computing resources, and AI expertise. The OECD has published research on AI adoption by SMEs (December 2025) and participates in the G7 SME AI Adoption Blueprint. However, no specific reduced documentation, fee waivers, or delayed timelines are mandated — these are left to national implementation. The OECD has also published work on regulatory sandboxes for AI that could benefit SMEs.

**Liability Model:** none_specified

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** For large enterprises: The OECD AI Principles serve as the foundational reference for corporate AI governance programs. Case studies with AXA, AWS, IBM, Meta, Microsoft, NEC, and PwC (through Business at OECD/BIAC) show major companies voluntarily aligning their AI ethics frameworks with the OECD principles. For SMEs and startups: The impact is primarily indirect — the principles shape national regulations that SMEs must comply with (e.g., EU AI Act using OECD AI definitions). The non-binding nature means no direct compliance costs, but the principles create normative expectations that investors, partners, and customers increasingly reference. VC investment in AI startups has increased nine-fold since 2019 (when the principles were adopted), suggesting the framework has not hindered investment. The OECD's emphasis on interoperable governance frameworks is specifically designed to reduce compliance fragmentation for international businesses. For European startups in particular, the OECD definitions embedded in the EU AI Act create a coherent conceptual framework that simplifies compliance mapping across jurisdictions.

**Innovation Effect:** Generally innovation-promoting. The principles explicitly balance trustworthiness with innovation: Recommendation 2.1 calls for public and private R&D investment, including in open-source AI. The framework's voluntary nature avoids imposing compliance burdens that could stifle experimentation. The emphasis on interoperable governance (Recommendation 2.3) is designed to reduce regulatory fragmentation that can hamper cross-border innovation. The OECD's work on regulatory sandboxes for AI provides concrete pro-innovation mechanisms. However, critics note that purely voluntary principles may be insufficient to prevent AI harms, creating a tension between the innovation-friendly approach and the need for enforceable protections.

**Global Influence:** The OECD AI Principles are arguably the most influential international AI governance framework. They serve as the foundation for: (1) G20 AI Principles (adopted June 2019); (2) EU AI Act's AI system definition and lifecycle framework; (3) US National AI Initiative Act definitions; (4) Council of Europe Framework Convention on AI; (5) Japan's AI governance guidelines. The OECD's AI definitions have become the global standard, adopted in legislation across multiple continents. The merger of GPAI into OECD in July 2024 further consolidated the OECD's central role in international AI governance, creating an integrated partnership of 44 countries across six continents. This represents a significant 'soft law to hard law' pipeline: OECD voluntary principles are progressively codified into binding national and regional legislation.

**Stakeholder Positions:** Supporters: Most OECD member governments, major tech companies (through Business at OECD/BIAC), and international organizations view the principles as a pragmatic foundation for interoperable AI governance. Industry generally appreciates the voluntary, flexible nature and the principles' role in harmonizing definitions globally. Critics: Civil society organizations and some academics argue the non-binding nature is insufficient to address AI risks, that industry self-regulation leads to 'ethics washing,' and that the principles lack teeth without enforcement mechanisms. The 'soft law' approach is seen by critics as allowing companies to claim alignment without meaningful accountability. Labour unions have pushed for stronger provisions on worker protections and have been partially addressed in the 2024 update (risk management now includes labour rights). Some developing countries note the OECD framework reflects priorities of wealthy nations, though the GPAI integration aims to increase developing country participation.

**Deregulatory Signals:** The OECD AI Principles themselves are not deregulatory — they are a voluntary framework that sits above national regulation. However, they can be characterized as embodying a 'regulate through principles, not prescriptions' philosophy that some jurisdictions use to justify lighter-touch approaches. The 2024 update did not weaken any provisions; it strengthened them (adding information integrity, environmental sustainability, IP protections). The OECD's emphasis on 'enabling governance' and 'fostering innovation' in Recommendations 2.1-2.3 can be read as signaling against overly prescriptive regulation. The framework's influence on Japan's and India's 'innovation-first' regulatory approaches shows how voluntary principles can anchor lighter regulatory regimes.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (uses OECD AI system definition; principles align with EU trustworthy AI framework)
- G20 AI Principles (directly derived from OECD AI Principles, adopted June 2019)
- Council of Europe Framework Convention on Artificial Intelligence (uses OECD definitions)
- US Executive Order 14110 on AI (Biden, 2023 — references OECD framework; rescinded by Trump EO 14148)
- US National AI Initiative Act (adopted OECD AI definitions)
- GDPR (OECD principles reinforce data protection concepts relevant to AI)
- Japan AI governance guidelines (aligned with OECD principles)
- UNESCO Recommendation on Ethics of AI (complementary global framework)

**Key Actors:**

- Mathias Cormann — OECD Secretary-General, key advocate for the 2024 update
- Karine Perset — Acting Head of OECD AI and Emerging Digital Technologies Division, oversees OECD.AI Policy Observatory and GPAI integration
- AIGO (AI Group of Experts at the OECD) — 50+ experts from government, industry, civil society, trade unions, academia who drafted original principles
- Business at OECD (BIAC) — Industry advisory body that launched implementation project in 2021 with AXA, AWS, IBM, Meta, Microsoft, NEC, PwC
- GPAI (Global Partnership on Artificial Intelligence) — Merged with OECD AI work in July 2024; 29 member countries expanding to welcome low/middle-income nations
- India — GPAI Chair in 2024, hosted the New Delhi Summit (July 3-4, 2024) where GPAI-OECD merger was announced
- OECD Committee on Digital Economy Policy (CDEP) — Lead committee overseeing the AI Principles

**Recent Developments:**

- May 3, 2024: OECD Council adopted substantive revision of AI Principles at Ministerial Council Meeting, addressing generative AI, information integrity, environmental sustainability, IP rights, and misuse prevention
- July 3-4, 2024: GPAI and OECD announced merger at New Delhi Summit, creating integrated partnership of 44 countries with expert support centres in Paris, Montreal, and Tokyo
- November 2023: Prior revision updated the OECD definition of AI system to address systems that evolve after deployment and generative AI content production
- February 28, 2025: OECD published 'Towards a common reporting framework for AI incidents' policy paper
- May 2, 2025: OECD published 'The Adoption of Artificial Intelligence in Firms' report analyzing AI uptake across G7 and Brazil
- January 2025 - March 2026: AI Policy Toolkit project underway — developing Self-Assessment Tool for countries to evaluate alignment with AI Principles, plus Implementation Guidance with practical examples
- June 3-4, 2025: OECD Council Ministerial Meeting in Paris discussed AI Policy Toolkit scoping note
- August 2025: OECD published further global AI governance framework analysis
- December 2025: OECD published research on 'AI adoption by small and medium-sized enterprises'
- Ongoing: OECD.AI Policy Observatory database now tracks over 1,000 AI policy initiatives from 70+ countries, with updated AI Incidents Monitor and country dashboards

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** The OECD AI Principles are essential context for AI entrepreneurs operating internationally. Key implications: (1) Definitional foundation — the OECD's AI system definition is embedded in the EU AI Act, US law, and other frameworks. Understanding this definition helps entrepreneurs determine whether their product falls under AI regulations across jurisdictions. (2) Compliance mapping — since multiple national regulations reference OECD principles, understanding the source framework helps map compliance requirements across markets more efficiently. (3) Investor signaling — demonstrating alignment with OECD AI Principles is increasingly expected by investors, particularly in Europe. The BIAC case studies (AXA, AWS, IBM) show how large companies implement the principles, setting expectations for startups in their supply chains. (4) Market access — adherence to OECD-aligned governance practices facilitates access to the 47 adherent countries' markets (80%+ of global GDP). (5) No direct compliance cost — unlike the EU AI Act, the principles themselves impose no compliance burden, but they create the normative expectations that hard law then codifies. (6) GPAI resources — the integrated GPAI partnership offers expert support centres and multistakeholder resources that startups can leverage. (7) Regulatory intelligence — the OECD.AI Policy Observatory is the best free resource for tracking AI regulation globally, essential for startups planning international expansion.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary mapping; also relevant to Session 3 (AI project management frameworks) for the governance and compliance aspects, and Session 4 (AI business models) for market access and regulatory landscape understanding

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High

**Cross Reference:**

- OECD official press release (May 2024): https://www.oecd.org/en/about/news/press-releases/2024/05/oecd-updates-ai-principles-to-stay-abreast-of-rapid-technological-developments.html
- OECD.AI Policy Observatory principles overview: https://oecd.ai/en/ai-principles

**Caveats:**

- As a non-binding Recommendation, the practical impact depends entirely on national implementation, which varies significantly across the 47 adherents
- The 2024 update text was not fully available in plain-text form during research — some details are synthesized from multiple secondary analyses rather than direct text quotes
- The GPAI-OECD merger is still in its early stages (announced July 2024); the full operational integration and its effects on the expert community are still unfolding
- The AI Policy Toolkit Self-Assessment Tool (January 2025 – March 2026) is still under development and its final form is not yet known
- The count of '47 adherents' was the figure at the time of the May 2024 update; this may have changed since
- Some developing countries and civil society organizations consider the OECD framework to be insufficiently representative of non-Western perspectives, despite the GPAI expansion

### 25. Responsible AI Frameworks (Industry) — Corporate RAI Programs from Microsoft, Google, Meta, Anthropic, OpenAI, and Amazon

_Source: `Responsible_AI_Frameworks_Industry.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** Responsible AI Frameworks (Industry) — Corporate RAI Programs from Microsoft, Google, Meta, Anthropic, OpenAI, and Amazon

**Type:** framework

**Jurisdiction:** Global (headquartered in US, but applied worldwide; increasingly shaped by EU AI Act compliance requirements)

**Status:** voluntary

**Effective Date:** Varies by company — Google AI Principles: June 2018; Microsoft Responsible AI Standard v2: June 2022 (transparency report series launched 2024); Anthropic RSP v1: September 2023, v2.2: May 2025; OpenAI Preparedness Framework v1: December 2023, v2: April 2025; Meta RAI team: 2019 (disbanded November 2023); Amazon AWS AI Service Cards: December 2022 (expanded 2024-2025); Seoul Frontier AI Safety Commitments: May 2024

**Regulatory Philosophy:** voluntary_principles

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** Major technology companies have developed internal Responsible AI (RAI) frameworks that govern how they build, deploy, and monitor AI systems. These range from principle-based governance structures (Microsoft, Google) to quantitative risk-tiering systems (Anthropic's AI Safety Levels, OpenAI's Preparedness Framework, Google DeepMind's Frontier Safety Framework). While voluntary, these frameworks are increasingly converging around common elements — red teaming, safety evaluations, model cards, and frontier capability thresholds — driven by the Seoul Frontier AI Safety Commitments (May 2024) and the Paris AI Action Summit (February 2025), and by the need to demonstrate EU AI Act compliance.

**Key Provisions:**

- MICROSOFT — Responsible AI Standard & Governance:
- Six core principles: Fairness, Reliability & Safety, Privacy & Security, Inclusiveness, Transparency, Accountability
- Office of Responsible AI (ORA) sets company-wide policy; AI, Ethics and Effects in Engineering and Research (Aether) committee provides cross-company advisory
- Responsible AI Standard v2 (2022) establishes mandatory product-level requirements mapped to the NIST AI RMF Govern-Map-Measure-Manage loop
- Frontier Governance Framework (new in 2025) adds specific protocols for frontier capabilities with national security or public safety implications
- 67 AI Red Team (AIRT) operations in 2024 covering every flagship Azure OpenAI model and all Phi releases
- 30 responsible AI tools with 155+ features released for customer use (Azure AI Content Safety, Responsible AI dashboard, etc.)
- 2025 Transparency Report: second annual report covering model development, customer tooling, and EU AI Act preparation
- GOOGLE — AI Principles & Frontier Safety Framework:
- Seven AI Principles (June 2018): Be socially beneficial, Avoid creating or reinforcing unfair bias, Be built and tested for safety, Be accountable to people, Incorporate privacy design principles, Uphold high standards of scientific excellence, Be made available for uses that accord with these principles
- Four application areas Google will not pursue: technologies causing overall harm, weapons, surveillance violating norms, technologies contravening international law/human rights
- Google DeepMind Frontier Safety Framework (FSF): identifies Critical Capability Levels (CCLs) across four risk domains — autonomy, biosecurity, cybersecurity, ML R&D — plus manipulation (added 2025)
- FSF v3.0 (late 2025) includes Recommendations for Heightened Security, Deployment Mitigations Procedure, and Deceptive Alignment Risk assessment
- Responsible Innovation team coordinates company-wide implementation; governance covers model development, application deployment, and post-launch monitoring
- 6th annual Responsible AI Progress Report (February 2025) aligned with NIST AI RMF structure
- META — Open Source Safety Ecosystem (post-RAI team restructure):
- Dedicated Responsible AI (RAI) team established 2019, disbanded November 2023 with staff redistributed to Generative AI and ML infrastructure teams
- Current approach centres on open-source safety tooling: LlamaFirewall (agent security guardrails), Llama Guard 4 (12B multimodal content moderation), Prompt Guard 2 (prompt injection detection), Code Shield (insecure code filtering), CyberSecEval 4 (cybersecurity benchmarks)
- System-level safety approach: Meta designs Llama models as components within broader systems, giving developers control over safety guardrails
- Model cards and system cards document intended use cases, limitations, and evaluation results for each Llama release
- Acceptable Use Policy governs downstream Llama usage under the community license
- Shift in 2025-2026: reports indicate Meta has moved toward closed-source for next-generation models (abandoning Llama 4 Behemoth open release), raising questions about future open-source governance commitments
- ANTHROPIC — Responsible Scaling Policy (RSP):
- AI Safety Levels (ASL) inspired by Biosafety Levels: ASL-1 (trivial risk) through ASL-4+ (catastrophic risk potential)
- RSP v2.2 (effective May 14, 2025): introduces proportional protection principle — safeguards scale with capability
- New capability thresholds trigger mandatory safeguard upgrades; refined safety case methodology for evaluation
- ASL-3 protections activated May 2025 for the first time, establishing precedent for escalated safety measures
- Responsible Scaling Officer: Jared Kaplan (Co-Founder & Chief Science Officer), succeeding Sam McCandlish
- Constitutional AI framework ensures model outputs align with defined principles through automated red-teaming
- Commitment not to train or deploy models unless adequate safeguards are implemented at the corresponding ASL
- OPENAI — Preparedness Framework:
- Preparedness Framework v2 (April 15, 2025): two capability thresholds — 'High' (amplifies existing harm pathways) and 'Critical' (introduces unprecedented harm pathways)
- Three tracked risk categories: Biological & Chemical capabilities, Cybersecurity capabilities, AI Self-improvement capabilities
- Safety Advisory Group (SAG): internal cross-functional body making safeguard recommendations; OpenAI Leadership approves/rejects; Board Safety and Security Committee provides oversight
- High-capability systems require deployment-level safeguards; Critical-capability systems require development-level safeguards before any further training
- Academic criticism (arXiv:2509.24394): affordance analysis argues the framework does not guarantee any specific AI risk mitigation practices — commitments are procedural, not outcome-based
- AMAZON/AWS — AI Service Cards & Responsible AI Program:
- Eight responsible AI dimensions: fairness, explainability, privacy & security, safety, controllability, veracity & robustness, governance, transparency
- AI Service Cards provide standardized documentation for each AWS AI service (intended use, limitations, design choices, performance optimization)
- Service Cards expanded throughout 2024-2025: Nova Reel, Nova Canvas, Nova Micro/Lite/Pro, Titan Image Generator, Titan Text Embeddings, Nova Act (December 2025)
- Enterprise-wide leadership review model for AI governance (less formalized than Microsoft or Google's dedicated RAI offices)
- CROSS-INDUSTRY — Seoul Frontier AI Safety Commitments (May 2024):
- 20 organizations signed voluntary commitments including Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta, Microsoft, OpenAI, NVIDIA, Samsung, Naver
- Key commitments: continuous risk assessment, acceptable risk thresholds, transparency, and a pledge not to deploy if unacceptable risks cannot be mitigated
- Required signatories to publish safety frameworks by the Paris AI Action Summit (February 2025)
- CROSS-INDUSTRY — Agentic AI Foundation (AAIF, 2025):
- Co-founded by OpenAI, Anthropic, and Block under the Linux Foundation, with support from Google, Microsoft, AWS, Bloomberg, Cloudflare
- Provides neutral stewardship for open, interoperable infrastructure for agentic AI production systems
- CROSS-INDUSTRY — ISO/IEC 42001:2023 Adoption:
- World's first certifiable AI Management System (AIMS) standard, applicable to all industries
- 76% of organizations plan to pursue ISO 42001 alignment (CSA 2025 benchmark)
- Notable certifications: KPMG US (November 2025), KPMG International (December 2025), Cornerstone Galaxy (December 2025)

**Risk Classification:** Each company uses a different risk-tiering system: (1) Anthropic's ASL levels (ASL-1 through ASL-4+) classify models by catastrophic risk potential, inspired by biosafety levels; (2) OpenAI's Preparedness Framework uses 'High' and 'Critical' capability thresholds across biological/chemical, cybersecurity, and AI self-improvement risks; (3) Google DeepMind's Frontier Safety Framework uses 'Critical Capability Levels' (CCLs) across autonomy, biosecurity, cybersecurity, ML R&D, and manipulation domains; (4) Microsoft's Frontier Governance Framework assesses frontier capabilities for national security or public safety risks; (5) Amazon/AWS does not publish a formal risk classification but uses service-level documentation. These frameworks are converging: all major labs now conduct pre-deployment evaluations, define capability thresholds, and commit to not deploying models exceeding thresholds without adequate safeguards.

**Covered Entities:** Each framework primarily governs the company's own AI development and deployment. Microsoft and AWS extend tooling and guidance to downstream customers (Azure AI users, AWS service users). Meta's open-source approach places safety responsibility on downstream developers using Llama, with the Acceptable Use Policy as the primary governance mechanism. Anthropic and OpenAI govern their own model development and API access. Seoul Commitments cover the 20 signatory organizations.

**Sector Scope:** Cross-sectoral — these frameworks apply to all AI products and services each company offers, from cloud AI services to consumer products. Microsoft covers Azure OpenAI, Copilot, and Phi models. Google covers Gemini, Google Cloud AI, and all product integrations. Meta covers Llama models and Meta AI products. Anthropic covers Claude. OpenAI covers GPT models and ChatGPT. Amazon covers all Nova and Titan models plus Bedrock services.

**Open Source Treatment:** The industry is sharply divided on open source and safety: (1) Meta has been the most prominent open-source advocate, releasing Llama models with community licenses and open-source safety tools (LlamaFirewall, Llama Guard, Code Shield), but reports in 2025-2026 suggest a potential pivot toward closed-source for frontier models; (2) Anthropic and OpenAI maintain closed-source model weights but publish safety research and frameworks; (3) Google releases some models as open-weight (Gemma family) while keeping Gemini closed; (4) Microsoft supports open models through Phi releases while also deploying OpenAI models through Azure; (5) The Agentic AI Foundation (AAIF) under Linux Foundation represents industry convergence on open infrastructure for agentic systems. ISO 42001 applies regardless of open/closed source status.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** These frameworks technically apply only to each company's own operations, but their practical reach is global: (1) Azure AI and AWS serve customers in 60+ countries, making Microsoft's and Amazon's responsible AI tools and policies globally applicable; (2) Llama's open-source release means Meta's safety tooling and acceptable use policies propagate to any developer worldwide; (3) The Seoul Commitments explicitly cross borders, with signatories from North America, Europe, Asia, and the Middle East; (4) EU AI Act compliance requirements are forcing all major US tech companies to implement governance structures that meet European standards globally (Microsoft's 2025 report explicitly addresses EU AI Act preparations). The 'Brussels Effect' means these corporate frameworks increasingly converge with EU regulatory requirements.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** No direct penalties from the frameworks themselves — they are voluntary corporate governance structures. However, indirect penalty exposure is significant: (1) EU AI Act violations can reach EUR 35 million or 7% of global annual turnover; companies are building their RAI frameworks to demonstrate EU compliance; (2) FTC enforcement actions in the US have targeted deceptive AI practices; (3) Reputational penalties are substantial — Meta's disbanding of its RAI team drew significant public criticism; (4) The Seoul Commitments carry no formal penalties but create peer pressure and public accountability; (5) Investors increasingly factor RAI governance into valuations and ESG scoring.

**Enforcement Body:** Internal governance bodies vary by company: Microsoft — Office of Responsible AI (ORA) + Aether Committee; Google — Responsible Innovation team + Advanced Technology Review Council; Anthropic — Responsible Scaling Officer (Jared Kaplan); OpenAI — Safety Advisory Group (SAG) + Board Safety & Security Committee; Amazon — Enterprise leadership review. External oversight: Seoul Summit commitments are monitored by METR (Model Evaluation and Threat Research); ISO 42001 is audited by accredited certification bodies (DNV, BSI, NSF). No government body enforces these voluntary frameworks directly, though the EU AI Office will enforce EU AI Act compliance.

**Compliance Timeline:** Key milestones: Google AI Principles published June 2018 (earliest major framework); Microsoft RAI Standard v2: June 2022; Anthropic RSP v1: September 2023; OpenAI Preparedness Framework v1: December 2023; Seoul Frontier AI Safety Commitments: May 2024 (required safety framework publication by February 2025 Paris Summit); Anthropic RSP v2.2: May 14, 2025 (ASL-3 activation same month); OpenAI Preparedness Framework v2: April 15, 2025; Google DeepMind FSF v3.0: late 2025; ISO 42001 certifications: KPMG US November 2025, KPMG International December 2025. EU AI Act compliance deadlines are driving accelerated implementation across all companies through 2025-2027.

**Reporting Requirements:** Varies significantly by company: (1) Microsoft: publishes annual Responsible AI Transparency Report (2024 inaugural, 2025 second edition) — the most comprehensive public reporting in the industry; (2) Google: publishes annual Responsible AI Progress Report (6th edition February 2025); (3) Anthropic: publishes RSP updates and safety case reports (ASL-3 activation report May 2025); (4) OpenAI: publishes framework updates and system cards for major model releases; (5) Meta: publishes model cards and system cards for Llama releases but no consolidated annual RAI report; (6) Amazon: AI Service Cards per service but no consolidated annual report. Seoul Commitments require publication of safety frameworks. ISO 42001 requires documented AI Management System with regular internal audits.

**Sme Provisions:** These industry frameworks are designed primarily for the companies themselves and their enterprise customers, with limited direct SME provisions. However: (1) Microsoft's 30 responsible AI tools (155+ features) are available to Azure customers including SMEs; (2) Meta's open-source safety tools (LlamaFirewall, Llama Guard) are freely available, significantly reducing safety implementation costs for startups; (3) AWS AI Service Cards provide free transparency documentation that SMEs can reference; (4) ISO 42001 certification costs may be prohibitive for early-stage startups (typically EUR 15,000-50,000+ for audit and certification), though the standard is applicable to organizations of all sizes; (5) The Agentic AI Foundation aims to provide open infrastructure that benefits smaller players. There are no specific fee waivers, delayed timelines, or reduced documentation requirements for SMEs in any of these frameworks.

**Liability Model:** none_specified

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** For large enterprises: These frameworks establish the governance infrastructure that enterprise AI buyers increasingly demand. Microsoft's and AWS's responsible AI tooling creates competitive moats — customers building on Azure or AWS benefit from built-in compliance tooling. ISO 42001 certification is becoming a procurement requirement for enterprise AI vendors. For SMEs and startups: The impact is multi-layered: (1) Startups building on major cloud platforms (Azure, AWS, Google Cloud) inherit some responsible AI governance through platform tools, reducing their standalone compliance burden; (2) Startups using Llama benefit from Meta's open-source safety tools but must implement their own governance around the Acceptable Use Policy; (3) Startups competing with or selling to large enterprises must demonstrate RAI governance — 93% of organizations acknowledge AI brings risks but only 9% feel prepared, creating market opportunity for RAI-focused startups; (4) Compliance cost estimates for AI governance range from $50K-500K+ annually for mid-size companies, with large enterprises spending millions; (5) Only 29% of organizations have comprehensive AI governance plans, indicating a large addressable market for governance tooling. For the French/European ecosystem specifically: EU AI Act compliance requirements make these industry frameworks immediately relevant — startups must understand how the governance practices of their cloud providers (Microsoft, Google, Amazon) intersect with their own EU AI Act obligations.

**Innovation Effect:** Mixed effects on innovation: (1) Pro-innovation: Open-source safety tools (Meta's LlamaFirewall, Llama Guard) democratize safety implementation, enabling startups to build responsible AI without massive internal safety teams; Microsoft's and AWS's compliance tooling reduces friction for responsible AI development; the AAIF's open infrastructure approach promotes innovation in agentic AI; (2) Potentially constraining: Anthropic's ASL system and OpenAI's capability thresholds can slow model deployment timelines — ASL-3 activation required additional security and safety measures before deployment; red teaming at Microsoft's scale (67 operations) is resource-intensive; ISO 42001 certification imposes process overhead; (3) Net assessment: The frameworks appear to channel innovation rather than block it — companies that invest heavily in RAI (Microsoft, Anthropic) are also among the most commercially successful AI developers. The convergence around safety evaluations creates a shared evaluation infrastructure that benefits the entire ecosystem. Academic criticism (arXiv:2509.24394) notes that frameworks like OpenAI's Preparedness Framework are procedural rather than outcome-based, potentially allowing innovation to proceed even when risks are not fully mitigated.

**Global Influence:** Significant 'corporate standard-setting' effect: (1) Microsoft's Responsible AI Standard and transparency reporting set the benchmark for corporate RAI governance globally — other companies benchmark against it; (2) Google's early publication of AI Principles (2018) established the norm that major AI companies should publish principles; (3) The Seoul Commitments created the first cross-company, cross-border voluntary safety framework, with signatories from North America, Asia, Europe, and the Middle East; (4) The risk-tiering approaches (Anthropic ASL, OpenAI thresholds, DeepMind CCLs) are influencing how regulators conceptualize AI risk — the EU AI Act's risk-based approach mirrors industry practice; (5) Meta's open-source safety tools set global norms for what safety tooling should accompany model releases; (6) ISO 42001 is creating a universal certification standard that transcends individual company frameworks; (7) The Paris AI Action Summit (February 2025) saw EUR 109 billion in private-sector commitments, demonstrating industry's influence on global AI governance. These corporate frameworks collectively shape what 'responsible AI' means in practice — potentially more influential than government regulation in the near term.

**Stakeholder Positions:** Industry: Major tech companies publicly champion responsible AI governance but face scrutiny over implementation. Microsoft is generally viewed as the most mature and transparent (annual reports, extensive tooling). Anthropic positions itself as 'safety-first' with the most quantitative risk framework (ASL levels). OpenAI faces criticism for gap between safety rhetoric and practice (departure of safety team members in 2024, dissolution of the Superalignment team). Meta's disbanding of its RAI team in November 2023 was widely criticized as prioritizing speed-to-market over safety, though Meta argues safety is now embedded across teams rather than siloed. Civil society: Organizations like the AI Now Institute, Access Now, and the Partnership on AI argue that voluntary corporate frameworks are insufficient and that mandatory regulation is needed. The lack of external enforcement, audit, or accountability mechanisms is a persistent critique. Critics note that companies can publish principles without meaningful implementation. Academic community: Researchers provide both supportive analysis (safety evaluation benchmarks, red teaming methodologies) and criticism (arXiv:2509.24394 analyzing OpenAI framework gaps). The Midas Project criticized Anthropic's RSP for not going far enough. Governments: Regulators are cautiously supportive of voluntary frameworks but moving toward mandatory requirements — the EU AI Act, UK AISI, and US state laws all indicate governments view voluntary corporate action as insufficient on its own. The Seoul/Paris summit process tries to bridge the gap between voluntary industry commitments and government expectations.

**Deregulatory Signals:** The US federal government under the Trump administration (from January 2025) has shifted decisively toward deregulation of AI, rescinding Biden's Executive Order 14110 and signaling that industry self-governance is preferred over prescriptive regulation. This has elevated the importance of voluntary corporate RAI frameworks — if government is not going to regulate, industry frameworks become the primary governance mechanism. However, this creates tensions: (1) Companies like Microsoft and Google are simultaneously preparing for EU AI Act compliance (prescriptive) and operating in a deregulated US environment; (2) The Seoul Commitments demonstrate that industry recognizes the need for some governance structure even absent regulation; (3) State-level AI legislation in the US (Colorado, Illinois, California) is filling the federal vacuum, creating a patchwork that may push companies toward corporate-wide standards anyway; (4) The AAIF under the Linux Foundation represents industry self-organizing around governance for agentic AI, which could either complement or substitute for government regulation. The net effect is that corporate RAI frameworks are becoming more, not less, important as the regulatory landscape fragments.

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** Understanding industry RAI frameworks is critical for AI entrepreneurs at multiple levels: (1) Platform selection: Startups building on Azure, AWS, or Google Cloud inherit different RAI tooling — Microsoft offers the most comprehensive compliance-ready toolkit (30 tools, 155+ features), AWS provides AI Service Cards for documentation, Google offers responsible AI practices and model evaluation tools. Platform choice affects compliance costs. (2) Open-source leverage: Meta's open-source safety tools (LlamaFirewall, Llama Guard, Prompt Guard, Code Shield) allow startups to implement production-grade safety without building from scratch — this is a massive cost advantage. A startup using Llama + LlamaFirewall can achieve safety tooling comparable to large companies at near-zero software cost. (3) Investor expectations: VCs and corporate investors increasingly expect demonstrable RAI governance. ISO 42001 certification or alignment with NIST AI RMF can serve as credibility markers. In fundraising, being able to articulate how your company manages AI risk (referencing industry frameworks) differentiates serious operators from hobbyists. (4) B2B sales: Enterprise customers (especially in regulated sectors — finance, healthcare, public sector) require AI vendors to demonstrate responsible AI practices. Aligning with Microsoft's or Google's framework standards makes it easier to pass procurement reviews. (5) EU market access: For startups targeting the EU market, understanding how major tech companies are preparing for EU AI Act compliance provides a roadmap — Microsoft's 2025 transparency report explicitly details their EU AI Act preparation across prohibited practices, model policies, and customer documentation. (6) Competitive moat: Startups that build RAI governance early create competitive advantages — 71% of organizations lack comprehensive AI governance plans, so having one differentiates you. (7) Talent attraction: The Meta RAI team disbanding displaced experienced responsible AI professionals into the market — an opportunity for startups to hire senior RAI talent. (8) Risk management: The academic critique of OpenAI's framework (procedural vs. outcome-based) is a cautionary tale — startups should ensure their governance produces real safety outcomes, not just paperwork. (9) Certification strategy: ISO 42001 certification costs EUR 15,000-50,000+ but increasingly opens doors; startups should factor this into their compliance budget for 2025-2027.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary mapping for governance structures and ethical frameworks; also relevant to Session 3 (AI project management frameworks) for the NIST AI RMF alignment and build-vs-buy decisions around safety tooling; and Session 4 (AI business models) for understanding how RAI governance creates competitive moats and affects platform economics

**Caveats:**

- Corporate RAI frameworks are self-reported and self-enforced — there is no independent external audit of whether companies actually follow their own frameworks (except ISO 42001 certified programs)
- The gap between published principles and actual practice is difficult to assess from public information alone — high-profile departures from OpenAI's safety team in 2024 suggest internal tensions not captured in public documents
- Meta's RAI approach is in rapid flux: the dedicated team was disbanded (November 2023), open-source safety tools continue to be released, but reports suggest a potential pivot to closed-source for frontier models — the long-term direction is uncertain
- The Seoul Frontier AI Safety Commitments are voluntary with no enforcement mechanism — METR monitors compliance but has no power to sanction non-compliance
- Academic critique of framework effectiveness (arXiv:2509.24394) suggests these frameworks may provide 'safety theater' rather than meaningful risk mitigation — this is an active debate in the research community
- Framework versions evolve rapidly — by the time this research is used in teaching (2026), newer versions may have been published
- The deregulatory shift in the US (Trump administration from January 2025) creates uncertainty about whether voluntary corporate frameworks will face future mandatory requirements or remain the primary governance mechanism
- ISO 42001 adoption data (76% plan to pursue) is from a CSA survey that may overrepresent compliance-conscious organizations; actual adoption rates across the broader market may be lower

### 26. Trump AI Executive Orders 2025 (EO 14179 'Removing Barriers to American Leadership in Artificial Intelligence', EO 14319 'Preventing Woke AI in the Federal Government', and EO 'Ensuring a National Policy Framework for Artificial Intelligence')

_Source: `Trump_AI_Executive_Orders_2025.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** Trump AI Executive Orders 2025 (EO 14179 'Removing Barriers to American Leadership in Artificial Intelligence', EO 14319 'Preventing Woke AI in the Federal Government', and EO 'Ensuring a National Policy Framework for Artificial Intelligence')

**Type:** executive_order

**Jurisdiction:** US (federal)

**Status:** in_force

**Effective Date:** EO 14179 signed 23 January 2025 (Biden EO 14110 rescinded 20 January 2025); AI Action Plan released 23 July 2025; EO 14319 'Preventing Woke AI' signed 23 July 2025; EO on National AI Policy Framework signed 11 December 2025

**Regulatory Philosophy:** innovation_first_light_touch

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** A series of executive orders constituting the Trump administration's AI policy, centred on the principle that the US must 'sustain and enhance America's global AI dominance.' The January 2025 EO rescinded Biden's EO 14110 (which required safety testing, red-teaming, and equity-focused AI governance) and directed agencies to develop a pro-innovation AI Action Plan within 180 days. Subsequent orders in July 2025 targeted 'ideological bias' in government-procured AI and in December 2025 sought to preempt state-level AI regulation through federal enforcement mechanisms.

**Key Provisions:**

- Rescission of Biden's EO 14110 on 20 January 2025 via 'Initial Rescissions of Harmful Executive Orders and Actions', eliminating mandatory red-team safety tests for foundation models, equity and civil rights safeguards, and federal AI best practices frameworks
- EO 14179 (23 Jan 2025): Directs development of a federal AI Action Plan within 180 days to sustain US global AI dominance, focusing on human flourishing, economic competitiveness, and national security
- EO 14179: Mandates review, suspension, or rescission of all prior government actions from EO 14110 that may be inconsistent with the new deregulatory policy
- EO 14179: Promotes AI development 'free from ideological bias or social agendas' as official US policy
- AI Action Plan ('Winning the Race', July 2025): 90 federal policy positions across three pillars — Accelerating Innovation, Building American AI Infrastructure, and Leading in International Diplomacy and Security
- AI Action Plan: Directs NIST to revise its AI Risk Management Framework to 'eliminate references to misinformation, Diversity Equity and Inclusion, and climate change'
- AI Action Plan: Fast-tracks construction and permitting of data centres and energy infrastructure for AI development, replacing Biden's EO 14141
- AI Action Plan: Establishes full-stack AI export package programme within 90 days, covering hardware, data pipelines, AI models, security measures, and applications
- EO 14319 'Preventing Woke AI' (23 Jul 2025): Establishes two 'Unbiased AI Principles' for government procurement — Truth-Seeking (LLMs must prioritise historical accuracy, scientific inquiry, objectivity) and Ideological Neutrality (LLMs must not encode partisan or DEI-aligned judgments unless user-prompted)
- EO 14319: Directs OMB to issue guidance within 120 days on implementing Unbiased AI Principles in federal procurement, requiring contractors to disclose how AI models are built, trained, or modified
- EO 'National AI Policy Framework' (11 Dec 2025): Establishes federal policy for a 'minimally burdensome national policy framework for AI' and directs DOJ to create an AI Litigation Task Force to challenge state AI laws
- December 2025 EO: Secretary of Commerce must publish evaluation of state AI laws conflicting with federal policy within 90 days and make non-compliant states ineligible for federal grants (including BEAD broadband funds)
- December 2025 EO: Directs FCC and FTC proceedings to establish federal reporting standards and policy statements on AI that preempt conflicting state laws
- December 2025 EO: Preserves state authority for children's safety, AI compute and data centre infrastructure, and state government procurement/use of AI

**Risk Classification:** No formal risk classification system. The Trump executive orders deliberately reject the Biden administration's risk-based approach to AI governance. Instead, they establish a general posture of minimal regulation, intervening only where national security or specific federal interests are at stake. The AI Action Plan references protecting against 'misuse, theft, or other risks posed by malicious actors' but does not define risk tiers or assessment frameworks.

**Covered Entities:** Federal agencies (must comply with rescission directives and review prior actions); AI companies contracting with the federal government (must comply with 'Unbiased AI Principles' for procurement under EO 14319); state governments (subject to federal preemption challenges under December 2025 EO); indirectly, all US AI developers and deployers benefit from the deregulatory environment

**Sector Scope:** Horizontal across all sectors at the federal level, with explicit focus on: federal government AI procurement, energy and data centre infrastructure, national security and defence AI, export controls for AI technology stacks. The December 2025 EO exempts children's safety, AI compute infrastructure, and state government procurement from federal preemption.

**Open Source Treatment:** The AI Action Plan explicitly supports open-source AI development, stating that 'open-source and open-weight models could become global standards in some areas of business and in academic research worldwide.' The plan recommends ensuring access to large-scale computing resources for open-source AI development. It frames open-source AI as geostrategically important, noting the need to counter China's leadership in open-source AI (DeepSeek, Qwen) by promoting American open-source alternatives. No specific regulatory burdens or obligations are imposed on open-source AI models, in stark contrast to the EU AI Act's GPAI threshold approach.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** Primarily domestic in legal scope, but with significant extraterritorial implications. The full-stack AI export package programme explicitly aims to shape AI adoption by US allies. The December 2025 EO's preemption of state laws effectively creates a single US market standard that multinational companies operating in the US must navigate. The refusal to join international AI safety declarations (e.g., Paris summit, February 2025) signals unwillingness to adopt extraterritorial governance norms, while the export package programme projects US AI standards outward to allied nations.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** No direct civil or criminal penalties established by the executive orders themselves. Enforcement mechanisms are indirect: (1) Federal procurement exclusion — AI companies not complying with 'Unbiased AI Principles' risk losing government contracts; (2) Federal grant ineligibility — states with AI laws conflicting with federal policy may lose access to federal funds including BEAD broadband deployment grants; (3) DOJ litigation — the AI Litigation Task Force can bring legal challenges against state AI laws on constitutional grounds (interstate commerce, federal preemption).

**Enforcement Body:** Multiple federal agencies: White House Office of Science and Technology Policy (OSTP) coordinated the AI Action Plan; Office of Management and Budget (OMB) issues procurement guidance; Department of Justice AI Litigation Task Force (established under Dec 2025 EO) challenges state laws; Federal Trade Commission and Federal Communications Commission directed to issue preemptive standards; Department of Commerce evaluates state AI law conflicts; Special Advisor for AI and Crypto oversees implementation

**Compliance Timeline:** 20 Jan 2025: Biden EO 14110 rescinded immediately; 23 Jan 2025: EO 14179 signed, agencies begin reviewing prior actions; 23 Jul 2025: AI Action Plan released alongside EO 14319 on 'Woke AI'; 21 Oct 2025: 90-day deadline for Commerce to establish full-stack AI export programme; Nov 2025: 120-day deadline for OMB guidance on Unbiased AI Principles; 11 Dec 2025: National AI Policy Framework EO signed; Dec 2025: OMB issues procurement guidance on 'Woke AI' EO; Mar 2026 (approx): 90-day deadline for Commerce to evaluate state AI laws; ongoing DOJ AI Litigation Task Force challenges to state laws

**Reporting Requirements:** Under EO 14319 ('Preventing Woke AI'), federal contractors must provide federal agency customers with information on how AI models are built, trained, or modified — a transparency/disclosure requirement rather than a regulatory reporting obligation. The December 2025 EO directs the FCC to initiate proceedings to determine whether to adopt a federal reporting and disclosure standard for AI models. No mandatory red-teaming, safety testing, or incident reporting requirements — these were eliminated with the rescission of Biden's EO 14110.

**Sme Provisions:** The Trump AI policy is broadly framed as beneficial to smaller companies by reducing regulatory burden. The AI Action Plan explicitly notes that open-source models 'level the playing field between small/medium-sized organisations and large companies.' The December 2025 EO argues that state-level regulation creates 'a patchwork of 50 different regulatory regimes' making compliance disproportionately burdensome for startups. However, no specific SME-targeted provisions (such as reduced fees, sandbox access, or simplified procedures) are established in the executive orders themselves.

**Liability Model:** none_specified

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** LARGE ENTERPRISES: Major tech companies (OpenAI, Google, Microsoft, Anthropic, xAI) are the primary beneficiaries, gaining reduced federal oversight, accelerated infrastructure permitting, and federal preemption of state regulations they lobbied against. OpenAI submitted a five-point strategy to the AI Action Plan emphasising regulatory preemption, export controls, copyright protections, and infrastructure investment. xAI received a Defence Department contract worth up to $200 million. STARTUPS AND SMEs: Mixed impact — the deregulatory stance reduces compliance costs relative to the Biden framework, but the December 2025 EO creates legal uncertainty as DOJ challenges state laws, potentially leaving companies in limbo between federal and state requirements. TechCrunch reported that 'startups may get legal limbo instead' of the promised single rulebook. The 'Woke AI' procurement requirements introduce new compliance costs for companies seeking government contracts. OVERALL: Critics argue the policy is 'a handout to already-entrenched, powerful tech companies' rather than a genuine innovation stimulus, as the removal of safeguards primarily benefits incumbents with existing market power.

**Innovation Effect:** PROMOTING: Removal of mandatory safety testing and red-teaming requirements reduces time-to-market; fast-tracked data centre and energy infrastructure permits address a key bottleneck for AI scaling; open-source AI support counters Chinese competition; export programme opens international markets. CONSTRAINING: NIST framework revision to remove references to misinformation and bias may reduce trust in AI systems; legal uncertainty from state preemption battles may chill investment in states with existing frameworks; the 'Woke AI' procurement requirements impose subjective compliance standards that are 'politically and emotionally charged but legally vague' (Brookings); removal of safety standards may lead to incidents that trigger reactive, more restrictive regulation later. Stanford HAI and Brookings analyses suggest the deregulatory approach may produce short-term acceleration at the cost of long-term trust and safety infrastructure.

**Global Influence:** Significant divergence from other major jurisdictions. At the February 2025 Paris AI Summit, the US declined to join the EU-backed 'Inclusive and Sustainable AI' declaration. The UK signed a 'Technology Prosperity Deal' with the US in September 2025 — the export programme creates a hub-and-spoke model for allied AI adoption. The AI Action Plan explicitly targets China competition, framing open-source and export packages as strategic tools. IMPACT ON EU: The US deregulatory stance has created pressure on the EU, contributing to the Commission's Digital Omnibus proposal to simplify the AI Act — the EU 'engaging with Trump administration on potential adjustments.' However, the EU AI Act retains its fundamental architecture. Chatham House analysis: Trump's plan 'seeks customers, not partners', promoting US technology stacks rather than collaborative governance. CEPA found that the AI Act is 'inspiring few copycats,' with only Canada and Brazil drafting similar frameworks — the US deregulatory counter-model may be contributing to this limited diffusion.

**Stakeholder Positions:** SUPPORTERS: Big Tech companies broadly welcomed deregulation — OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, and xAI have all received government contracts or engaged positively with the administration's framework. The tech industry supported state law preemption, arguing a patchwork of 50 regulatory regimes would stifle innovation. CRITICS: Brookings Institution published analysis that the orders 'politicise AI', particularly the NIST framework revision and 'Woke AI' mandates. Brennan Center for Justice warned the policy 'could compromise the technology' by replacing safety standards with political criteria. In These Times characterised it as benefiting OpenAI and incumbent tech companies. 17 Republican governors publicly opposed the December 2025 state preemption EO. Congress overwhelmingly rejected the preemption approach — it was removed from the National Defence Authorisation Act in a 99-1 Senate vote. State attorneys general (December 2025) warned Microsoft, OpenAI, and Google to fix 'delusional' AI outputs, asserting state enforcement authority. Northeastern University AI experts noted that 'when a state does a restriction, there's usually a good reason behind it.' MIT Technology Review described 'America's coming war over AI regulation' between federal and state authorities.

**Deregulatory Signals:** This is the most aggressive AI deregulatory agenda of any major jurisdiction. SPECIFIC MECHANISMS: (1) Outright rescission of Biden's EO 14110 with its safety testing, red-teaming, and equity mandates on Day 1; (2) Directive to agencies to suspend, revise, or rescind all prior AI actions inconsistent with the new deregulatory posture; (3) NIST framework revision removing bias, misinformation, and climate references; (4) Active federal litigation programme (DOJ AI Task Force) to strike down state AI laws; (5) Federal funding conditionality tied to absence of 'onerous' state AI regulations; (6) FTC and FCC proceedings designed to establish preemptive federal standards; (7) Fast-tracked infrastructure permitting. The approach goes beyond mere deregulation into active anti-regulation, using federal enforcement power to prevent other levels of government from regulating AI.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- Biden EO 14110 on 'Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy AI' (30 Oct 2023) — rescinded on 20 Jan 2025, was the primary predecessor and policy target
- Biden EO 14141 on 'Advancing United States Leadership in Artificial Intelligence Infrastructure' — revoked and replaced by July 2025 AI infrastructure EO
- EU AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689) — primary international counterpoint, represents the comprehensive horizontal approach that the Trump orders explicitly reject
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework — directed to be revised to remove references to misinformation, DEI, and climate change
- State AI laws (California SB-1047 vetoed; Colorado AI Act; other state proposals) — primary domestic targets of the December 2025 preemption EO
- OECD AI Principles — US participation under question given divergence from multilateral governance
- Paris AI Summit Declaration (Feb 2025) — US declined to sign the 'Inclusive and Sustainable AI' declaration
- UK-US Technology Prosperity Deal (Sep 2025) — bilateral arrangement aligned with export package programme

**Key Actors:**

- President Donald Trump — signed all executive orders, framed AI as a national competitiveness and 'dominance' issue
- White House OSTP (Office of Science and Technology Policy) — coordinated the AI Action Plan, solicited industry input via RFI
- Special Advisor for AI and Crypto — oversees implementation of AI policy across government, directed to develop legislative recommendations
- David Sacks — Silicon Valley venture capitalist appointed as White House AI and Crypto Czar
- Office of Management and Budget (OMB) — issued guidance on 'Woke AI' procurement requirements (Dec 2025)
- DOJ AI Litigation Task Force — established under Dec 2025 EO to challenge state AI laws
- OpenAI — submitted five-point strategy to the AI Action Plan process, major beneficiary of deregulation
- xAI (Elon Musk) — received $200M Defence Department contract, Musk's advisory role raised conflict-of-interest concerns
- Google, Anthropic, Microsoft — major AI companies engaged with the administration's framework
- 17 Republican governors — publicly opposed the December 2025 state preemption executive order
- US Senate (99-1 vote) — rejected AI preemption provision in National Defence Authorisation Act
- State attorneys general — warned AI companies about 'delusional' outputs, asserting state enforcement authority

**Recent Developments:**

- 20 Jan 2025: Biden's EO 14110 rescinded on Trump's first day in office
- 23 Jan 2025: EO 14179 signed, establishing the 'removing barriers' and 'AI dominance' policy framework
- Feb 2025: US declined to sign EU-backed 'Inclusive and Sustainable AI' declaration at Paris AI Summit
- Apr 2025: AI education initiatives launched for American youth; Section 232 actions on critical minerals to secure AI supply chains
- 23 Jul 2025: Comprehensive AI Action Plan ('Winning the Race') released alongside EO 14319 on 'Preventing Woke AI' and data centre infrastructure EO
- Sep 2025: UK-US Technology Prosperity Deal signed
- Nov 2025: Genesis Mission launched to accelerate scientific discovery using AI with integrated federal resources
- Dec 2025: OMB released procurement guidance implementing 'Woke AI' EO, requiring contractor transparency on model training
- 10 Dec 2025: State attorneys general issued warnings to Microsoft, OpenAI, Google about AI output accuracy
- 11 Dec 2025: EO on 'Ensuring a National Policy Framework for AI' signed, targeting state AI laws through DOJ litigation task force, FTC/FCC preemption proceedings, and federal funding conditionality
- Dec 2025: Congress killed NDAA provision that would have stopped states from enforcing their own AI laws (99-1 Senate vote)
- Jan 2026: MIT Technology Review published analysis on 'America's coming war over AI regulation' between federal and state levels
- Ongoing: DOJ AI Litigation Task Force preparing legal challenges against state AI laws; Commerce Department evaluating state AI law conflicts (90-day deadline from Dec 2025 EO)

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** CRITICAL CASE STUDY FOR COMPARING REGULATORY PHILOSOPHIES: The Trump AI EOs represent the polar opposite of the EU AI Act approach, making them ideal for a comparative analysis in class. KEY POINTS FOR ENTREPRENEURS: (1) Regulatory arbitrage — US-based AI startups face near-zero federal compliance costs while EU-based startups face EUR 160K-330K per high-risk system; however, US startups wanting to sell in the EU must still comply with the AI Act; (2) State-level uncertainty — despite federal deregulation, companies operating across multiple US states face a fragmented landscape until the DOJ Task Force and courts resolve preemption battles (TechCrunch called this 'legal limbo'); (3) Government procurement market — the 'Woke AI' EO creates new requirements for federal contractors (transparency on training, ideological neutrality), an interesting example of how deregulation in one area can coexist with new requirements in another; (4) Open-source strategy — the US policy actively promotes open-source AI as a geopolitical tool against Chinese models (DeepSeek, Qwen), which is relevant for startups deciding between open-source and proprietary strategies; (5) Infrastructure investment — fast-tracked data centre permitting benefits capital-intensive AI companies but requires significant upfront investment; (6) Export opportunities — the full-stack AI export package programme creates market access to allied nations but also locks them into US technology stacks. DISCUSSION QUESTIONS: 'If you were launching an AI startup, would you incorporate in the US or the EU — and why?' and 'Does removing safety requirements make it easier or harder to build trust with enterprise customers?'

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary; also relevant to Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) for the regulatory arbitrage and market access dimensions, and Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) for the Build vs Buy implications of open-source US policy

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official White House executive order texts, Federal Register publications, law firm analyses (Sidley Austin, Skadden, Paul Hastings, Mayer Brown, DLA Piper), policy think tanks (Brookings, CSET Georgetown, Stanford HAI, Brennan Center), and reputable journalism (TechCrunch, NPR, MIT Technology Review)

**Cross Reference:** White House official publications (whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions), Federal Register (federalregister.gov), Wikipedia article on EO 14179, TechPolicy.Press timeline, multiple law firm client alerts confirming same provisions and dates, Brookings and CSET Georgetown independent analyses

**Caveats:** Executive orders can be rescinded by future administrations (as Trump rescinded Biden's). The state preemption agenda faces significant legal obstacles — an EO cannot override state law (only Congress or courts can), and Congress has already rejected the preemption approach (99-1 Senate vote). The DOJ AI Litigation Task Force's legal challenges are untested and may fail on constitutional grounds. The 'Woke AI' provisions are legally vague — what constitutes 'ideological bias' is undefined and may face First Amendment challenges. The situation is highly dynamic as of February 2026, with ongoing litigation, state-level resistance, and potential legislative developments.
