### 27. Executive Order 14365 — Ensuring a National Policy Framework for Artificial Intelligence

_Source: `Trump_EO_14365_State_AI_Preemption_Dec_2025.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** Executive Order 14365 — Ensuring a National Policy Framework for Artificial Intelligence

**Type:** executive_order

**Jurisdiction:** US (Federal, targeting state-level AI legislation across all 50 states)

**Status:** in_force

**Effective Date:** Signed December 11, 2025; published in Federal Register December 16, 2025. Key deadlines: AI Litigation Task Force established by January 10, 2026 (done); Commerce Department evaluation of state AI laws by March 11, 2026; FTC policy statement on preemption by March 11, 2026; FCC reporting/disclosure proceeding within 90 days of Commerce evaluation; legislative recommendation timeline not specified.

**Regulatory Philosophy:** innovation_first_light_touch — explicitly seeks 'minimally burdensome national policy framework' and frames state regulation as an obstacle to US AI dominance. Represents a federal deregulatory approach that uses preemption, litigation, and funding leverage to override state-level AI governance.

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** Executive Order 14365 directs a coordinated federal effort to challenge and constrain state AI regulations that the administration views as conflicting with US national AI policy. It establishes a DOJ AI Litigation Task Force to sue states, leverages $42.45 billion in BEAD broadband funding as a compliance incentive, directs the Commerce Department to evaluate and identify 'onerous' state AI laws, and tasks the FTC and FCC with creating preemptive federal standards. The order also calls for legislative recommendations establishing a uniform federal AI framework that would formally preempt conflicting state laws.

**Key Provisions:**

- Section 2 (Policy): Declares US policy to sustain global AI dominance through a 'minimally burdensome national policy framework for AI'
- Section 3 (AI Litigation Task Force): Directs the Attorney General to establish within 30 days a Task Force whose sole responsibility is to challenge state AI laws that unconstitutionally regulate interstate commerce, are preempted by federal regulations, or are otherwise unlawful
- Section 4 (Evaluation of State AI Laws): Directs the Secretary of Commerce to publish within 90 days an evaluation identifying 'onerous' state AI laws conflicting with the EO's policy, with special focus on laws requiring AI models to alter truthful outputs or compelling disclosures that violate the First Amendment
- Section 5 (Restrictions on State Funding): Directs Commerce to issue a Policy Notice making states with 'onerous' AI laws ineligible for remaining BEAD Program non-deployment funds (~$21 billion undisbursed); also instructs all agencies to assess whether discretionary grants can be conditioned on states not enacting or not enforcing conflicting AI laws
- Section 6 (Federal Reporting Standard): Directs the FCC Chairman to initiate a proceeding to determine whether to adopt a federal reporting and disclosure standard for AI models that preempts conflicting state laws
- Section 7 (Preemption via FTC Act): Directs the FTC Chairman to issue within 90 days a policy statement on when state laws requiring alterations to truthful AI outputs are preempted by the FTC Act's prohibition on deceptive acts or practices
- Section 8 (Legislative Recommendation): Directs the Special Advisor for AI and Crypto and the OSTP Director to prepare a legislative recommendation for uniform federal AI policy that preempts conflicting state laws, with carve-outs for child safety, AI compute/data center infrastructure, and state government procurement/use of AI
- Explicitly names Colorado's SB 24-205 (algorithmic discrimination ban) as an example of an 'onerous' state law that may force AI models to produce 'false results'

**Risk Classification:** Not applicable — EO 14365 does not establish a risk classification framework for AI systems. Instead, it identifies categories of state laws considered problematic: (1) laws requiring AI models to alter 'truthful outputs'; (2) laws banning 'algorithmic discrimination' that may compel differential treatment; (3) laws mandating disclosures that may violate the First Amendment; (4) laws that regulate beyond state borders and impinge on interstate commerce.

**Sector Scope:** Horizontal in intent — targets state AI regulation across all sectors. Explicit carve-outs from proposed preemption: (1) child safety protections; (2) AI compute and data center infrastructure (other than permitting); (3) state government procurement and use of AI; (4) other topics to be determined. Notably does not carve out employment, healthcare, housing, credit, or other high-risk domains where states have been most active in regulating AI.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** No direct penalties on companies. The EO operates through three enforcement mechanisms against states: (1) Litigation — the DOJ AI Litigation Task Force will bring lawsuits challenging state AI laws in federal court; (2) Funding withholding — states with 'onerous' AI laws face loss of BEAD Program non-deployment funds (up to $21 billion undisbursed across all states) and potentially other discretionary federal grants; (3) Preemption — if the FTC and FCC issue the directed policy statements and rules, conflicting state laws would be preempted as a matter of federal law.

**Enforcement Body:** Multiple federal entities: (1) DOJ AI Litigation Task Force — chaired by the Attorney General, with the Associate Attorney General as Vice Chair, representatives from the Office of the Deputy AG, Office of the Solicitor General, and the Civil Division; (2) Department of Commerce/NTIA — evaluates state laws and conditions BEAD funding; (3) FTC — issues preemption policy statement; (4) FCC — initiates federal AI disclosure standard proceeding. White House coordination through David Sacks (Special Advisor for AI and Crypto), OSTP Director, and the Counsel to the President.

**Compliance Timeline:** January 10, 2026: AI Litigation Task Force established (completed — DOJ memorandum issued January 9, 2026). March 11, 2026: Commerce Department publishes evaluation of state AI laws identifying 'onerous' laws; FTC issues policy statement on preemption of state laws mandating deceptive AI conduct; Commerce issues BEAD Policy Notice conditioning funding. ~June 2026: FCC initiates proceeding on federal AI reporting/disclosure standard (90 days after Commerce evaluation). Legislative recommendation timeline: not specified but implied near-term. Court challenges: likely to take months to years if states resist.

**Reporting Requirements:** The EO does not impose reporting requirements on AI companies. Instead, it directs: (1) Commerce to publish an evaluation of state laws; (2) FCC to consider whether to adopt a federal reporting and disclosure standard for AI models (which would replace state-level requirements); (3) The implication is that any future federal standard would be lighter than existing or proposed state requirements.

**Sme Provisions:** The EO explicitly frames state regulatory fragmentation as particularly burdensome for startups: 'State-by-State regulation by definition creates a patchwork of 50 different regulatory regimes that makes compliance more challenging, particularly for start-ups.' The intent is to reduce compliance burden for SMEs by eliminating state-level requirements, though no specific SME-targeted provisions are included beyond the general deregulatory thrust.

**Liability Model:** none_specified — The EO does not establish a liability framework. By seeking to remove state-level AI regulations (which in some cases create liability exposure through algorithmic discrimination bans, transparency requirements, etc.), the EO effectively reduces the liability landscape for AI companies. No federal liability substitute is proposed.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** LARGE AI COMPANIES: Strongly beneficial — removes the patchwork of state compliance requirements that large multi-state operators find costly. Companies like OpenAI, Google, Meta, and Anthropic would no longer need to navigate 50 different state AI regulatory regimes. Reduces compliance costs for bias audits, impact assessments, and disclosure requirements mandated by states like Colorado, Illinois, and Texas. STARTUPS AND SMEs: Mixed — the stated goal is to reduce regulatory burden that disproportionately affects startups, and eliminating multi-state compliance requirements would indeed lower costs. However, startups focused on 'responsible AI' or 'AI safety' as competitive differentiators may lose their regulatory moat. The uncertainty during the transition period (while lawsuits and evaluations are pending) creates planning challenges. STATE-DEPENDENT BUSINESSES: States receiving BEAD funding face pressure to repeal AI laws, creating uncertainty for companies that built compliance programs around those laws. AI GOVERNANCE/COMPLIANCE INDUSTRY: Potentially negative — a lighter regulatory environment means less demand for compliance services, auditing tools, and AI governance consulting. However, the near-term effect is increased uncertainty rather than immediate deregulation, as state laws remain in force until courts enjoin them or Congress acts.

**Innovation Effect:** The administration frames the EO as pro-innovation, arguing that regulatory fragmentation stifles AI development. Potential positive effects: reduced compliance costs free up R&D budgets, especially for startups; regulatory uniformity reduces legal uncertainty that can deter investment; signals US commitment to AI-friendly environment may attract capital. Potential negative effects: removing algorithmic discrimination protections may reduce incentives for responsible AI development; eliminating transparency requirements could undermine public trust in AI systems; the approach prioritizes speed-to-market over safety, potentially leading to harmful deployments that trigger future backlash and heavier regulation. Legal experts note the near-term effect is 'procedural, not substantive' — state AI laws remain in force unless courts enjoin them, meaning the innovation impact depends on how litigation and legislation develop over 2026-2027.

**Global Influence:** EO 14365 positions the US firmly on the deregulatory end of the global AI governance spectrum, in direct contrast to the EU AI Act's comprehensive approach. This creates a transatlantic regulatory divergence that could: (1) encourage 'regulatory arbitrage' where AI companies headquarter in the US to avoid EU-style regulation; (2) undermine efforts at international AI governance harmonization (e.g., through the OECD or G7 Hiroshima Process); (3) put pressure on other jurisdictions (UK, Japan, Singapore, Canada) to adopt lighter-touch approaches to remain competitive with the US; (4) complicate EU-US cooperation on AI safety standards. For EU AI Act enforcement, US-based companies may be less motivated to comply with EU rules if domestic regulatory pressure is absent, though the EU's extraterritorial reach still applies to companies serving the EU market.

**Stakeholder Positions:** SUPPORTERS: AI industry trade groups and major AI companies have identified state AI law preemption as a top priority. The America First Policy Institute (AFPI) applauded the EO and published detailed recommendations for building upon it. Republican members of Congress generally support the federal preemption approach. The US Chamber of Commerce and tech industry lobbying groups favor a single federal standard. CRITICS: Center for American Progress called the EO 'an unprecedented, unconstitutional, and dangerous assertion of the federal executive branch into the powers of state and local government.' Civil rights organizations argue it strips protections against algorithmic discrimination. Colorado Senate Majority Leader Robert Rodriguez (SB 205 sponsor) predicted lawsuits and questioned the President's authority to override state law. Governor Polis took a pragmatic approach, convening an AI Policy Working Group to develop alternative legislation that would satisfy federal requirements while maintaining some protections. State attorneys general may challenge the EO's funding conditions as unconstitutional coercion (citing NFIB v. Sebelius precedent). Legal scholars note that executive orders cannot independently preempt state law — only congressional action or valid agency regulations can do so.

**Deregulatory Signals:** EO 14365 is the strongest deregulatory signal in AI governance globally. Specific mechanisms: (1) Direct litigation against state laws via DOJ Task Force — unprecedented use of federal litigation resources to challenge state consumer protection laws in a specific technology domain; (2) Financial coercion — conditioning $42.45 billion in BEAD funds on states repealing or not enforcing AI laws; (3) Agency-level preemption — directing FTC and FCC to issue preemptive federal standards designed to override state requirements; (4) Legislative pathway — directing preparation of preemptive federal legislation; (5) Framing of AI safety requirements as 'ideological bias' and 'censorship' — recasting algorithmic fairness as a threat to free speech and truthful AI outputs. This builds on EO 14179 (January 2025) which revoked Biden's EO 14110 and declared a policy of removing barriers to AI development. Together, these represent a comprehensive deregulatory agenda that uses every available executive tool.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EO 14179 — Removing Barriers to American Leadership in AI (Jan 23, 2025): foundational Trump AI order that revoked Biden's EO 14110 and established the deregulatory approach that EO 14365 builds upon
- Biden EO 14110 — Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy AI (Oct 30, 2023): predecessor order revoked by Trump on day one, which had established safety testing requirements, transparency mandates, and risk-based governance
- Colorado SB 24-205 — Colorado Artificial Intelligence Act (signed May 2024, effective Feb 1, 2026): explicitly named in EO 14365 as an example of 'onerous' state law banning algorithmic discrimination
- Texas RAIGA — Responsible AI Governance Act (effective 2026): identified by legal analysts as a likely target of EO 14365
- California TFAIA — Transparency in Foundation AI Act: identified as a likely target given its proximity to EO 14365's signing date
- Illinois AI Video Interview Act and other state employment AI laws effective in 2026
- EU AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689): the EO positions the US approach in direct contrast to EU comprehensive regulation
- BEAD Program (Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act, 2021): $42.45 billion broadband deployment program used as funding leverage

**Key Actors:**

- President Donald Trump: signed the EO, framing state AI regulation as a threat to US global AI dominance
- Attorney General (Pam Bondi): chairs the AI Litigation Task Force, responsible for bringing lawsuits against state AI laws
- David Sacks: White House Special Advisor for AI and Crypto, coordinates EO implementation across agencies
- Secretary of Commerce (Howard Lutnick): responsible for evaluating state AI laws and conditioning BEAD funding
- Arielle Roth: NTIA Administrator, must issue the BEAD Policy Notice by March 11, 2026
- FTC Chairman (Andrew Ferguson): must issue policy statement on preemption of state AI laws requiring 'deceptive' outputs
- FCC Chairman (Brendan Carr): must initiate proceeding on federal AI disclosure standard
- Colorado Governor Jared Polis: convened AI Policy Working Group to develop alternative legislation to avoid losing BEAD funds
- Colorado Senator Robert Rodriguez: SB 205 sponsor, predicted lawsuits over the EO
- America First Policy Institute (AFPI): published detailed recommendations for building on EO 14365
- Center for American Progress: published strong critique calling the EO unconstitutional
- Associate Attorney General: Vice Chair of the AI Litigation Task Force

**Recent Developments:**

- December 11, 2025: EO 14365 signed by President Trump
- December 16, 2025: Published in the Federal Register
- January 9, 2026: DOJ issued internal memorandum formally establishing the AI Litigation Task Force, with the Attorney General as Chair and the Associate Attorney General as Vice Chair, with representatives from the Solicitor General's office and Civil Division
- January 2026: Colorado Governor Polis convened an AI Policy Working Group to reach consensus on alternative AI legislation that might satisfy federal requirements while preserving some protections, to avoid losing BEAD funding
- Pending (March 11, 2026): Commerce Department evaluation of state AI laws due; FTC policy statement on preemption due; BEAD Policy Notice due
- Colorado SB 24-205 took effect February 1, 2026, despite the EO — making it a live target for the Task Force
- Multiple state AI laws took effect January 1, 2026, including employment-related AI laws in Illinois and Texas
- Legal commentators widely expect constitutional challenges to the EO's funding conditions, citing the Supreme Court's anti-coercion doctrine from NFIB v. Sebelius (2012)
- Near-term: state AI laws remain in force unless and until courts enjoin them or Congress passes preemptive legislation — the EO itself does not have legal force to preempt state law

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** CRITICAL FOR AI ENTREPRENEURS: (1) Regulatory environment: EO 14365 signals that the US federal government will actively oppose state-level AI regulation, creating a fundamentally different compliance landscape from the EU. US-based AI startups face less regulatory burden in the near term but must watch for the eventual federal framework. (2) Compliance strategy: Startups should avoid over-investing in compliance with state AI laws that may be preempted, but should not abandon responsible AI practices entirely — public trust and B2B customer requirements still matter. (3) Market access decisions: The EU-US regulatory divergence means startups must make strategic choices about which markets to prioritize. EU market access requires AI Act compliance regardless of US deregulation. (4) Funding landscape: The deregulatory signal may attract more VC investment to US AI startups by reducing perceived regulatory risk. (5) Competitive dynamics: 'Responsible AI' startups that relied on regulatory moats (compliance-as-a-service, bias auditing) may see their market shrink in the US. (6) BEAD funding nexus: Startups in broadband-adjacent sectors should understand that states may repeal AI protections to preserve broadband funding — creating a secondary policy impact. (7) Constitutional federalism: The EO illustrates the tension between federal innovation policy and state consumer protection — a recurring theme in US technology regulation (cf. state privacy laws vs. potential federal privacy law). (8) For French/European entrepreneurs: this demonstrates why EU-US regulatory divergence matters for transatlantic AI businesses and why the EU AI Act's 'Brussels effect' may be weaker than GDPR's.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary: federal vs. state regulatory dynamics, deregulation as policy choice, constitutional dimensions of AI governance. Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) — secondary: how regulatory uncertainty affects Build vs. Buy decisions and AI project planning. Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) — tertiary: regulatory arbitrage as business strategy, compliance costs as competitive factor.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on the full text of the Executive Order from the White House website, the DOJ memorandum establishing the AI Litigation Task Force, Federal Register publication, and analysis from multiple major law firms (White & Case, Baker Botts, Reed Smith, Phillips Lytle, Paul Hastings, Latham & Watkins, Cooley, Taft) and policy organizations (Center for American Progress, America First Policy Institute).

**Cross Reference:**

- Federal Register: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/12/16/2025-23092/ensuring-a-national-policy-framework-for-artificial-intelligence

**Caveats:** CRITICAL LEGAL CAVEAT: Executive orders cannot independently preempt state law — only congressional legislation or valid federal agency regulations can do so. The EO is a directive to federal agencies, not a law. State AI laws remain in force unless and until courts grant injunctions or Congress passes preemptive legislation. The BEAD funding conditions may face constitutional challenge under the anti-coercion doctrine (NFIB v. Sebelius). The Commerce Department evaluation (due March 2026) will determine which specific state laws are targeted — until then, the list of 'onerous' laws is speculative. FTC and FCC proceedings will involve public comment and may not produce the outcomes the EO envisions. Political context: the EO's durability depends on the administration remaining in office and maintaining this policy direction; a future administration could reverse course. The framing of algorithmic fairness requirements as 'mandating deceptive conduct' is a contested legal and policy claim, not settled law.

### 28. Executive Order 14110 — Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence

_Source: `US_Executive_Order_14110_on_AI_Biden.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** Executive Order 14110 — Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence

**Type:** executive_order

**Jurisdiction:** US (federal)

**Status:** rescinded

**Effective Date:** Signed October 30, 2023; published in Federal Register November 1, 2023 (88 FR 75191); revoked January 20, 2025 by EO 14148, replaced January 23, 2025 by EO 14179

**Regulatory Philosophy:** patchwork_federalism

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** The most sweeping US executive action on AI, EO 14110 established a government-wide framework for governing AI development and deployment through eight guiding principles covering safety, equity, civil rights, consumer protection, privacy, innovation, worker support, and international leadership. It used existing legal authorities — notably the Defense Production Act — to compel reporting from developers of the most powerful AI models (dual-use foundation models) and directed over 50 federal agencies to take more than 150 specific actions on deadlines ranging from 90 to 365 days.

**Key Provisions:**

- Defined 'dual-use foundation models' as AI models trained on broad data using self-supervision, containing tens of billions of parameters, applicable across wide contexts, and exhibiting or potentially exhibiting high-level performance at tasks posing serious risk to security, health, or safety (e.g., CBRN weapon design, cyberattack facilitation, evasion of human control)
- Set compute threshold at 10^26 floating-point operations (FLOPs) for models subject to mandatory reporting — at the time of signing, this exceeded any model trained to date
- Invoked Defense Production Act (DPA) Title VII to compel developers of dual-use foundation models to notify the federal government when training such models and share results of red-team safety tests, cybersecurity measures, and model weight ownership information
- Directed NIST to develop: (a) guidelines and best practices for AI red-teaming; (b) a companion resource to the AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF) for generative AI; (c) a companion to the Secure Software Development Framework for generative AI and dual-use foundation models; (d) AI auditing and evaluation guidelines focused on cybersecurity and biosecurity
- Required the Department of Commerce to develop guidance for watermarking and content authentication of AI-generated content (text, images, audio, video) to combat synthetic content and deepfakes
- Directed Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) to propose regulations requiring US IaaS (Infrastructure-as-a-Service) providers to verify identities of foreign persons obtaining IaaS accounts — Know Your Customer (KYC) rules for cloud compute
- Required agencies to address AI-related risks to critical infrastructure (energy, transportation, telecommunications, financial services, healthcare) in coordination with the Department of Homeland Security and sector-specific agencies
- Established AI safety and security board within DHS for critical infrastructure AI governance
- Directed HHS to develop an AI assurance policy for healthcare, establish an AI safety program, and develop a strategy for regulating AI use in drug development and healthcare delivery
- Instructed DOJ and federal civil rights offices to combat algorithmic discrimination and ensure AI does not perpetuate unlawful bias in housing, lending, employment, and criminal justice
- Directed agencies to attract and retain AI talent, including streamlining visa processes for AI specialists and establishing AI hiring programs across the federal workforce
- Called for development of AI privacy protections, including evaluating how agencies collect and use commercially available data containing personal information
- Promoted AI competition and innovation, including directing the SBA and NSF to support AI research at small businesses and startups
- Directed US engagement with international allies and organizations (G7, OECD, UN) to establish common AI safety standards and ethical frameworks

**Risk Classification:** No formal tiered risk classification system (unlike the EU AI Act). Instead, the EO focused on a compute-based threshold approach: models trained using more than 10^26 FLOPs (or biological sequence models using more than 10^23 FLOPs) triggered mandatory reporting obligations. This threshold was designed to capture only the most capable frontier models. Below this threshold, the EO relied on sector-specific agency guidance rather than a unified risk taxonomy.

**Covered Entities:** Companies developing or demonstrating intent to develop dual-use foundation models exceeding the compute threshold; US IaaS providers (cloud infrastructure); federal agencies (over 50 agencies received directives); government contractors using AI. The EO did not directly regulate end-users or deployers of AI systems in the private sector — its authority over private companies was limited primarily to reporting and transparency requirements under the DPA.

**Sector Scope:** Cross-cutting federal government-wide scope, with sector-specific directives for: healthcare and drug development (HHS); education (Department of Education); housing and lending (HUD, CFPB); criminal justice and law enforcement (DOJ); critical infrastructure (DHS); national security and defense (DOD, intelligence community); labor and employment (Department of Labor); immigration (State Department visa processing); financial services (Treasury); transportation; and energy. Military and intelligence AI systems were addressed but through separate classified annexes and existing authorities.

**Open Source Treatment:** The EO directed the NTIA (National Telecommunications and Information Administration) to produce a report on dual-use foundation models with widely available model weights (i.e., open-weight/open-source models). The resulting NTIA report (published July 2024) recommended that the government 'actively monitor for potential risks but refrain from restricting the availability of open model weights for currently available systems.' The EO itself did not create explicit exemptions for open-source models from reporting requirements — the 10^26 FLOP threshold applied regardless of whether model weights were released publicly. However, the NTIA report's recommendation signaled a policy preference against restricting open-weight models, and the practical effect was that most open-source models at the time fell below the compute threshold.

**Extraterritorial Reach:** Limited extraterritorial reach compared to the EU AI Act. The EO's core mechanism — DPA-based reporting requirements — applied to companies developing models within US jurisdiction. The IaaS KYC provision had some extraterritorial implications, requiring US cloud providers to verify identities of foreign customers. However, the EO could not directly compel compliance from non-US companies operating outside US territory. Indirect extraterritorial effects existed through: (a) US cloud infrastructure dominance (most frontier model training runs on US cloud), making the IaaS rules globally impactful; (b) the US government's ability to condition market access and government contracts on compliance.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** The EO itself did not create new penalty structures. Enforcement relied on existing legal authorities: (1) Defense Production Act Title VII: willful failure to comply with reporting requirements is punishable by up to $10,000 fine and/or up to 1 year imprisonment per violation; the government can also seek court injunctions to enforce compliance; (2) Federal agencies could use existing regulatory authorities within their domains to enforce sector-specific directives; (3) Government contracting sanctions — non-compliant companies could face suspension or debarment from federal contracts. In practice, the DPA penalties were modest compared to EU AI Act fines, and no enforcement actions were publicly initiated before the EO's revocation.

**Enforcement Body:** No single dedicated enforcement body was created. Enforcement was distributed across existing agencies: Department of Commerce (via BIS) for reporting requirements and IaaS KYC rules; NIST for standards development (advisory, not enforcement); individual sector agencies (HHS, DOJ, DHS, etc.) for their respective directives; Office of Management and Budget (OMB) for government AI use policies; White House Office of Science and Technology Policy (OSTP) for coordination. The AI Safety Institute within NIST was established to evaluate frontier models but had advisory rather than enforcement authority.

**Compliance Timeline:** Aggressive phased timeline with 150+ actions across multiple deadlines: 90 days (by late January 2024) — 21 requirements including initial NIST guidelines, DPA reporting framework setup, and initial agency AI assessments (White House claimed all 21 completed; independent verification confirmed only 11 of 21 = 52%); 180 days (by late April 2024) — 69 requirements including IaaS KYC rule proposal, DHS AI safety board establishment, and healthcare AI strategy (independent verification: 49 of 69 = 71% completed); 270 days (by late July 2024) — NIST guidelines and best practices for trustworthy AI, energy sector AI standards; 365 days (by October 2024) — remaining agency actions, comprehensive reports. BIS proposed its formal reporting rule in September 2024, with a 30-day response requirement once companies received questions. The entire framework was revoked before many deadlines were reached or rules finalized.

**Reporting Requirements:** Companies developing dual-use foundation models (above 10^26 FLOP threshold) were required to: (1) notify the federal government when beginning to train such models; (2) share results of red-team safety tests conducted per NIST guidelines; (3) report physical and cybersecurity measures protecting model weights; (4) disclose model weight ownership and access controls; (5) report on model capabilities, limitations, and potential misuse vectors. BIS proposed quarterly reporting cadence. Upon receiving a notice of applicable activities, BIS would issue specific questions to the reporting entity, which had 30 calendar days to respond. Federal agencies themselves had extensive internal reporting requirements: annual AI use inventories, risk assessments, and compliance reports to OMB.

**Sme Provisions:** Limited specific SME provisions compared to the EU AI Act. The EO directed the SBA (Small Business Administration) to support AI innovation at small businesses, and NSF to fund AI research at smaller institutions. The 10^26 FLOP compute threshold effectively exempted all but the largest companies from DPA reporting requirements — at the time of signing, only a handful of companies (primarily OpenAI, Google/DeepMind, Anthropic, Meta, and a few others) had the resources to train models at that scale. The NIST AI Safety Institute was directed to provide resources and guidance that would be accessible to smaller developers. However, the EO did not include formal regulatory sandbox provisions, reduced compliance fees, or structured support mechanisms specifically for startups in the way the EU AI Act does.

**Liability Model:** none_specified

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** LARGE ENTERPRISES (frontier AI labs): The most significant impact fell on the 5-10 companies capable of training models above the 10^26 FLOP threshold — primarily OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Anthropic, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, and Inflection AI. These companies faced DPA-mandated reporting obligations but many were already conducting voluntary safety testing and red-teaming. The compliance burden was moderate relative to their resources. STARTUPS AND SMEs: Minimal direct regulatory burden due to the high compute threshold, which effectively excluded all but the largest labs. Indirect effects included: (a) potential benefits from NIST safety standards that could reduce downstream liability risk; (b) access to government AI talent programs and SBA support; (c) positive signal for responsible AI development that could attract enterprise customers. CLOUD PROVIDERS: The IaaS KYC requirements created new compliance costs for AWS, Azure, GCP, and other cloud providers, requiring identity verification infrastructure for foreign customers. GOVERNMENT CONTRACTORS: Federal agencies' AI procurement and use guidelines created new market opportunities for AI companies selling to government but also imposed additional documentation and testing requirements.

**Innovation Effect:** Contested effects with strong partisan divide. CONSTRAINING arguments: (1) Chamber of Commerce and tech industry groups (NetChoice, SIIA, with members Amazon, Meta, Google) argued the EO threatened to hinder private sector innovation through burdensome reporting and government oversight; (2) Senator Ted Cruz called it 'barriers to innovation disguised as safety measures'; (3) The 10^26 FLOP threshold, while high, could quickly become binding as compute scales exponentially; (4) BIS quarterly reporting created administrative overhead for frontier labs. PROMOTING arguments: (1) The EO invested in AI research and talent (visa streamlining, NSF/SBA funding); (2) NIST safety standards could reduce uncertainty and lower barriers to responsible AI deployment; (3) Red-teaming requirements arguably improved model quality and safety; (4) International AI governance leadership could establish US companies as trusted AI providers globally. NET ASSESSMENT: The direct innovation constraint was narrow (only affecting a handful of frontier labs), but the signaling effect — that comprehensive regulation was coming — influenced investment decisions and corporate strategy more broadly.

**Global Influence:** Significant influence as the most comprehensive AI governance action by any major country at the time of signing (October 2023). INTERNATIONAL EFFECTS: (1) Influenced the UK AI Safety Summit (November 2023) and Bletchley Declaration, with many countries citing the US EO as inspiration; (2) The G7 Hiroshima AI Process and OECD AI governance discussions were shaped by EO 14110 provisions; (3) Established the US AI Safety Institute as a counterpart to the UK AI Safety Institute, creating a bilateral safety testing framework; (4) Influenced the EU AI Act's final negotiations (completed December 2023), particularly on GPAI model safety testing; (5) Provided a template for countries considering AI governance measures. LIMITATIONS: (1) As an executive order rather than legislation, it was inherently fragile — demonstrated by its rapid revocation; (2) The revocation sent an international signal that US AI policy is unstable across administrations; (3) Post-revocation, US global credibility on AI safety governance diminished, potentially accelerating other countries' independent regulatory efforts.

**Stakeholder Positions:** SUPPORTERS: Democratic members of Congress — Sen. Richard Blumenthal (D-CT), Rep. Ted Lieu (D-CA), Rep. Don Beyer (D-VA, House AI Caucus leader) praised it as 'comprehensive strategy for responsible innovation'; Civil rights organizations — ACLU praised anti-discrimination provisions; Leadership Conference on Civil and Human Rights CEO Maya Wiley praised fairness provisions while urging congressional action; AI safety community — organizations like the Center for AI Safety and Partnership on AI generally supported the safety testing framework; Some industry players — Microsoft and Google publicly expressed support for responsible AI governance (while their trade associations expressed concerns). CRITICS: Republican members of Congress — Sen. Ted Cruz called it 'barriers to innovation disguised as safety measures'; House Oversight Committee held hearings arguing reporting requirements were 'broad and overreaching' and would 'stifle US innovation'; Industry trade groups — Chamber of Commerce, NetChoice (Amazon, Meta, Google members), SIIA argued the EO hindered innovation; Open-source advocates — expressed concern that compute thresholds could eventually constrain open-weight model development; Libertarian-leaning organizations — George Washington University's Regulatory Studies Center warned of 'structural challenges' in the EO's ambitious timeline and agency coordination.

**Deregulatory Signals:** The EO itself was pro-regulation, but its fate illustrates the strongest deregulatory signal in global AI governance. Trump revoked EO 14110 on his first day in office (January 20, 2025) via EO 14148, then signed EO 14179 ('Removing Barriers to American Leadership in Artificial Intelligence') on January 23, 2025. The replacement order explicitly frames AI regulation as a barrier to American innovation and dominance. Specific deregulatory mechanisms: (1) Immediate revocation of all EO 14110 provisions; (2) Review and potential rescission of all policies, regulations, and directives created pursuant to EO 14110; (3) OMB directed to revise memoranda M-24-10 and M-24-18 (government AI use guidance); (4) 180-day AI Action Plan development with innovation-first philosophy; (5) No mention of safety testing, red-teaming, or reporting obligations; (6) Framing that AI systems should be 'free from ideological bias or engineered social agendas' — signaling retreat from equity/civil rights provisions. The BIS reporting rule was never finalized and is effectively dead. NIST AI Safety Institute continues to operate but with reduced mandate and uncertain future.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EO 14179 (Trump, Jan 2025) — 'Removing Barriers to American Leadership in AI', direct replacement of EO 14110 with innovation-first deregulatory approach
- EO 14148 (Trump, Jan 20, 2025) — the specific revocation order that formally rescinded EO 14110
- EO 13960 (Trump first term, Dec 2020) — 'Promoting the Use of Trustworthy AI in the Federal Government', predecessor EO focused on government AI use
- EU AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689) — the comprehensive EU legislation that EO 14110 was often compared to; the two frameworks influenced each other during development
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0, January 2023) — pre-existing voluntary framework that EO 14110 built upon and directed NIST to extend
- UK AI Safety Institute / Bletchley Declaration (November 2023) — international AI safety commitments influenced by EO 14110
- US state-level AI legislation (Colorado AI Act, California SB 1047 vetoed) — state efforts partly motivated by perceived federal governance gap
- Defense Production Act of 1950 (Title VII) — the statutory authority invoked for reporting requirements
- OECD AI Principles (2019, updated 2024) — international framework aligned with EO 14110 principles
- America's AI Action Plan (July 2025) — the Trump administration's replacement policy document

**Key Actors:**

- President Joe Biden — signed EO 14110 on October 30, 2023, as the centerpiece of his AI governance agenda
- President Donald Trump — revoked EO 14110 on January 20, 2025, replacing it with a deregulatory approach
- White House OSTP (Office of Science and Technology Policy) — coordinated cross-agency implementation
- Department of Commerce / BIS — responsible for DPA reporting rules and IaaS KYC regulations
- NIST — tasked with developing AI safety standards, red-teaming guidelines, and AI RMF extensions; housed the US AI Safety Institute
- NTIA — produced the report on open-weight AI models (July 2024)
- Elizabeth Kelly — first director of the US AI Safety Institute at NIST
- Gina Raimondo — Secretary of Commerce during EO 14110 implementation
- Sen. Richard Blumenthal (D-CT) — key congressional AI governance advocate, praised the EO
- Sen. Ted Cruz (R-TX) — leading congressional critic of the EO
- Rep. Don Beyer (D-VA) — House AI Caucus leader, supported the EO
- OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Anthropic, Meta — primary companies affected by dual-use model reporting requirements
- Chamber of Commerce, NetChoice, SIIA — industry groups that criticized the EO
- ACLU, Leadership Conference on Civil and Human Rights — civil rights organizations that supported the EO
- David Sacks — Trump's Special Advisor for AI and Crypto, tasked with developing replacement AI policy

**Recent Developments:**

- Jan 20, 2025: President Trump revoked EO 14110 via EO 14148 on his first day in office, effective immediately
- Jan 23, 2025: Trump signed EO 14179 'Removing Barriers to American Leadership in Artificial Intelligence' as replacement, directing a 180-day AI Action Plan focused on innovation and deregulation
- Jan-Feb 2025: Agencies began reviewing and suspending/rescinding actions taken pursuant to EO 14110; OMB directed to revise AI governance memoranda M-24-10 and M-24-18
- Feb 2025: BIS reporting rule for dual-use foundation models (proposed September 2024) effectively abandoned, never finalized
- Mar 2025: NIST AI Safety Institute continued operating but with reduced scope and uncertain mandate under new administration; several key staff departed
- Jul 2025: Trump administration published 'America's AI Action Plan' — the replacement policy document focusing on maintaining US AI dominance through deregulation and public-private partnership rather than safety-focused oversight
- 2025-2026: Multiple US states advanced their own AI legislation (Colorado AI Act implementation, various state proposals) partly to fill the federal governance gap left by EO 14110 revocation
- 2025-2026: International partners (UK, EU, Japan, Canada) continued bilateral AI safety cooperation despite US federal policy shift, working with NIST AI Safety Institute on voluntary basis

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** EO 14110 is a critical case study for AI entrepreneurs on three levels: (1) REGULATORY RISK: The rapid cycle of enactment (Oct 2023) and revocation (Jan 2025) — just 15 months — demonstrates that AI startups cannot rely on stable federal AI policy in the US; founders must build compliance strategies that are robust to policy reversals across administrations, a uniquely American challenge compared to the EU's legislative approach. (2) COMPUTE THRESHOLD DESIGN: The 10^26 FLOP threshold was deliberately set high enough to exempt all but the largest frontier labs, illustrating how regulatory design can either protect or burden startups — a useful contrast with the EU AI Act's broader scope. For entrepreneurs, this means understanding that the same regulatory concept (AI safety rules) can be implemented in ways that are startup-friendly or startup-hostile depending on thresholds and definitions. (3) STRATEGIC POSITIONING: The existence of EO 14110 (even after revocation) created lasting market dynamics — enterprise customers now expect AI vendors to demonstrate safety testing and responsible AI practices, regardless of legal requirements; safety and compliance became competitive differentiators. The compliance market itself became an opportunity — companies like Anthropic positioned safety-first messaging as a go-to-market advantage. (4) GOVERNMENT AS CUSTOMER: The EO directed federal agencies to adopt AI responsibly, creating a massive government AI procurement market; startups selling to government must understand that procurement rules may persist even after the parent EO is revoked. (5) INTERNATIONAL IMPLICATIONS: Startups operating globally cannot optimize for one jurisdiction — what the US deregulates, the EU may mandate; founders must factor multi-jurisdictional compliance into their business models from day one.

**Session Mapping:** 5

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High

**Cross Reference:**

- Federal Register (88 FR 75191) — official text of EO 14110
- White House archives — original fact sheet and implementation tracking
- Federal Register (EO 14148 and EO 14179) — revocation and replacement orders
- Congressional Research Service (CRS) report R47843 — nonpartisan analysis of EO 14110
- Stanford HAI executive action tracker — independent verification of implementation milestones
- NIST AI Safety Institute publications — technical standards developed under the EO
- NTIA open weight model report (July 2024) — key deliverable under the EO
- Fenwick, Wiley, Skadden, King & Spalding law firm analyses — legal implications of both enactment and revocation
- Wikipedia article on EO 14110 — comprehensive overview with sourced claims

**Caveats:** The EO has been formally revoked (January 20, 2025), making it a historical document rather than current law. However: (1) Many actions taken pursuant to the EO (NIST standards, agency guidelines) were not automatically nullified — agencies must individually review and rescind them; (2) The legal status of actions-in-progress at the time of revocation remains ambiguous for some provisions; (3) State-level AI legislation continues to evolve partly in response to the federal governance gap; (4) The BIS reporting rule was never finalized, so its exact requirements remain in proposed form only; (5) Implementation tracking data shows significant shortfalls — independent verification found only 52% of 90-day requirements and 71% of 180-day requirements were completed, suggesting the EO was aspirational in scope; (6) Some provisions (particularly around government AI use) may be partially preserved or modified rather than fully rescinded under the new administration.

### 29. US State AI Laws Patchwork (Colorado, California, Illinois, New York, Texas, Utah, and others)

_Source: `US_State_AI_Laws_Patchwork.json`_

#### Basic Information

**Name:** US State AI Laws Patchwork (Colorado, California, Illinois, New York, Texas, Utah, and others)

**Type:** legislation

**Jurisdiction:** US (state-level, across all 50 states — no comprehensive federal AI law exists)

**Status:** enacted — multiple state laws already in force (Utah AI Policy Act since May 2024, Illinois HB 3773 since January 2026, Texas TRAIGA since January 2026); others taking effect soon (Colorado SB 24-205 on June 30, 2026, NY RAISE Act on January 1, 2027). Over 1,200 AI bills introduced in 2025 alone, with 145 enacted. Legislative activity continues to accelerate in 2026.

**Effective Date:** Varies by state. Key dates: Utah AI Policy Act (SB 149) — May 1, 2024; Colorado AI Act (SB 24-205) — June 30, 2026 (delayed from February 1, 2026); California TFAIA (SB 53) — January 1, 2026; California AB 2013 — signed September 28, 2024; Illinois HB 3773 — January 1, 2026; Texas TRAIGA (HB 149) — January 1, 2026; New York RAISE Act — January 1, 2027.

**Regulatory Philosophy:** patchwork_federalism — In the absence of comprehensive federal AI legislation, US states are independently crafting AI regulations reflecting their own policy priorities. The result is a fragmented landscape where Colorado prioritizes algorithmic discrimination prevention, California focuses on frontier model safety and transparency, Illinois targets employment discrimination, Texas emphasizes innovation-friendly governance with safe harbors, and Utah takes a minimal-disclosure approach. No single state has adopted a comprehensive horizontal approach like the EU AI Act; most target specific domains (employment, consumer protection, frontier models) or specific harms (discrimination, deepfakes, child safety).

#### Scope And Content

**Summary:** US state AI regulation constitutes a rapidly growing patchwork of laws addressing AI governance in the absence of comprehensive federal legislation. Key landmark laws include: Colorado's SB 24-205 (first comprehensive state AI law targeting algorithmic discrimination in high-risk AI systems), California's SB 53 (frontier AI model safety and transparency), California's AB 2013 (training data transparency), Illinois' HB 3773 (AI in employment decisions), Texas' TRAIGA (prohibited AI uses and regulatory sandbox), Utah's SB 149 (first state AI disclosure law), and New York's RAISE Act (frontier model transparency and incident reporting). Together, these laws create overlapping and sometimes conflicting compliance obligations for companies operating across state lines.

**Key Provisions:**

- COLORADO SB 24-205 (Colorado AI Act): Requires developers and deployers of high-risk AI systems (those making 'consequential decisions' about consumers) to use reasonable care to protect against algorithmic discrimination. Developers must provide deployers with system documentation and impact assessment materials. Deployers must implement risk management programs, complete impact assessments, annually review deployments, notify consumers of AI use, and allow consumers to correct incorrect personal data. Covers decisions about employment, education, financial services, healthcare, housing, insurance, legal services, and essential government services. Enforceable exclusively by the Colorado Attorney General; violations treated as deceptive trade practices under the Colorado Consumer Protection Act ($20,000 per violation per consumer/transaction).
- CALIFORNIA SB 53 (Transparency in Frontier AI Act, signed Sept 29, 2025): Requires 'large frontier developers' (those training models using >10^26 FLOPS) to draft and publish safety protocols for managing catastrophic risk, publish transparency reports, report critical safety incidents to state regulators, and establish whistleblower protections. Defines catastrophic risk as potential to kill 50+ people via cyberattack, facilitate CBRN weapons, or cause >$1 billion in theft/damage. Civil penalties up to $1 million per violation enforced by the California AG. Includes robust whistleblower provisions with anti-retaliation protections.
- CALIFORNIA AB 2013 (AI Training Data Transparency Act, signed Sept 28, 2024): Mandates that generative AI developers publish information about training datasets including sources, data types, whether copyrighted material is included, and collection timeframes.
- CALIFORNIA SB 243 (Companion Chatbot Law): Requires operators of AI companion chatbots to disclose AI nature, maintain protocols to prevent suicidal ideation and self-harm content, and implement special protections for minors (including break reminders every three hours).
- ILLINOIS HB 3773 (AI Employment Discrimination Act, signed Aug 9, 2024): Amends the Illinois Human Rights Act to prohibit employers from using AI in ways that discriminate against employees or applicants based on protected characteristics (including unintentional/disparate-impact discrimination). Requires employers to provide notice when AI is used in recruitment, hiring, promotions, and other employment decisions. Prohibits use of zip codes as proxy for protected classes. Enforced by Illinois Department of Human Rights; penalties up to $5,000 per violation plus back pay, reinstatement, and emotional distress damages.
- TEXAS TRAIGA (HB 149, signed June 22, 2025): Prohibits AI systems intentionally designed to harm persons, engage in criminal activity, infringe constitutional rights, unlawfully discriminate, or produce CSAM. Uses an intent-based liability standard (disparate impact alone insufficient). Requires government entities to disclose AI interactions to consumers. Creates safe harbors for organizations substantially complying with NIST AI RMF or discovering violations through internal testing. Establishes a Texas AI Council and regulatory sandbox program. Enforced exclusively by the Texas AG; no private right of action.
- UTAH AI POLICY ACT (SB 149, signed March 13, 2024): First state AI law in the US. Requires disclosure of generative AI use upon consumer request. Regulated professionals (e.g., physicians) must disclose AI use proactively at the start of interactions. Creates the Office of Artificial Intelligence Policy and a regulatory sandbox for AI experimentation. Penalties up to $2,500 per administrative violation, $5,000 per court/administrative order violation.
- NEW YORK RAISE ACT (signed Dec 19, 2025): Requires frontier AI model developers to create and publish safety protocols, report incidents to the state within 72 hours, and comply with transparency obligations. Originally used compute-cost thresholds; chapter amendments (expected January 2026) replace these with a $500 million revenue threshold for covered developers. Penalties up to $1 million for first violation, $3 million for subsequent violations. Creates oversight office within Department of Financial Services. Enforceable only by NY Attorney General; effective January 1, 2027.
- ADDITIONAL SECTOR-SPECIFIC LAWS: Multiple states have passed narrower laws targeting deepfakes in elections, AI in healthcare, AI-generated content labeling, and AI in insurance decisions. In 2025, 38 states passed at least one AI-related bill.

**Risk Classification:** No unified risk classification across states. Colorado comes closest with its 'high-risk AI system' definition: an AI system that 'makes, or is a substantial factor in making, a consequential decision' — decisions with 'material legal or similarly significant effect' on consumers' access to employment, education, financial services, healthcare, housing, insurance, legal services, or essential government services. California SB 53 uses a compute threshold (>10^26 FLOPS) to identify 'frontier models' posing catastrophic risk. Texas focuses on intentional harmful uses rather than risk categories. Illinois is sector-specific (employment only). Each state defines 'AI,' 'high-risk,' and 'consequential decisions' differently, creating definitional fragmentation that complicates compliance.

**Covered Entities:** Varies significantly by state. Colorado: 'developers' (those who build or substantially modify AI systems) and 'deployers' (those who use AI systems for consequential decisions about consumers). California SB 53: 'large frontier developers' (those training models >10^26 FLOPS). Illinois: employers who use AI in employment decisions. Texas: any person who develops or deploys AI in Texas or serves Texas residents. Utah: any person causing generative AI to interact with consumers in Utah. New York: developers of 'frontier models' meeting revenue thresholds ($500 million).

**Sector Scope:** No single uniform sectoral approach. Colorado: employment, education, financial/lending, healthcare, housing, insurance, legal services, government services. Illinois: employment only. California SB 53: horizontal (any frontier model posing catastrophic risk). California SB 243: AI companion chatbots (child safety). Texas: horizontal with focus on prohibited harmful uses. Utah: horizontal (any generative AI consumer interaction). New York: horizontal (frontier models regardless of sector). Additional state laws address specific sectors: elections (deepfakes), healthcare, insurance, and education.

**Open Source Treatment:** Colorado SB 24-205 is the most explicit: developers who release a general-purpose AI model under a free and open-source license, with publicly available model parameters (weights, architecture, usage information), are exempt from developer obligations — unless the model is deployed as a high-risk AI system. The developer bears the burden of proving the exemption applies. California SB 53 does not contain a specific open-source exemption; its compute threshold (>10^26 FLOPS) effectively limits applicability to a handful of large labs (likely including Meta/Llama). Texas TRAIGA focuses on intent-based prohibitions rather than developer/deployer duties, making the open-source distinction less relevant. The New York RAISE Act uses revenue thresholds ($500 million), which would generally exclude open-source-only organizations but capture large labs releasing open-weight models.

#### Compliance And Enforcement

**Penalties:** Penalties vary significantly by state. Colorado: up to $20,000 per violation per consumer/transaction under the Colorado Consumer Protection Act, with 60-day cure period before enforcement. California SB 53: up to $1 million per violation enforced by California AG. New York RAISE Act: up to $1 million for first violation, $3 million for subsequent violations. Illinois HB 3773: up to $5,000 per violation plus compensatory remedies (back pay, reinstatement, emotional distress damages, attorneys' fees). Texas TRAIGA: civil penalties enforced by Texas AG (amount to be determined by rulemaking). Utah: up to $2,500 per administrative violation, $5,000 per court/administrative order violation. Notable: no state law currently includes criminal penalties for AI-related violations. Most states do not provide a private right of action — enforcement is typically reserved for state attorneys general.

**Enforcement Body:** All major state AI laws grant exclusive enforcement authority to the state attorney general, with some variations. Colorado: AG has exclusive enforcement and rulemaking authority. California: AG enforces SB 53. Illinois: Department of Human Rights enforces HB 3773 (employees can file charges). Texas: AG has exclusive enforcement, plus an online complaint mechanism. Utah: Division of Consumer Protection and AG. New York: AG enforces the RAISE Act, with oversight office within the Department of Financial Services publishing annual reports. No state has created a dedicated AI regulatory agency comparable to the EU AI Office.

**Compliance Timeline:** Key compliance deadlines (as of February 2026): ALREADY IN EFFECT: Utah AI Policy Act (May 1, 2024), Illinois HB 3773 (January 1, 2026), Texas TRAIGA (January 1, 2026), California SB 53 (January 1, 2026). UPCOMING: Colorado SB 24-205 (June 30, 2026 — delayed from February 1, 2026 to allow further legislative consideration), New York RAISE Act (January 1, 2027). PENDING: Colorado's 2026 regular legislative session (January-May 2026) may adopt substantive amendments to SB 24-205. New York chapter amendments expected to finalize the RAISE Act's revenue-based threshold. Multiple states considering new comprehensive AI bills in 2026 sessions (Connecticut, Virginia, Massachusetts, New Mexico tracking Colorado's model).

**Reporting Requirements:** Colorado: developers must provide deployers with system documentation; deployers must complete annual impact assessments and make them available on request. California SB 53: frontier developers must publish transparency reports, report critical safety incidents to regulators within 15 days, and (beginning 2027) the AG publishes anonymized annual whistleblower activity reports. New York RAISE Act: developers must report incidents to the state within 72 hours. Texas: government entities must disclose AI interactions. Illinois: employers must provide notice to employees/applicants when AI is used in employment decisions; IDHR draft rules specify recordkeeping requirements. Utah: disclosure upon consumer request (proactive for regulated professions).

**Liability Model:** Varies by state. Colorado: fault_based — requires showing failure to exercise 'reasonable care' with rebuttable presumption for compliant entities; violations treated as deceptive trade practices. Illinois: reversed_burden_of_proof for disparate impact claims — employer must demonstrate AI system does not discriminate once disparate impact is shown, consistent with existing IHRA framework. Texas: fault_based with intent requirement — 'intentionally designed' standard means disparate impact alone is insufficient; safe_harbor for NIST AI RMF compliance. California SB 53: fault_based — civil penalties for failure to comply with safety, transparency, and reporting obligations. New York RAISE Act: fault_based — penalties for failure to submit reports or making false statements. Utah: fault_based — standard consumer protection enforcement. No state has adopted strict liability for AI harms.

#### Impact And Analysis

**Business Impact:** LARGE ENTERPRISES: Face the highest absolute compliance costs but have resources to manage multi-state compliance. Companies like Google, Microsoft, Meta, OpenAI, and Anthropic must comply with the union of all state requirements since they operate nationally. This creates a de facto national standard set by the strictest state (currently Colorado for algorithmic discrimination, California for frontier model safety). Large enterprises are investing in AI governance teams, compliance tooling, and impact assessment processes. Some industry estimates suggest compliance adds ~17% overhead to AI system expenses. STARTUPS AND SMEs: Disproportionately burdened by the patchwork. The US Chamber of Commerce reports that California privacy and cybersecurity requirements alone impose ~$16,000 in annual compliance costs on small businesses. Multi-state compliance requires legal expertise most startups cannot afford. A 2024 US Chamber survey found 25% of small businesses not using AI cite legal/compliance concerns. However, several states include SME-friendly thresholds: California SB 53 and NY RAISE Act's compute/revenue thresholds effectively exempt startups; Colorado's small deployer exemption reduces obligations for companies under 50 employees. The Common Sense Institute projects Colorado's SB 24-205 will cost the state 40,000 jobs and $7 billion in economic output by 2030 (industry-funded study, disputed by consumer advocates). AI GOVERNANCE/COMPLIANCE INDUSTRY: The patchwork creates significant demand for compliance-as-a-service, AI auditing, bias testing, and legal advisory services. Companies like TrustArc, OneTrust, and specialized AI audit firms are expanding offerings. However, Trump's EO 14365 threatens this market by seeking to eliminate state-level requirements. SECTOR-SPECIFIC IMPACTS: Employment AI laws (Illinois, Colorado, NYC Local Law 144) most directly affect HR tech companies and hiring platforms. Healthcare AI regulations affect healthtech startups. Financial services AI rules add to existing regulatory burden from federal banking regulators.

**Global Influence:** The US state patchwork serves as both a cautionary tale and a model for other jurisdictions. CAUTIONARY TALE: The fragmentation illustrates the risks of sector-specific, jurisdiction-level regulation without federal coordination — a problem the EU explicitly sought to avoid with the AI Act's horizontal approach. Other countries (Canada, Australia, Japan) have cited the US patchwork as motivation for national-level AI frameworks. TEMPLATE EFFECT: Colorado's SB 24-205 is emerging as a template for other US states, with Connecticut, Massachusetts, New Mexico, New York, and Virginia considering bills tracking its framework. California's SB 53 and New York's RAISE Act together create a bicoastal standard for frontier model regulation that may become a de facto global template for frontier AI safety governance. REGULATORY COMPETITION: The interplay between state activism and federal deregulation (EO 14365) creates a dynamic tension that other federal systems (e.g., EU member states, Australian states) are watching. The outcome — whether federal preemption prevails or states retain regulatory authority — will influence how other federal jurisdictions approach AI governance.

**Stakeholder Positions:** INDUSTRY/PRO-INNOVATION: The US Chamber of Commerce strongly opposes the patchwork, arguing it stifles small business AI adoption and costs jobs. Tech industry trade groups (TechNet, Chamber Technology Engagement Center, ACT-The App Association) advocate for a single federal framework. Major AI labs (OpenAI, Google, Anthropic) generally prefer lighter-touch federal regulation to the current state patchwork. The Trump administration explicitly frames state AI regulation as a barrier to US global AI dominance. CONSUMER/CIVIL RIGHTS ADVOCATES: The Center for Democracy and Technology supports Colorado's SB 24-205 as a model for algorithmic accountability. The ACLU and civil rights organizations support state laws addressing AI discrimination, particularly in employment and housing. The Electronic Frontier Foundation has raised concerns about some state laws being too narrowly focused. Consumer groups argue that in the absence of federal action, state laws are the only available protection. STATE LEGISLATORS: Bipartisan support exists for state AI regulation — Colorado's SB 24-205 passed with both Democratic and Republican sponsors. State attorneys general (through NAAG) have published supportive analyses. However, some states (notably Texas) have taken more innovation-friendly approaches with intent-based liability and safe harbors. ACADEMIC/POLICY: The Future of Privacy Forum (FPF), IAPP, and Brookings Institution have published detailed analyses generally supporting thoughtful state regulation while acknowledging the patchwork problem.

**Deregulatory Signals:** Strong deregulatory signals at the federal level, mixed signals at the state level. FEDERAL: Trump's EO 14365 (December 2025) explicitly targets state AI laws, establishing a DOJ AI Litigation Task Force to challenge 'onerous' state laws, conditioning BEAD broadband funding on states not enforcing conflicting AI laws, and directing FTC/FCC to issue preemptive federal standards. Colorado's SB 24-205 is explicitly named as an example of a problematic law. STATE-LEVEL: Colorado delayed its own law from February to June 2026, with Governor Polis convening an AI Policy Working Group to consider amendments that might satisfy federal requirements. Virginia's comprehensive AI bill was vetoed by the governor in 2025. Several ambitious state bills were significantly weakened during legislative processes (e.g., California's original SB 1047 in 2024 was vetoed; the replacement SB 53 is narrower). However, New York moved forward with the RAISE Act despite federal opposition, and Illinois' employment AI law took effect on schedule. The net trajectory is uncertain: states continue legislating aggressively (1,200+ bills in 2025) while facing unprecedented federal pushback.

#### Connections And Context

**Related Regulations:**

- EU AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689): Comprehensive horizontal AI regulation that the US state patchwork contrasts with — the EU chose a unified federal approach while the US lacks one
- Trump EO 14365 — Ensuring a National Policy Framework for AI (Dec 11, 2025): Federal executive order explicitly targeting state AI laws for preemption, naming Colorado SB 24-205 specifically
- Trump EO 14179 — Removing Barriers to American Leadership in AI (Jan 23, 2025): Foundational deregulatory AI order that revoked Biden's EO 14110
- Biden EO 14110 — Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy AI (Oct 30, 2023): Predecessor federal AI governance order, revoked by Trump on inauguration day
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0): Referenced as a safe harbor standard in Texas TRAIGA and as a compliance benchmark in Colorado's guidance
- NYC Local Law 144 (2023): New York City law requiring bias audits for automated employment decision tools — an early precursor to state-level AI regulation
- OECD AI Principles: International framework that influenced several state laws' approach to transparency and accountability

**Key Actors:**

- Colorado Governor Jared Polis: Signed SB 24-205 while expressing reservations; convened AI Policy Working Group in January 2026 to consider amendments compatible with federal requirements
- California Governor Gavin Newsom: Vetoed SB 1047 (2024 frontier model safety bill) as too broad; signed narrower SB 53 (2025) and AB 2013
- New York Governor Kathy Hochul: Signed the RAISE Act (December 2025) after negotiation with legislators who pushed back against weakening the bill to match California's version
- Illinois Governor J.B. Pritzker: Signed HB 3773 (August 2024) amending the Human Rights Act for AI in employment
- Texas Governor Greg Abbott: Signed TRAIGA (June 2025) with its innovation-friendly safe harbor approach
- Utah Governor Spencer Cox: Signed SB 149 (March 2024), the first state AI policy act
- Colorado Senator Robert Rodriguez: Lead sponsor of SB 24-205 and vocal defender of state AI regulation against federal preemption
- California Senator Scott Wiener: Authored both SB 1047 (vetoed) and SB 53 (signed); a leading voice for frontier AI safety regulation
- US Chamber of Commerce / Chamber Technology Engagement Center (Michael Richards): Leading industry critic of state patchwork, advocates federal preemption
- ACT-The App Association: Industry group arguing the patchwork hurts small business competitiveness
- Center for Democracy and Technology: Published supportive FAQ on Colorado AI Act; advocates for state-level consumer protection
- Future of Privacy Forum: Published detailed analyses of state legislative approaches, tracking trends across all 50 states
- IAPP (International Association of Privacy Professionals): Maintains the most comprehensive US State AI Governance Legislation Tracker
- National Conference of State Legislatures (NCSL): Tracks and publishes AI legislation data across all states

**Recent Developments:**

- 2024-03-13: Utah Governor Cox signs SB 149, the first US state AI policy act, effective May 1, 2024
- 2024-05-17: Colorado Governor Polis signs SB 24-205, the first comprehensive US state AI law
- 2024-08-09: Illinois Governor Pritzker signs HB 3773 amending Human Rights Act for AI in employment
- 2024-09-28: California Governor Newsom signs AB 2013 (training data transparency) and vetoes SB 1047 (frontier model safety)
- 2025-05-31: Texas Legislature passes TRAIGA (HB 149); signed June 22, 2025
- 2025-08-28: Colorado Governor Polis signs SB 25B-004 delaying the Colorado AI Act effective date from February 1, 2026 to June 30, 2026
- 2025-09-29: California Governor Newsom signs SB 53 (Transparency in Frontier AI Act), effective January 1, 2026
- 2025-12-11: Trump signs EO 14365 targeting state AI laws for federal preemption, explicitly naming Colorado SB 24-205
- 2025-12-19: New York Governor Hochul signs the RAISE Act, effective January 1, 2027
- 2026-01-01: Multiple state laws take effect — Illinois HB 3773, Texas TRAIGA, California SB 53
- 2026-01-09: DOJ formally establishes AI Litigation Task Force per EO 14365
- 2026-01 (ongoing): Colorado AI Policy Working Group meets to consider amendments to SB 24-205 that might satisfy federal requirements while preserving consumer protections
- 2026 sessions: Connecticut, Massachusetts, New Mexico, Virginia considering comprehensive AI bills tracking Colorado's model
- 2026-02 (current): Over 1,200 AI bills were introduced across all 50 states in 2025 with 145 enacted; 2026 legislative sessions expected to see continued or accelerated activity
- Pending (2026-03-11): Commerce Department evaluation of state AI laws due under EO 14365; FTC preemption policy statement due; BEAD funding conditions to be issued
- Pending (2026-06-30): Colorado AI Act SB 24-205 scheduled to take effect (may be further amended or delayed)

#### Teaching Relevance

**Entrepreneurial Implications:** CRITICAL COMPLIANCE LANDSCAPE FOR AI STARTUPS: (1) Multi-state compliance burden: Any AI startup serving customers across US states faces overlapping obligations — algorithmic discrimination prevention (Colorado), employment AI notice requirements (Illinois), frontier model safety (California, New York), prohibited uses (Texas), and basic disclosure (Utah). This creates a compliance burden that industry estimates at ~17% overhead on AI system costs. (2) Strategic threshold awareness: California SB 53 (>10^26 FLOPS) and NY RAISE Act ($500M revenue) only affect the largest AI labs — most startups are exempt from frontier model requirements. Colorado's small deployer exemption (< 50 employees, no custom training) offers relief for early-stage companies. Texas safe harbors reward NIST AI RMF adoption. Startups should understand which laws actually apply to them versus which generate compliance anxiety. (3) Market access decisions: Startups must decide whether to comply with the strictest standard (becoming compliant everywhere) or to geofence services away from heavily regulated states. The de facto national standard set by Colorado/California may make the 'comply everywhere' approach more practical. (4) Competitive moats from compliance: Startups that proactively build bias testing, impact assessment, and transparency capabilities gain a competitive advantage in B2B sales to enterprises that require AI governance from their vendors. 'Responsible AI' as a product differentiator works particularly well when selling to regulated industries (finance, healthcare, HR). (5) Regulatory uncertainty: The federal-state tension (EO 14365 vs. state laws) creates planning challenges. Prudent startups should continue complying with state laws while monitoring the federal framework development. Over-investing in compliance with laws that may be preempted is a risk, but abandoning responsible AI practices would be a bigger one. (6) EU contrast: The US patchwork vs. EU AI Act comparison is a key decision factor for startups choosing which market to prioritize. EU compliance (one framework, high cost) vs. US compliance (50 frameworks, fragmented but many exemptions for small companies) presents a genuine strategic tradeoff. (7) Compliance-as-a-service opportunity: The patchwork creates a market for AI governance SaaS — bias auditing, impact assessment automation, multi-state compliance dashboards — that entrepreneurial students might consider building. (8) For French/European entrepreneurs entering the US: understanding the patchwork is essential for market entry strategy — which states to target first, which compliance obligations to prioritize, and how the federal preemption battle may simplify or complicate the landscape.

**Session Mapping:** Session 5 (Ethics, governance & final presentations) — primary: comparison of US patchwork vs. EU AI Act approaches to AI governance, state-federal tension, regulatory philosophy differences. Session 4 (AI business models & strategy) — secondary: compliance costs as business factor, regulatory moats, market access strategy across jurisdictions. Session 3 (Framing & managing AI projects) — tertiary: impact assessments, risk management requirements (Colorado), and how regulatory requirements should inform AI project planning and Build vs. Buy decisions.

#### Confidence

**Data Quality:** High — based on official state legislative texts, governor signing announcements, analysis from major law firms (Latham & Watkins, Baker Botts, DLA Piper, Norton Rose Fulbright, Wilson Sonsini, Morrison Foerster, Gibson Dunn, Mayer Brown, Seyfarth Shaw, Ogletree), policy organizations (FPF, CDT, IAPP, Brookings), trade associations (US Chamber of Commerce), and government sources (NCSL, state legislature websites, governor.ca.gov, governor.ny.gov).

**Caveats:** RAPIDLY EVOLVING LANDSCAPE: This is the most dynamic area of AI regulation globally as of February 2026. Key uncertainties: (1) Colorado's 2026 legislative session (January-May) may substantially amend SB 24-205 before its June 30 effective date, potentially weakening its algorithmic discrimination provisions to satisfy federal requirements under EO 14365. (2) The Commerce Department evaluation of state AI laws due March 11, 2026 will determine which state laws are officially designated 'onerous' and targeted for federal challenge. (3) The DOJ AI Litigation Task Force may file lawsuits against state laws at any time, creating constitutional uncertainty that won't be resolved for months or years. (4) Congressional action on a comprehensive federal AI framework could preempt all state laws, but no bill has advanced significantly as of early 2026. (5) The 2026 state legislative sessions are expected to introduce hundreds of new AI bills, further expanding the patchwork. (6) The Common Sense Institute's economic impact estimates ($7B cost, 40,000 jobs lost from Colorado's law) are industry-funded and disputed by consumer advocates — these figures should be treated as advocacy data, not independent analysis. (7) State-level definitions of key terms ('AI system,' 'high-risk,' 'consequential decision,' 'developer,' 'deployer') are inconsistent and may evolve through rulemaking and case law.
